萨拉把耳朵凑上去。起初只听得急促喘促的呼吸——随后是几句破碎的话，同呼吸混乱地交杂着：
「我还没说完——你必须起誓——凑近，凑近，再凑近——第三桩——你老爷——起誓交给他——」
最后几个字极轻极轻地散了。那两片艰难地嚅动着它们的嘴唇，忽然一开，就此不再合上。萨拉扑向门口，拉开门，朝过道呼救；随即奔回床边，抄起那张由太太囗授写就的信纸，藏进怀中。她这么做时，特雷弗顿太太眼里的最后一瞥，严峻而含责地钉在她身上，并且就在其余五官一时扭曲的当囗，仍保持着那份神情，足足一个令人屏息的瞬间。那一瞬过去，下一瞬，那先行于死神驾临的阴影，悄悄掩上来，只一个安静的刹那，便把生命之光从整张脸上关灭了。
大夫进屋来，看护和一名仆人跟在身后；他抢步到床边，一眼便看出，他来此效力的时刻已永远过去。他先对跟着来的仆人说话。
「去回你家老爷，」他说，「请他在自己屋里稍候，等我过去同他说话。」
萨拉还立在床边——不动，不言，谁也不理会。
看护走近来要合拢帐幔，一见她的脸吃了一惊，转向大夫。
「这位还是出去的好吧，先生？」看护说，语气神色间带着几分轻蔑。「出了这样的事，她像是受了不必要的惊吓。」
「很对，」大夫说。「她还是退下的好。——听我劝一句，先离开我们一会儿，」他碰了碰萨拉的胳膊，添道。
她疑疑忌忌地缩后，抬起一只手，按住怀中藏信的地方，紧紧压着，另一只手则伸出去接蜡烛。
「您最好回自己屋里歇一歇，」大夫说着，把蜡烛递给她。「不过且慢，」他想了片刻又续道。「我要去向您老爷报凶信，兴许他会急着想听特雷弗顿太太临终前当着您说过什么话。

===Original===

Perhaps you had better come with me, and wait while I go into Captain Treverton’s room.”
“No! no!—oh, not now—not now, for God’s sake!” Speaking those words in low, quick, pleading tones, and drawing back affrightedly to the door, Sarah disappeared without waiting a moment to be spoken to again.
“A strange woman!” said the doctor, addressing the nurse. “Follow her, and see where she goes to, in case she is wanted and we are obliged to send for her. I will wait here until you come back.”
When the nurse returned she had nothing to report but that she had followed Sarah Leeson to her own bedroom, had seen her enter it, had listened outside, and had heard her lock the door.
“A strange woman!” repeated the doctor. “One of the silent, secret sort.”
“One of the wrong sort,” said the nurse. “She is always talking to herself, and that is a bad sign, in my opinion. I distrusted her, Sir, the very first day I entered the house.”

===Chinese===

「兴许您还是随我来，在我进特雷弗顿舰长屋里时在外头等着好。」
「不！不！——哦，不是现在——看上帝份上，别是现在！」萨拉用低而急的恳求声调说着这几句，惶惶地退向门口，没等别人再开口，人已不见了。
「怪人一个！」大夫对看护说。「跟着她，看她去哪儿，万一要找她、得打发人去叫。我在这儿等你回来。」
看护回来，能报的只有这些：她跟着萨拉·利森到了她自家的卧室，见她进了屋，在外面听了听，听见她锁上了门。
「怪人一个！」大夫重说。「闷葫芦、藏心事的这一路人。」
「不正路的那一路，」看护说。「她总是自言自语，依我看，那不是好兆头。我进这宅子的头一天，就信不过她了，先生。」

## II The Child / 二 孩子
===Original===

The instant Sarah Leeson had turned the key of her bedroom door, she took the sheet of notepaper from its place of concealment in her bosom—shuddering, when she drew it out, as if the mere contact of it hurt her—placed it open on her little dressing-table, and fixed her eyes eagerly on the lines which the note contained. At first they swam and mingled together before her. She pressed her hands over her eyes, for a few minutes, and then looked at the writing again.
The characters were clear now—vividly clear, and, as she fancied, unnaturally large and near to view. There was the address: “To my Husband”; there the first blotted line beneath, in her dead mistress’s handwriting; there the lines that followed, traced by her own pen, with the signature at the end—Mrs. Treverton’s first, and then her own. The whole amounted to but very few sentences, written on one perishable fragment of paper, which the flame of a candle would have consumed in a moment. Yet there she sat, reading, reading, reading, over and over again; never touching the note, except when it was absolutely necessary to turn over the first page; never moving, never speaking, never raising her eyes from the paper. As a condemned prisoner might read his death-warrant, so did Sarah Leeson now read the few lines which she and her mistress had written together not half an hour since.
The secret of the paralyzing effect of that writing on her mind lay, not only in itself, but in the circumstances which had attended the act of its production.
The oath which had been proposed by Mrs. Treverton under no more serious influence than the last caprice of her disordered faculties, stimulated by confused remembrances of stage words and stage situations, had been accepted by Sarah Leeson as the most sacred and inviolable engagement to which she could bind herself. The threat of enforcing obedience to her last commands from beyond the grave, which the mistress had uttered in mocking experiment on the superstitious fears of the maid, now hung darkly over the weak mind of Sarah, as a judgment which might descend on her, visibly and inexorably, at any moment of her future life. When she roused herself at last, and pushed away the paper and rose to her feet, she stood quite still for an instant, before she ventured to look behind her.

===Chinese===

萨拉·利森一转过卧室门上的钥匙，就从怀中藏信的地方抽出那张信纸——抽出来时打了个寒噤，仿佛单是碰着它也伤人——把纸摊开在小梳妆台上，急切地把眼睛钉在信里的字句上。起初那些字在她眼前浮漾混作一片。她用手捂住双眼，捂了几分钟，再看那笔迹。
这回字迹清楚了——清楚得刺眼，而且她觉得，大得反常，近得反常。收信人那行是「致吾夫」；底下一行是洇开的字迹，出自死去的主母之手；再底下的几行出自她自己笔下，末尾是两个签名——特雷弗顿太太的在前，她的在后。统共不过寥寥数句，写在一片薄薄的纸上，一支烛火片刻便能烧尽。可她坐在那里，读，读，读，一遍又一遍；除了非翻页不可时，绝不碰那张信；不动，不言，眼睛不离信纸。正如死囚读自己的死刑状，萨拉·利森此刻读着这几行字——她同主母半小时前才一起写下的。
那笔迹在她心上之所以有麻木之力，秘密不只在字句本身，也在写下它们时的种种情形。
特雷弗顿太太提出那道誓言时，不过是她紊乱心神的最后一阵任性，夹着对台词与桥段的混沌记忆；萨拉·利森却把它当作了自己所能缔结的最神圣、最不可背弃的盟约。主母以身后显灵、迫人遵命作威胁，本是拿女仆的迷信恐惧戏试一场；此刻这威胁却黑压压悬在萨拉软弱的心头，像一桩随时可能降临的审判，看得见，躲不掉，一生中任何一个时刻都可能来。她终于醒转过来，推开信纸站起身，却先在原地立了一瞬，才敢回头看身后。

===Original===

When she did look, it was with an effort and a start, with a searching distrust of the empty dimness in the remoter corners of the room.
Her old habit of talking to herself began to resume its influence, as she now walked rapidly backward and forward, sometimes along the room and sometimes across it. She repeated incessantly such broken phrases as these: “How can I give him the letter?—Such a good master; so kind to us all.—Why did she die, and leave it all to *me*?—I can’t bear it alone; it’s too much for me.” While reiterating these sentences, she vacantly occupied herself in putting things about the room in order, which were set in perfect order already. All her looks, all her actions, betrayed the vain struggle of a weak mind to sustain itself under the weight of a heavy responsibility. She arranged and rearranged the cheap china ornaments on her chimneypiece a dozen times over—put her pincushion first on the looking-glass, then on the table in front of it—changed the position of the little porcelain dish and tray on her wash-hand-stand, now to one side of the basin, and now to the other. Throughout all these trifling actions the natural grace, delicacy, and prim neat-handedness of the woman still waited mechanically on the most useless and aimless of her occupations of the moment. She knocked nothing down, she put nothing awry; her footsteps at the fastest made no sound—the very skirts of her dress were kept as properly and prudishly composed as if it was broad daylight and the eyes of all her neighbors were looking at her.
From time to time the sense of the words she was murmuring confusedly to herself changed. Sometimes they disjointedly expressed bolder and more self-reliant thoughts. Once they seemed to urge her again to the dressing-table and the open letter on it, against her own will. She read aloud the address, “To my Husband,” and caught the letter up sharply, and spoke in firmer tones. “Why give it to him at all? Why not let the secret die with her and die with me, as it ought? Why should he know it? He shall *not* know it!”
Saying those last words, she desperately held the letter within an inch of the flame of the candle. At the same moment the white curtain over the window before her stirred a little, as the freshening air found its way through the old-fashioned, ill-fitting sashes.

===Chinese===

看时，她用了一把力，还蓦地一惊，带着搜索的疑忌扫视屋里远角空漠的昏暗。
她那自言自语的老毛病，又乘势发了作——此刻她在屋里疾步来回，忽而顺着走，忽而横着穿。嘴里反反复复只有些碎语：「我怎么交给他？——那么好的一位老爷；待我们个个都好。——她为什么死，把这些全撂给*我*？——我一个人担不动；太重了。」口里念着，手上还空空地忙：把屋里早已齐整的东西摆弄来摆弄去。她的一瞥一动，都露出一个软弱的心在重负之下挣扎撑持的徒劳。壁炉架上那几件便宜的瓷摆设，她摆了又摆，足有十几遍——针插先放上穿衣镜，又移到镜前的桌上——洗手台上那副小瓷盆碟，先挪到盆这边，又挪到盆那边。而在这些琐碎动作里，那女人天性里的端雅、纤秀和一丝不苟的利落，依旧机械地伺候着她此刻最无益、最无谓的忙碌。她碰不倒一件东西，放不歪一件东西；脚步最快也不闻声响——连裙裾都收束得那么守礼、那么拘谨，仿佛正是大白天，满宅邻人的眼睛都望着她。
她口中嗫嚅的字句，含义时时变换。有时碎碎地透出更大胆、更自主的心思。有一回，那字句仿佛违背她的本意，又把她拽向梳妆台和台上摊开的信。她高声念出信封上那句「致吾夫」，猛地把信抓起，声调硬了起来：「为什么非要给他？为什么不叫秘密随她死、随我死，如它本来该当的那样？他凭什么要知道？他就*不该*知道！」
说着末了几个字，她不顾死活地把信举到离烛火只一寸的地方。同一时刻，她面前窗上那道白窗帘轻轻一动——渐紧的晨风，从老式不严的窗框缝里钻了进来。

===Original===

Her eye caught sight of it, as it waved gently backward and forward. She clasped the letter suddenly to her breast with both hands, and shrank back against the wall of the room, her eyes still fastened on the curtain with the same blank look of horror which they had exhibited when Mrs. Treverton had threatened to claim her servant’s obedience from the other world.
“Something moves,” she gasped to herself, in a breathless whisper. “Something moves in the room.”
The curtain waved slowly to and fro for the second time. Still fixedly looking at it over her shoulder, she crept along the wall to the door.
“Do you come to me already?” she said, her eyes riveted on the curtain while her hand groped over the lock for the key. “Before your grave is dug? Before your coffin is made? Before your body is cold?”
She opened the door and glided into the passage; stopped there for a moment, and looked back into the room.
“Rest!” she said. “Rest, mistress—he shall have the letter.”
The staircase-lamp guided her out of the passage. Descending hurriedly, as if she feared to give herself time to think, she reached Captain Treverton’s study, on the ground-floor, in a minute or two. The door was wide open, and the room was empty.
After reflecting a little, she lighted one of the chamber-candles standing on the hall-table, at the lamp in the study, and ascended the stairs again to her master’s bedroom. After repeatedly knocking at the door and obtaining no answer, she ventured to go in. The bed had not been disturbed, the candles had not been lit—to all appearance the room had not even been entered during the night.
There was but one other place to seek him—the chamber in which his wife lay dead. Could she summon the courage to give him the letter there? She hesitated a little—then whispered, “I must! I must!”
The direction she now compelled herself to take led her a little way down the stairs again. She descended very slowly this time, holding cautiously by the banisters, and pausing to take breath almost at every step. The door of what had been Mrs. Treverton’s bedroom was opened, when she ventured to knock at it, by the nurse, who inquired, roughly and suspiciously, what she wanted there.
“I want to speak to my master.”
“Look for him somewhere else. He was here half an hour ago. He is gone now.”
“Do you know where he has gone?”
“No.

===Chinese===

她一眼望见窗帘在轻轻前后拂动，便双手猛地把信扣在胸口，缩身退到墙边，眼睛仍死死盯住那窗帘，眼神里正是特雷弗顿太太说要到另一个世界来索取仆人服从时，她露出的那副失魂落魄的恐怖。
「有东西在动，」她气咻咻地自语，喘不成声。「屋里有东西在动。」
窗帘第二次缓缓拂动。她仍从肩头定定望着它，贴着墙一路蹭到门口。
「您这就来找我了？」她说，眼睛钉着窗帘，手在锁上摸钥匙。「坟还没挖？棺还没做？身上还没凉？」
她开门溜进过道；在那里立了片刻，回头朝屋里望。
「安息吧！」她说。「安息吧，太太——信会交给他的。」
楼梯口的灯引她出了过道。她急急下楼，仿佛怕给自己留出思考的工夫，一两分钟便到了底层特雷弗顿舰长的书房。门大敞着，屋里没人。
她略想了想，从书房的灯上点了一支门厅桌上放着的房间烛，又上楼到主人的卧室去。敲了几回门没人应，她便斗胆进去。床没有动过，蜡烛没有点过——看样子夜里根本没人进过这屋。
找他只剩一个去处——他亡妻停灵的那间屋。她可有胆量在那里把信交给他？她踌躇了一下——随即低语：「我必须！我必须！」
这回她逼着自己走的路，又把她带下几级楼梯。这一回她下得极慢，小心地扶着栏杆，几乎每级都停下来喘一口气。走到特雷弗顿太太生前的卧室门前，她鼓起勇气敲门，开门的是看护，又粗声又疑心地问她要做什么。
「我要跟老爷说话。」
「去别处找他吧。半小时前他还在这里。这会儿人走了。」
「您知道他去哪儿了么？」
「不知道。

===Original===

I don’t pry into other people’s goings and comings. I mind my own business.”
With that discourteous answer, the nurse closed the door again. Just as Sarah turned away from it she looked toward the inner end of the passage. The door of the nursery was situated there. It was ajar, and a dim gleam of candlelight was flickering through it.
She went in immediately, and saw that the candlelight came from an inner room, usually occupied, as she well knew, by the nursery-maid and by the only child of the house of Treverton—a little girl named Rosamond, aged, at that time, nearly five years.
“Can he be there?—in that room, of all the rooms in the house!”
Quickly as the thought arose in her mind, Sarah raised the letter (which she had hitherto carried in her hand) to the bosom of her dress, and hid it for the second time, exactly as she had hidden it on leaving her mistress’s bedside.
She then stole across the nursery on tiptoe toward the inner room. The entrance to it, to please some caprice of the child’s, had been arched, and framed with trellis-work, gayly colored, so as to resemble the entrance to a summerhouse. Two pretty chintz curtains, hanging inside the trellis-work, formed the only barrier between the day-room and the bedroom. One of these was looped up, and toward the opening thus made Sarah now advanced, after cautiously leaving her candle in the passage outside.
The first object that attracted her attention in the child’s bedroom was the figure of the nursemaid, leaning back, fast asleep, in an easy-chair by the window. Venturing, after this discovery, to look more boldly into the room, she next saw her master sitting with his back toward her, by the side of the child’s crib. Little Rosamond was awake, and was standing up in bed with her arms round her father’s neck. One of her hands held over his shoulder the doll that she had taken to bed with her, the other was twined gently in his hair. The child had been crying bitterly, and had now exhausted herself, so that she was only moaning a little from time to time, with her head laid wearily on her father’s bosom.
The tears stood thick in Sarah’s eyes as they looked on her master and on the little hands that lay round his neck.

===Chinese===

旁人来来去去，我不打听。我只管我自己的事。」
看护丢下这句不客气的话，又把门关上。萨拉刚要转身，目光落到过道里头那端。育儿室的门就在那里，虚掩着，一线昏黄的烛光在门缝里闪烁。
她立刻走进去，看清烛光来自里面一间小屋——她深知那通常是保姆同特雷弗顿家独苗的住处——一个名叫罗莎蒙德（Rosamond）的小女孩，那时年近五岁。
「他会在那里？——满宅子的屋子，偏偏那间！」
这念头才起，萨拉便把一直拿在手里的信举到胸前，第二次把它藏进怀里，藏法同离开太太床边时一模一样。
随后她蹑着脚，悄悄穿过育儿室，向里间走去。为迁就孩子的一时兴致，里间的入口做成了拱形，围着一圈涂得花花绿绿的格棚，好看得像凉亭的入口。棚内挂两幅漂亮的花布帘，便是日间屋同卧室之间唯一的隔断。其中一幅挽了起来，萨拉小心地把烛留在过道里，朝那道挽开的口子走去。
孩子卧室里第一个吸住她目光的，是保姆的身影——仰靠在窗边的安乐椅里，睡得正沉。见了这个，她才敢放胆往屋里细看，于是看见了主人：背朝着她，坐在童床边。小罗莎蒙德醒着，站在床上，两臂搂着父亲的脖颈。一只手越过他的肩头，举着她带进被窝的玩偶；另一只手轻轻地绕在他头发里。孩子方才大哭过，如今哭乏了，只偶而低低呻咿一声，把头无力地偎在父亲的胸口。
萨拉望着主人，望着搭在他颈间的那双小手，眼里噙满了泪水。

===Original===

She lingered by the raised curtain, heedless of the risk she ran, from moment to moment, of being discovered and questioned—lingered until she heard Captain Treverton say soothingly to the child:
“Hush, Rosie, dear! hush, my own love! Don’t cry any more for poor mamma. Think of poor papa, and try to comfort him.”
Simple as the words were, quietly and tenderly as they were spoken, they seemed instantly to deprive Sarah Leeson of all power of self-control. Reckless whether she was heard or not, she turned and ran into the passage as if she had been flying for her life. Passing the candle she had left there, without so much as a look at it, she made for the stairs, and descended them with headlong rapidity to the kitchen-floor. There one of the servants who had been sitting up met her, and, with a face of astonishment and alarm, asked what was the matter.
“I’m ill—I’m faint—I want air,” she answered, speaking thickly and confusedly. “Open the garden door, and let me out.”
The man obeyed, but doubtfully, as if he thought her unfit to be trusted by herself.
“She gets stranger than ever in her ways,” he said, when he rejoined his fellow-servant, after Sarah had hurried past him into the open air. “Now our mistress is dead, she will have to find another place, I suppose. I, for one, shan’t break my heart when she’s gone. Shall you?”

===Chinese===

她在挽起的门帘边流连不去，忘了随时可能被人撞见、被人盘问——流连着，直到听见特雷弗顿舰长柔声哄那孩子：
「嘘，罗茜，乖！嘘，我的心肝！别再为可怜的妈妈哭了。想想可怜的爸爸，来宽宽爸爸的心。」
这几句话虽简单，说虽说得温存，却仿佛立时夺走了萨拉·利森全部的自持。她不管有没有人听见，转身便奔进过道，像逃命一般。路过自己留下的蜡烛，看也不看一眼，直奔楼梯，一路飞跌到厨房那层。一个守夜的仆人在那里碰见她，满面惊愕，问出了什么事。
「我难受——我发晕——我要透口气，」她答，说得又滞又乱。「开花园门，让我出去。」
那人照办了，心里却犯嘀咕，像是不放心让她独自一人。
「她那做派，比先前更邪了，」萨拉匆匆擦过他身边走进晨光里之后，他回到同伴那里说。「如今太太死了，她怕得另寻去处了。她走那天，我是不会伤心的。你呢？」

## III The Hiding of the Secret / 三 秘密的藏匿
===Original===

The cool, sweet air in the garden, blowing freshly over Sarah’s face, seemed to calm the violence of her agitation. She turned down a side walk, which led to a terrace and overlooked the church of the neighboring village.
The daylight out of doors was clear already. The misty auburn light that goes before sunrise was flowing up, peaceful and lovely, behind a line of black-brown moorland, over all the eastern sky. The old church, with the hedge of myrtle and fuchsia growing round the little cemetery in all the luxuriance which is only seen in Cornwall, was clearing and brightening to view, almost as fast as the morning firmament itself. Sarah leaned her arms heavily on the back of a garden-seat, and turned her face toward the church. Her eyes wandered from the building itself to the cemetery by its side, rested there, and watched the light growing warmer and warmer over the lonesome refuge where the dead lay at rest.
“Oh, my heart! my heart!” she said. “What must it be made of not to break?”
She remained for some time leaning on the seat, looking sadly toward the churchyard, and pondering over the words which she had heard Captain Treverton say to the child. They seemed to connect themselves, as everything else now appeared to connect itself in her mind, with the letter that had been written on Mrs. Treverton’s deathbed. She drew it from her bosom once more, and crushed it up angrily in her fingers.
“Still in my hands! still not seen by any eyes but mine!” she said, looking down at the crumpled pages. “Is it all my fault? If she was alive now—if she had seen what I saw, if she had heard what I heard in the nursery—could she expect me to give him the letter?”
Her mind was apparently steadied by the reflection which her last words expressed. She moved away thoughtfully from the garden-seat, crossed the terrace, descended some wooden steps, and followed a shrubbery path which led round by a winding track from the east to the north side of the house.
This part of the building had been uninhabited and neglected for more than half a century past.

===Chinese===

花园里清凉甜润的空气，新鲜地拂过萨拉的脸，似乎平息了她激动的狂势。她拐上一条侧径，径通一处高台，俯瞰邻村的教堂。
屋外的天光已经明亮。日出前那种雾蒙蒙的赭金色，正静静而可爱地从一线黑棕色的荒原背后漫上来，铺满整个东天。老教堂连同绕着小墓园生长的桃金娘与倒挂金钟篱——那种只在康沃尔见得到的繁茂——正在眼前渐渐显清、变亮，速度几乎赶得上晨空本身。萨拉把双臂沉沉搁在一张花园座椅的靠背上，把脸转向教堂。她的目光从建筑移到旁侧的墓园，停在那里，看天光在那片亡人安息的冷清庇护地上，一分一分地暖起来。
「哦，我的心！我的心！」她说。「到底是什么做的，竟还不碎？」
她倚着座椅又待了一阵，凄然望着教堂墓园，细细回味舰长对那孩子说的话。那几句话，同如今她心里所有的事一样，仿佛都自动牵连到那封在特雷弗顿太太临终床上写下的信。她又把信从怀里抽出来，在指间气恼地攥作一团。
「还在我手里！除了我的眼睛，还是谁的眼睛都没看过！」她低头望着那几页皱纸说。「这全是我的错么？她若此刻还活着——她若见了我见的、听了我在育儿室里听的——还能指望我把信交给他么？」
这几句自省的话，显然把她的心神稳定了些。她若有所思地离开座椅，穿过高台，下了几级木阶，沿一条灌木小径绕行，曲曲折折从宅子的东侧转到北侧。
宅子的这一侧，至今已逾半个世纪无人居住、无人照管。

===Original===

In the time of Captain Treverton’s father the whole range of the north rooms had been stripped of their finest pictures and their most valuable furniture, to assist in redecorating the west rooms, which now formed the only inhabited part of the house, and which were amply sufficient for the accommodation of the family and of any visitors who came to stay with them. The mansion had been originally built in the form of a square, and had been strongly fortified. Of the many defenses of the place, but one now remained—a heavy, low tower (from which and from the village near, the house derived its name of Porthgenna Tower), standing at the southern extremity of the west front. The south side itself consisted of stables and outhouses, with a ruinous wall in front of them, which, running back eastward at right angles, joined the north side, and so completed the square which the whole outline of the building represented.
The outside view of the range of north rooms, from the weedy, deserted garden below, showed plainly enough that many years had passed since any human creature had inhabited them. The windowpanes were broken in some places, and covered thickly with dirt and dust in others. Here, the shutters were closed—there, they were only half opened. The untrained ivy, the rank vegetation growing in fissures of the stonework, the festoons of spiders’ webs, the rubbish of wood, bricks, plaster, broken glass, rags, and strips of soiled cloth, which lay beneath the windows, all told the same tale of neglect. Shadowed by its position, this ruinous side of the house had a dark, cold, wintry aspect, even on the sunny August morning when Sarah Leeson strayed into the deserted northern garden. Lost in the labyrinth of her own thoughts, she moved slowly past flowerbeds, long since rooted up, and along gravel walks overgrown by weeds; her eyes wandering mechanically over the prospect, her feet mechanically carrying her on wherever there was a trace of a footpath, lead where it might.
The shock which the words spoken by her master in the nursery had communicated to her mind, had set her whole nature, so to speak, at bay, and had roused in her, at last, the moral courage to arm herself with a final and desperate resolution.

===Chinese===

早在特雷弗顿舰长父亲的时代，整排北屋便已被剥去了最好的画与最值钱的家具，拿去装点西屋；如今西屋成了宅子里唯一住人的一翼，供全家及往来宾客起居绰绰有余。这座大宅原是四合的方形，工事曾经相当坚固。诸多防务如今只剩其一——一座低矮厚重的塔楼（宅子便连同近旁的村落，得名「波斯根纳塔楼」（Porthgenna Tower）），立于西面正前方的南端。南侧本身则是马厩与杂屋，前横一道倾颓的墙，向东直角折回，接上北侧，恰好围成了整个建筑轮廓的那个四方形。
从下面那荒草蔓生的废园仰望，北屋一排的外貌便足明白示人：多少年不曾有人住了。窗玻璃有的破了，有的积尘积垢厚厚一层；这里的百叶窗关死，那里的半开；无人修剪的常春藤，从石缝里疯长的野草，一绺绺垂挂的蛛网，还有窗下堆积的木料、砖块、灰泥、碎玻璃、破布烂条——都在诉说同一桩被弃置的故事。因为方位背阴，宅子这破败的一面带着一种阴冷萧索的冬日气象，哪怕在萨拉·利森误入废园的这个8月晴晨也不例外。她迷失在自己心绪的迷宫里，缓缓走过早已翻除的花圃，走过荒草吞没的石子小径；眼睛机械地扫着四外，两脚机械地往前走——只要寻得路的痕迹，通到哪里都行。
主人在育儿室里那几句话传到她心上的一震，可以说把她的整个天性逼上了绝地，也终于唤起她道德上的勇气，拿起一件最终而孤注一掷的决心作武器。

===Original===

Wandering more and more slowly along the pathways of the forsaken garden, as the course of her ideas withdrew her more and more completely from all outward things, she stopped insensibly on an open patch of ground, which had once been a well-kept lawn, and which still commanded a full view of the long range of uninhabited north rooms.
“What binds me to give the letter to my master at all?” she thought to herself, smoothing out the crumpled paper dreamily in the palm of her hand. “My mistress died without making me swear to do that. Can she visit it on me from the other world, if I keep the promises I swore to observe, and do no more? May I not risk the worst that can happen, so long as I hold religiously to all that I undertook to do on my oath?”
She paused here in reasoning with herself—her superstitious fears still influencing her out of doors, in the daylight, as they had influenced her in her own room, in the time of darkness. She paused—then fell to smoothing the letter again, and began to recall the terms of the solemn engagement which Mrs. Treverton had forced her to contract.
What had she actually bound herself to do? Not to destroy the letter, and not to take it away with her if she left the house. Beyond that, Mrs. Treverton’s desire had been that the letter should be given to her husband. Was that last wish binding on the person to whom it had been confided? Yes. As binding as an oath? No.
As she arrived at that conclusion, she looked up.
At first her eyes rested vacantly on the lonely, deserted north front of the house; gradually they became attracted by one particular window exactly in the middle, on the floor above the ground—the largest and the gloomiest of all the row; suddenly they brightened with an expression of intelligence. She started; a faint flush of color flew into her cheeks, and she hastily advanced closer to the wall of the house.
The panes of the large window were yellow with dust and dirt, and festooned about fantastically with cobwebs. Below it was a heap of rubbish, scattered over the dry mould of what might once have been a bed of flowers or shrubs. The form of the bed was still marked out by an oblong boundary of weeds and rank grass. She followed it irresolutely all round, looking up at the window at every step—then stopped close under it, glanced at the letter in her hand, and said to herself abruptly—
“I’ll risk it!”

===Chinese===

她沿废园的小径越走越慢，心念也越抽越远，把一切身外之物尽数擖开，不知不觉停在一片开阔地——那里原是一方精心侍弄的草坪，如今仍正对着北屋那一长排空置的房间。
「究竟是什么捆着我，非把信交给老爷不可？」她暗自思量，恍恍惚惚地把手心里那团皱纸抚平。「太太死时，并没有叫我起誓做这件事。我若守住誓言所命的一切、此外不做半点——她还能从另一个世界降罪于我么？只要我恭恭谨谨守住誓约里应承的一切，最坏的结局我就不能冒一冒险么？」
她自辩到这里停住了——那迷信的恐惧，在屋外、在天光之下，仍同方才在她自己屋里、在黑暗之中一样辖制着她。她停住——又开始抚平那封信，细细回想特雷弗顿太太逼她立下的庄重盟约的条款。
她实际应承了什么？不毁掉那封信；离开宅子的话，不随身带走。此外，特雷弗顿太太的心愿是信要交到她丈夫手里。这最后一愿，对受托之人也有约束力么？有。有誓言那么强的约束力么？没有。
得出这个结论时，她抬起头来。
起初她的眼睛空落落地停在宅子那荒僻冷落的北正面；渐渐地被正中一扇窗吸住了——就在底层之上那一排，是整排窗里最大、最晦暗的一扇；葠地，那双眼睛亮了，透出顿悟的神采。她一惊；淡淡的一层血色飞上双颊，急急向宅墙凑近。
那扇大窗的玻璃积尘蒙垢，黄了，四周蛛网垂挂得光怪陆离。窗下堆着一堆垃圾，散落在干土上——那地方从前或许是花床或灌木床。床形犹在，用一遭杂草恶草圈出的长方边界标着。她犹疑地绕着走了一整圈，步步抬头望那扇窗——随后在窗下站定，看一眼手里的信，突然对自己说——
「我冒这个险！」

===Original===

As the words fell from her lips, she hastened back to the inhabited part of the house, followed the passage on the kitchen-floor which led to the housekeeper’s room, entered it, and took down from a nail in the wall a bunch of keys, having a large ivory label attached to the ring that connected them, on which was inscribed, “Keys of the North Rooms.”
She placed the keys on a writing-table near her, took up a pen, and rapidly added these lines on the blank side of the letter which she had written under her mistress’s dictation—

> “If this paper should ever be found (which I pray with my whole heart it never may be), I wish to say that I have come to the resolution of hiding it, because I dare not show the writing that it contains to my master, to whom it is addressed. In doing what I now propose to do, though I am acting against my mistress’s last wishes, I am not breaking the solemn engagement which she obliged me to make before her on her deathbed. That engagement forbids me to destroy this letter, or to take it away with me if I leave the house. I shall do neither—my purpose is to conceal it in the place, of all others, where I think there is least chance of its ever being found again. Any hardship or misfortune which may follow as a consequence of this deceitful proceeding on my part, will fall on myself. Others, I believe in my conscience, will be the happier for the hiding of the dreadful Secret which this letter contains.”

===Chinese===

话音一落，她便赶回宅子里住人的一翼，穿过厨房那层通往管家房的过道，进了屋，从墙上一枚钉子摘下一大串钥匙；串起钥匙的环上拴着一块象牙大牌，牌上刻着「北屋诸房钥匙」。
她把钥匙放在近旁的写字台上，拿起笔，在那封奉太太囗授写就的信的背面空白处，飞快添上以下几行——

> 「这纸若有一日被人寻见（我全心祈祷永不），我要说明：我决意把它藏起，因为我不敢把里面的字给我的主人看——信正是写给他的。我如今要做的事，虽违了我主母的遗愿，却没有背弃她逼我在她临终榻前立下的庄重盟约。那盟约禁我毁掉此信，禁我离开此宅时将它随身带走。两样我都不做——我的打算，是把它藏到一个地方，在我看，天下再没有比那里更难被重新寻见的地方。我这份欺瞒行径招来的一切苦厄不幸，都落我一人身上。至于旁人——我凭良心相信——倒会因这封藏着可怕秘密的信之藏匿，而过得更安乐。」

===Original===

She signed those lines with her name—pressed them hurriedly over the blotting-pad that lay with the rest of the writing materials on the table—took the note in her hand, after first folding it up—and then, snatching at the bunch of keys, with a look all round her as if she dreaded being secretly observed, left the room. All her actions since she had entered it had been hasty and sudden; she was evidently afraid of allowing herself one leisure moment to reflect.
On quitting the housekeeper’s room, she turned to the left, ascended a back staircase, and unlocked a door at the top of it. A cloud of dust flew all about her as she softly opened the door; a mouldy coolness made her shiver as she crossed a large stone hall, with some black old family portraits hanging on the walls, the canvases of which were bulging out of the frames. Ascending more stairs, she came upon a row of doors, all leading into rooms on the first floor of the north side of the house.
She knelt down, putting the letter on the boards beside her, opposite the keyhole of the fourth door she came to after reaching the top of the stairs, peered in distrustfully for an instant, then began to try the different keys till she found one that fitted the lock. She had great difficulty in accomplishing this, from the violence of her agitation, which made her hands tremble to such a degree that she was hardly able to keep the keys separate one from the other. At length she succeeded in opening the door. Thicker clouds of dust than she had yet met with flew out the moment the interior of the room was visible; a dry, airless, suffocating atmosphere almost choked her as she stooped to pick up the letter from the floor. She recoiled from it at first, and took a few steps back toward the staircase. But she recovered her resolution immediately.
“I can’t go back now!” she said, desperately, and entered the room.
She did not remain in it more than two or three minutes. When she came out again her face was white with fear, and the hand which had held the letter when she went into the room held nothing now but a small rusty key.
After locking the door again, she examined the large bunch of keys which she had taken from the housekeeper’s room, with closer attention than she had yet bestowed on them.

===Chinese===

她在那几行下签了名——急急压在桌上随笔墨一同放着的吸墨纸上——先把信折好，握在手里——随即一把抓起那串钥匙，四下里张了一眼，仿佛怕有人在暗里窥看，出了屋。她进屋后的一应动作，全是急促的、突兀的；显然，她怕容自己得一刻闲工夫去细想。
出了管家房，她向左转，登上一道后楼梯，开了顶头一扇门。轻轻推门时，一团尘雾扑了她满身；穿过一间大石堂时，一股霉腐的阴凉叫她直打寒噤——堂壁上挂着几幅发黑的老祖宗画像，画布都从框子里鼓胀出来。再上几级楼梯，她见到一排门，扇扇通向宅子北侧第一层的房间。
她跪下来，把信搁在身边的木地板上，正对着楼梯顶头数来第四扇门的锁孔，疑疑忌忌地朝里窥了一瞬，便挨个试起钥匙来，直到找着一把对锁的。这事她做得极艰难：激荡得太厉害，手抖得几乎分不清哪把是哪把。到底还是开了门。屋里一露面，一股比先前遇到的都浓的尘雾扑出来；她俯身去捡地上的信时，一股干燥、不透风、令人窒息的气味几乎噎住她。起初她畏缩了一下，朝楼梯退了几步。但立刻又拿定了主意。
「如今回不了头了！」她豁出去似的说，进了屋。
她在屋里待了不过两三分钟。再出来时，她满脸惧白，进屋时握着信的那只手，如今只攥着一把生了锈的小钥匙。
重新锁好门后，她把从管家房取来的那大串钥匙细看了一遍，比先前看得仔细得多。

===Original===

Besides the ivory label attached to the ring that connected them, there were smaller labels, of parchment, tied to the handles of some of the keys, to indicate the rooms to which they gave admission. The particular key which she had used had one of these labels hanging to it. She held the little strip of parchment close to the light, and read on it, in written characters faded by time—

> “The Myrtle Room.”

===Chinese===

象牙大牌之外，有几把钥匙的柄上还拴着更小的羊皮纸签，标明各把开哪间屋。她方才用的那把，正挂着这样一枚签。她把那小条羊皮纸凑近亮处，见上面以被岁月褪淡的字迹写着——

> 「桃金娘房。」

===Original===

The room in which the letter was hidden had a name, then! A prettily sounding name that would attract most people, and keep pleasantly in their memories. A name to be distrusted by her, after what she had done, on that very account.
She took her housewife from its usual place in the pocket of her apron, and, with the scissors which it contained, cut the label from the key. Was it enough to destroy that one only? She lost herself in a maze of useless conjecture; and ended by cutting off the other labels, from no other motive than instinctive suspicion of them.
Carefully gathering up the strips of parchment from the floor, she put them, along with the little rusty key which she had brought out of the Myrtle Room, in the empty pocket of her apron. Then, carrying the large bunch of keys in her hand, and carefully locking the doors that she had opened on her way to the north side of Porthgenna Tower, she retraced her steps to the housekeeper’s room, entered it without seeing anybody, and hung up the bunch of keys again on the nail in the wall.
Fearful, as the morning hours wore on, of meeting with some of the female servants, she next hastened back to her bedroom. The candle she had left there was still burning feebly in the fresh daylight. When she drew aside the window-curtain, after extinguishing the candle, a shadow of her former fear passed over her face, even in the broad daylight that now flowed in upon it. She opened the window, and leaned out eagerly into the cool air.
Whether for good or for evil, the fatal Secret was hidden now—the act was done. There was something calming in the first consciousness of that one fact. She could think more composedly, after that, of herself, and of the uncertain future that lay before her.
Under no circumstances could she have expected to remain in her situation, now that the connection between herself and her mistress had been severed by death. She knew that Mrs. Treverton, in the last days of her illness, had earnestly recommended her maid to Captain Treverton’s kindness and protection, and she felt assured that the wife’s last entreaties, in this as in all other instances, would be viewed as the most sacred of obligations by the husband. But could she accept protection and kindness at the hand of the master whom she had been accessory to deceiving, and whom she had now committed herself to deceiving still?

===Chinese===

藏信的那间屋，原来有名有姓！一个大多数人听了都会心动、都会愉快地记住的悦耳名字。而在她，干了这桩事之后，正因这名字悦耳，倒要提防它。
她从围裙口袋惯放针线包的地方取出针线包，用里面的剪刀把那枚纸签从钥匙上剪下。只毁这一枚就够了么？她陷进一迷宫的无谓猜度；末了索性把别的纸签也统统剪掉——除了对它们本能的疑忌，再没有别的理由。
她小心地把地上的纸签一一拾起，连同从桃金娘房带出来的那把生锈小钥匙，一起放进围裙空着的口袋。然后一手提着那大串钥匙，来时开过的门扇扇回锁，沿原路折回管家房，没撞见任何人，把钥匙重新挂上墙上那枚钉子。
晨光渐深，她怕撞见女仆们，随即赶回自己卧室。留在那里的蜡烛，在清新的日光里还幽幽地燃着。吹熄蜡烛、拉开窗帘时，即使在漫进屋来的大天光里，先前的恐惧仍有一道阴影掠过她的脸。她推开窗，急切地把身子探进清凉的空气里。
无论为福为祸，那致死的秘密如今是藏好了——事已做下。头一回意识到这一事实，倒有一种叫人心定下来的东西。此后她便能更平静地想想自己，想想眼前渺茫的未来。
主母一死，她同主母的关联便断了，无论怎样她也不能指望再留在这个位置上。她知道太太在病末的几天里，曾恳切把她这女仆托付给特雷弗顿舰长的仁慈与庇护；她也确信，同其余一切一样，妻子临终的嘱托，在丈夫眼里必被奉为最神圣的义务。可是，对于那位她曾助着欺瞒、如今又立意继续欺瞒下去的主人，她还能受他的庇护与善待么？

===Original===

The bare idea of such baseness was so revolting, that she accepted, almost with a sense of relief, the one sad alternative that remained—the alternative of leaving the house immediately.
And how was she to leave it? By giving formal warning, and so exposing herself to questions which would be sure to confuse and terrify her? Could she venture to face her master again, after what she had done—to face him, when his first inquiries would refer to her mistress, when he would be certain to ask her for the last mournful details, for the slightest word that had been spoken during the death-scene that she alone had witnessed? She started to her feet, as the certain consequences of submitting herself to that unendurable trial all crowded together warningly on her mind, took her cloak from its place on the wall, and listened at her door in sudden suspicion and fear. Had she heard footsteps? Was her master sending for her already?
No; all was silent outside. A few tears rolled over her cheeks as she put on her bonnet, and felt that she was facing, by the performance of that simple action, the last, and perhaps the hardest to meet, of the cruel necessities in which the hiding of the Secret had involved her. There was no help for it. She must run the risk of betraying everything, or brave the double trial of leaving Porthgenna Tower, and leaving it secretly.
Secretly—as a thief might go? Without a word to her master? without so much as one line of writing to thank him for his kindness and to ask his pardon? She had unlocked her desk, and had taken from it her purse, one or two letters, and a little book of Wesley’s Hymns, before these considerations occurred to her. They made her pause in the act of shutting up the desk. “Shall I write?” she asked herself, “and leave the letter here, to be found when I am gone?”
A little more reflection decided her in the affirmative. As rapidly as her pen could form the letters, she wrote a few lines addressed to Captain Treverton, in which she confessed to having kept a secret from his knowledge which had been left in her charge to divulge; adding, that she honestly believed no harm could come to him, or to anyone in whom he was interested, by her failing to perform the duty entrusted to her; and ended by asking his pardon for leaving the house secretly, and by begging, as a last favor, that no search might ever be made for her.

===Chinese===

单是想一想这样的下贱，便叫她恶心；于是剩下的那一条可悲的路——即刻离开这座宅子——她几乎是带着一种解脱之感认下的。
可她怎么个离法？正式告辞，然后听任盘问，把自己搅得又乱又怕？她做了那样的事，还敢再见主人一面么——见他，而他开口头一件事必是问她的太太，必是要她讲临终的种种伤心细节，要她讲那只有她一人亲见的死别场面里吐过的每一个字？一想到那场受不住的考验及其必然后果，齐涌上心头发着警告，她霍地站起，从墙上取下斗篷，又在门边带着突发的疑惧听了听。是脚步声么？主人已经打发人来叫她了么？
不；门外一片寂静。她戴上软帽时，几颗泪滚下面颊——她感到，就在这一顶帽子的动作里，她正迎向藏匿秘密给她带来的诸多无情必需之中，最后一件、或许也最难承当的一件。无可奈何。她要么冒泄底之险，要么豁出去受那双重考验：离开波斯根纳塔楼，而且是不辞而别地走。
不辞而别——像贼一样地走么？对主人一句话不留？连一行字也不写，谢他的恩、求他的恕？这些念头浮上时，她已开了书桌的锁，取出了钱包、一两封信和一本小小的卫斯理《圣诗集》。这些念头叫她在关书桌的动作里停住了。「我写不写？」她问自己，「把信留在这里，等我走了再被人发现？」
再一细想，她决意写了。她的笔以最快的速度草就数行，呈特雷弗顿舰长：她自认瞒下了一桩托付在她手里、本该吐露的秘密；又说，她诚心相信，她未践所托，于他、于他所挂念的任何人，都不会招来祸害；末了求他宽恕她不辞而别，并求他最后一件恩典：永远不要寻访她。

===Original===

Having sealed this short note, and left it on her table, with her master’s name written outside, she listened again at the door; and, after satisfying herself that no one was yet stirring, began to descend the stairs at Porthgenna Tower for the last time.
At the entrance of the passage leading to the nursery she stopped. The tears which she had restrained since leaving her room began to flow again. Urgent as her reasons now were for effecting her departure without a moment’s loss of time, she advanced, with the strangest inconsistency, a few steps toward the nursery door. Before she had gone far, a slight noise in the lower part of the house caught her ear and instantly checked her further progress.
While she stood doubtful, the grief at her heart—a greater grief than any she had yet betrayed—rose irresistibly to her lips, and burst from them in one deep gasping sob. The sound of it seemed to terrify her into a sense of the danger of her position, if she delayed a moment longer. She ran out again to the stairs, reached the kitchen-floor in safety, and made her escape by the garden door which the servant had opened for her at the dawn of the morning.
On getting clear of the premises at Porthgenna Tower, instead of taking the nearest path over the moor that led to the high road, she diverged to the church; but stopped before she came to it, at the public well of the neighborhood, which had been sunk near the cottages of the Porthgenna fishermen. Cautiously looking round her, she dropped into the well the little rusty key which she had brought out of the Myrtle Room; then hurried on, and entered the churchyard. She directed her course straight to one of the graves, situated a little apart from the rest. On the headstone were inscribed these words:

> Sacred to the Memory

of

Hugh Polwheal,

Aged 26 Years.

He Met With His Death

Through the Fall of a Rock

in

Porthgenna Mine,

December 17th, 1823.

===Chinese===

封好这封短信，留在桌上，外皮写着主人的名字，她又到门边听了听；确信还没有人起身，便开始最后一次走下波斯根纳塔楼的楼梯。
在通往育儿室的过道口，她站住了。出屋后一直忍着的眼泪，又开始流。虽然此刻她亟需一刻也不耽搁地脱身，却怀着最不可解的矛盾心理，朝育儿室的门挪了几步。还没走多远，屋子下层一点轻微的响动入了耳，立时止住了她。
她迟疑地立着，心底的悲恸——比她此前流露过的任何悲恸都重——不由分说地涌上唇边，化作一声深长抽噎夺囗而出。这声音仿佛把她吓醒，叫她明白再耽搁一刻便有多大凶险。她返身奔回楼梯，平安到了厨房那层，从清晨仆人替她开过的花园门逃了出去。
一出了波斯根纳塔楼的地界，她没走通往大路的最近荒径，却绕向教堂；还没到，先在邻里的公井旁停了步——井就凿在波斯根纳渔户的村舍近旁。她小心地四下一望，把从桃金娘房带出的那把生锈小钥匙丢进井里；然后匆匆前行，进了教堂墓园。她径直走向一座坟——同其余的坟稍稍隔开的一座。墓碑上刻着这些字：

> 谨以此碑
> 纪念
> 休·波尔惠尔（Hugh Polwheal），
> 享年二十六岁。
> 他因波斯根纳矿中
> 岩石崩坠
> 而罹难，
> 时在一八二三年十二月十七日。

===Original===

Gathering a few leaves of grass from the grave, Sarah opened the little book of Wesley’s *Hymns* which she had brought with her from the bedroom of Porthgenna Tower, and placed the leaves delicately and carefully between the pages. As she did this, the wind blew open the titlepage of the *Hymns*, and displayed this inscription on it, written in large, clumsy characters—“Sarah Leeson, her book. The gift of Hugh Polwheal.”
Having secured the blades of grass between the pages of the book, she retraced her way toward the path leading to the high road. Arrived on the moor, she took out of her apron pocket the parchment labels that had been cut from the keys, and scattered them under the furze-bushes.
“Gone,” she said, “as I am gone! God help and forgive me—it is all done and over now!”
With those words she turned her back on the old house and the sea-view below it, and followed the moorland path on her way to the high road.
Four hours afterward Captain Treverton desired one of the servants at Porthgenna Tower to inform Sarah Leeson that he wished to hear all she had to tell him of the dying moments of her mistress. The messenger returned with looks and words of amazement, and with the letter that Sarah had addressed to her master in his hand.
The moment Captain Treverton had read the letter, he ordered an immediate search to be made after the missing woman. She was so easy to describe and to recognize, by the premature grayness of her hair, by the odd, scared look in her eyes, and by her habit of constantly talking to herself, that she was traced with certainty as far as Truro. In that large town the track of her was lost, and never recovered again.
Rewards were offered; the magistrates of the district were interested in the case; all that wealth and power could do to discover her was done—and done in vain. No clue was found to suggest a suspicion of her whereabouts, or to help in the slightest degree toward explaining the nature of the secret at which she had hinted in her letter. Her master never saw her again, never heard of her again, after the morning of the twenty-third of August, eighteen hundred and twenty-nine.

===Chinese===

萨拉从坟头摘下几片草叶，打开那本从波斯根纳塔楼卧室带来的卫斯理《圣诗集》（*Hymns*），把草叶细细地、小心地夹进书页。正夹着，风掀开了《圣诗集》的扉页，露出上面的题字，一笔一画又大又拙——「萨拉·利森藏书。休·波尔惠尔赠」。
草叶夹妥，她折回通往大路的小径。到了荒原上，她从围裙口袋里取出从钥匙上剪下的那些羊皮纸签，撒进了荆豆丛下。
「去了，」她说，「同我一样地去了！上帝救助我、饶恕我——如今一切都做完、都过去了！」
说完这几句，她背转身，背对那座老宅和宅下的大海，沿荒原小径向大路走去。
四个钟头之后，特雷弗顿舰长吩咐波斯根纳塔楼的一名仆人去告知萨拉·利森，他想听她讲讲太太弥留时刻的一切。去的人满脸满囗的惊讶转了回来，手里拿着萨拉呈她主人的那封信。
特雷弗顿舰长一读完信，即刻下令追寻那失踪的女人。凭她那早衰的灰发、那古怪惊惶的眼神、那自言自语不停的积习，她太好描摹、太好辨认了，一路被确凿追踪到特鲁罗（Truro）。在那座大镇子里，她的踪迹便断了，此后再没接上。
悬赏出过了；本区的治安官也过问了此案；财富与权势能为寻她做的一切都做了——都做了而徒劳。没有寻得半点线索，能引人疑到她的去向，也丝毫不能解明她在信里影影绰绰点过的那个秘密的性质。自一八二九年八月二十三日那个早晨之后，她的主人再没见过她，再没听到过她的消息。

# Book II / 第二部
===Original===



===Chinese===

第二部

## I Fifteen Years After / 一 十五年后
===Original===

The church of Long Beckley (a large agricultural village in one of the midland counties of England), although a building in no way remarkable either for its size, its architecture, or its antiquity, possesses, nevertheless, one advantage which mercantile London has barbarously denied to the noble cathedral church of St. Paul. It has plenty of room to stand in, and it can consequently be seen with perfect convenience from every point of view, all around the compass.
The large open space around the church can be approached in three different directions. There is a road from the village, leading straight to the principal door. There is a broad gravel walk, which begins at the vicarage gates, crosses the churchyard, and stops, as in duty bound, at the vestry entrance. There is a footpath over the fields, by which the lord of the manor, and the gentry in general who live in his august neighborhood, can reach the side door of the building, whenever their natural humility may incline them to encourage Sabbath observance in the stables by going to church, like the lower sort of worshipers, on their own legs.
At half-past seven o’clock, on a certain fine summer morning, in the year eighteen hundred and forty-four, if any observant stranger had happened to be standing in some unnoticed corner of the churchyard, and to be looking about him with sharp eyes, he would probably have been the witness of proceedings which might have led him to believe that there was a conspiracy going on in Long Beckley, of which the church was the rallying-point, and some of the most respectable inhabitants the principal leaders. Supposing him to have been looking toward the vicarage as the clock chimed the half-hour, he would have seen the vicar of Long Beckley, the Reverend Doctor Chennery, leaving his house suspiciously, by the back way, glancing behind him guiltily as he approached the gravel walk that led to the vestry, stopping mysteriously just outside the door, and gazing anxiously down the road that led from the village.
Assuming that our observant stranger would, upon this, keep out of sight, and look down the road, like the vicar, he would next have seen the clerk of the church—an austere, yellow-faced man—a Protestant Loyola in appearance, and a working shoemaker by trade—approaching with a look of unutterable mystery in his face, and a bunch of big keys in his hands.

===Chinese===

朗贝克利（Long Beckley）——英格兰中部某郡一个大大的农业村——的教堂，论大小、论建筑、论古旧，都毫不出奇，却有一桩优势，是市侩的伦敦残忍地拒不给予尊贵的圣保罗座堂的：它四周有的是立身之地，因而无论从哪个方位、绕罗盘一圈，都看得从容惬意。
教堂四周那片开阔地，有三个方向可以进。一条村路，直通正门。一条宽阔的砾石道，起于牧师宅的门闸，横穿教堂墓园，如职分所当，止于法衣室入口。还有一条田间小径，领主老爷连同住在「圣邻」左右的一般士绅，凡是天生的谦卑恰好鼓动起他们的兴致、想以身作则叫马厩也守安息日时，便可循此径走到教堂侧门——像下等信徒一样，用自己的两条腿走去。
一八四四年某个晴好的夏日早晨，七点半钟，倘有个眼尖的闲杂生人恰站在墓园某个不惹眼的角落里四下张望，多半会亲眼见着一幕，叫他以为朗贝克利正酝酿一桩阴谋：教堂是聚点，为首的是几位最体面的居民。假定钟敲半点时他正朝牧师宅那边望，便会看见朗贝克利的牧师切纳里博士（Reverend Doctor Chennery）出屋出得形迹可疑——走的是后门——近那条通往法衣室的砾石道时，做贼似的回头张望；在门外神秘地站定，朝村路那头焦灼地眺望。

===Original===

He would have seen the vicar nod in an abstracted manner to the clerk, and say, “Fine morning, Thomas. Have you had your breakfast yet?” He would have heard Thomas reply, with a suspicious regard for minute particulars: “I have had a cup of tea and a crust, Sir.” And he would then have seen these two local conspirators, after looking up with one accord at the church clock, draw off together to the side door which commanded a view of the footpath across the fields.
Following them—as our inquisitive stranger could not fail to do—he would have detected three more conspirators advancing along the footpath. The leader of this treasonable party was an elderly gentleman, with a weather-beaten face and a bluff, hearty manner. His two followers were a young gentleman and a young lady, walking arm-in-arm, and talking together in whispers. They were dressed in the plainest morning costume. The faces of both were rather pale, and the manner of the lady was a little flurried. Otherwise there was nothing remarkable to observe in them, until they came to the wicket-gate leading into the churchyard; and there the conduct of the young gentleman seemed, at first sight, rather inexplicable. Instead of holding the gate open for the lady to pass through, he hung back, allowed her to open it for herself, waited till she had got to the churchyard side, and then, stretching out his hand over the gate, allowed her to lead him through the entrance, as if he had suddenly changed from a grown man to a helpless little child.
Noting this, and remarking also that, when the party from the fields had arrived within greeting distance of the vicar, and when the clerk had used his bunch of keys to open the church-door, the young lady’s companion was led into the building (this time by Doctor Chennery’s hand), as he had been previously led through the wicket-gate, our observant stranger must have arrived at one inevitable conclusion—that the person requiring such assistance as this was suffering under the affliction of blindness. Startled a little by that discovery, he would have been still further amazed, if he had looked into the church, by seeing the blind man and the young lady standing together before the altar rails, with the elderly gentleman in parental attendance.

===Chinese===

他还会看见牧师心不在焉地朝教堂执事点点头，说：「早上天气好，托马斯（Thomas）。早饭用过没有？」他还会听见托马斯答话，对细枝末节一丝不苟地留心着：「用过一杯茶、一块干面包皮，先生。」随后他便看见这两位本地阴谋家，不约而同抬头望一眼教堂时钟，一齐退到那扇正对田间小径的侧门去。
跟着他们——我们那好事的生人断不会不跟——他还会侦得三名同谋沿田间小径行来。这一伙叛党的首领是位年长的绅士，风霜脸，一副爽直热诚的做派。随他的是一少一青两位，挽着臂，低声交谈。两人都穿着极素净的晨装。两张脸都颇苍白，女士的举止有些慌乱。除此之外本无可注意，直到他们走到通墓园的那扇便门——到了那里，青年绅士的行止乍看竟颇为费解：他非但不替女士拉开门请她先行，反倒逡巡不前，由她自己开门；待她到了墓园那侧，才隔着门伸过手来，由她牵着他进门——仿佛一霎间从大人变作无依无靠的小孩。
见此情形，再看到田间一行人走近牧师寒暄可及之处、执事用那串钥匙开了教堂门之后，年轻女士的同伴又被牵着进了堂——这一回牵他的是切纳里博士的手，先前牵他过便门一样——我们那位眼尖的生人便必得出一个无法回避的结论：需要这般搀扶的人，是个受失明之苦的人。这发现叫他微微一惊；他若再往堂里看，还要更吃一惊：盲青年同年轻女士并肩立在圣坛栏杆前，年长绅士一副父亲模样随侍在旁。

===Original===

Any suspicions he might now entertain that the bond which united the conspirators at that early hour of the morning was of the hymeneal sort, and that the object of their plot was to celebrate a wedding with the strictest secrecy, would have been confirmed in five minutes by the appearance of Doctor Chennery from the vestry in full canonicals, and by the reading of the marriage service in the reverend gentleman’s most harmonious officiating tones. The ceremony concluded, the attendant stranger must have been more perplexed than ever by observing that the persons concerned in it all separated, the moment the signing, the kissing, and congratulating duties proper to the occasion had been performed, and quickly retired in the various directions by which they had approached the church.
Leaving the clerk to return by the village road, the bride, bridegroom, and elderly gentleman to turn back by the footpath over the fields, and the visionary stranger of these pages to vanish out of them in any direction that he pleases—let us follow Doctor Chennery to the vicarage breakfast-table, and hear what he has to say about his professional exertions of the morning in the familiar atmosphere of his own family circle.
The persons assembled at the breakfast were, first, Mr. Phippen, a guest; secondly, Miss Sturch, a governess; thirdly, fourthly, and fifthly, Miss Louisa Chennery (aged eleven years), Miss Amelia Chennery (aged nine years), and Master Robert Chennery (aged eight years). There was no mother’s face present, to make the household picture complete. Doctor Chennery had been a widower since the birth of his youngest child.
The guest was an old college acquaintance of the vicar’s, and he was supposed to be now staying at Long Beckley for the benefit of his health. Most men of any character at all contrive to get a reputation of some sort which individualizes them in the social circle amid which they move. Mr. Phippen was a man of some little character, and he lived with great distinction in the estimation of his friends on the reputation of being A Martyr to Dyspepsia.
Wherever Mr. Phippen went, the woes of Mr. Phippen’s stomach went with him. He dieted himself publicly, and physicked himself publicly.

===Chinese===

他此刻若疑心：这班阴谋家清早聚拢的纽带，属于喜结连理一路；这桩密谋的目的，是要以最严的秘密办一场婚礼——那么不出五分钟，疑心便要坐实：切纳里博士法袍法冠齐整地从法衣室现身，又以这位可敬人物主持圣事时最和美的声调，诵起婚娶仪文。礼成之后，那位在场的生人只怕要愈发纳帘：与事诸人，一俟签名、亲吻、道贺诸般本分应酬做罢，立时各自散去，循着来时各路，迅速退远了。
让执事循村路自去，让新娘新郎同年长绅士循田间小径折返，让书页间这位虚构的生人随他高兴朝哪边隐没——我们且跟切纳里博士到牧师宅的早餐桌旁，听听他在自家的融融天伦里，怎样讲述他晨间的职分辛劳。
围早餐桌而坐的，头一位是菲平先生（Mr. Phippen），一位客人；第二位是斯特奇小姐（Miss Sturch），一位家庭女教师；第三、第四、第五位，是路易莎·切纳里小姐（十一岁）、阿米莉亚·切纳里小姐（九岁）、罗伯特·切纳里小少爷（八岁）。没有母亲的脸在场，这幅家宅图缺了一角。自幼子落地，切纳里博士便是鳏夫。
客人是牧师大学时代的老相识，如今住在朗贝克利，据说是为了将养身体。但凡有点脾性的男人，总能挣得某种令他在交际圈里独树一格的名声。菲平先生正有点小脾性，靠着「消化不良之殉道者」的名声，在朋友们的评价里活得风生水起。
菲平先生走到哪里，菲平先生那副肠胃的苦难便跟到哪里。他当众忌囗，当众服药。

===Original===

He was so intensely occupied with himself and his maladies, that he would let a chance acquaintance into the secret of the condition of his tongue at five minutes’ notice; being just as perpetually ready to discuss the state of his digestion as people in general are to discuss the state of the weather. On this favorite subject, as on all others, he spoke with a wheedling gentleness of manner, sometimes in softly mournful, sometimes in languidly sentimental tones. His politeness was of the oppressively affectionate sort, and he used the word “dear” continually in addressing himself to others. Personally, he could not be called a handsome man. His eyes were watery, large, and light gray; they were always rolling from side to side in a state of moist admiration of something or somebody. His nose was long, drooping, profoundly melancholy—if such an expression may be permitted, in reference to that particular feature. For the rest, his lips had a lachrymose twist; his stature was small; his head large, bald, and loosely set on his shoulders; his manner of dressing himself eccentric, on the side of smartness; his age about five-and-forty; his condition that of a single man. Such was Mr. Phippen, the Martyr to Dyspepsia, and the guest of the vicar of Long Beckley.
Miss Sturch, the governess, may be briefly and accurately described as a young lady who had never been troubled with an idea or a sensation since the day when she was born. She was a little, plump, quiet, white-skinned, smiling, neatly dressed girl, wound up accurately to the performance of certain duties at certain times; and possessed of an inexhaustible vocabulary of commonplace talk, which dribbled placidly out of her lips whenever it was called for, always in the same quantity, and always of the same quality, at every hour in the day, and through every change in the seasons. Miss Sturch never laughed, and never cried, but took the safe middle course of smiling perpetually. She smiled when she came down on a morning in January, and said it was very cold. She smiled when she came down on a morning in July, and said it was very hot. She smiled when the bishop came once a year to see the vicar; she smiled when the butcher’s boy came every morning for orders.

===Chinese===

他满心满脑只装着自己和自己的病，路遇一个泛泛之交，五分钟内便肯把舌苔的秘密相告；谈论自家消化的状况，他同常人谈论天气一样，永远随叫随到。在这门心爱的题目上，也同在一切题目上一样，他说话带着哄人的温存，时而是柔柔的哀声，时而是恹恹的感伤。他的客气属于腻死人的亲热一路，开口称呼别人，「亲爱的」不离囗。论相貌，算不得俊：一双水汪汪的浅灰色大眼，永远朝两边滚动着，对某人某物湿漉漉地叹赏；鼻子长而下垂，深深的忧郁——若容许这样形容那个器官的话。其余的：唇角带一点哭相；个子矮小；头颅大而秃，松松地架在肩上；穿衣打扮入时得古怪；年约四十五；独身。这便是菲平先生，消化不良之殉道者，朗贝克利牧师之客。
至于家庭女教师斯特奇小姐，只需一句便说得准确：一位自落地之日起，从不曾为一个念头或一阵感觉所扰的年轻女士。她是个小巧、丰润、安静、皮肤白皙、面带微笑、衣着利落的姑娘，上得极准的发条，几点做什么，分毫不爽；又备着一副永不枯竭的陈言套语，一唤便汩汱地、安详地从唇间流出——量永远那么多，质永远那么好，一日不分晨昏，一年不分寒暑。斯特奇小姐从不笑出声，也从不哭，只走中间的稳妥路线：永远含笑。一月里下楼，她笑着，说天真冷。七月里下楼，她笑着，说天真热。主教一年来探牧师一次，她笑；肉铺伙计每晨来取菜单，她也笑。

===Original===

Let what might happen at the vicarage, nothing ever jerked Miss Sturch out of the one smooth groove in which she ran perpetually, always at the same pace. If she had lived in a royalist family, during the civil wars in England, she would have rung for the cook, to order dinner, on the morning of the execution of Charles the First. If Shakespeare had come back to life again, and had called at the vicarage at six o’clock on Saturday evening, to explain to Miss Sturch exactly what his views were in composing the tragedy of Hamlet, she would have smiled and said it was extremely interesting, until the striking of seven o’clock; at which time she would have left him in the middle of a sentence, to superintend the housemaid in the verification of the washing-book. A very estimable young person, Miss Sturch (as the ladies of Long Beckley were accustomed to say); so judicious with the children, and so attached to her household duties; such a well-regulated mind, and such a crisp touch on the piano; just nice-looking enough, just well-dressed enough, just talkative enough; not quite old enough, perhaps, and a little too much inclined to be embraceably plump about the region of the waist—but, on the whole, a most estimable young person—very much so, indeed.
On the characteristic peculiarities of Miss Sturch’s pupils, it is not necessary to dwell at very great length. Miss Louisa’s habitual weakness was an inveterate tendency to catch cold. Miss Amelia’s principal defect was a disposition to gratify her palate by eating supplementary dinners and breakfasts at unauthorized times and seasons. Master Robert’s most noticeable failings were caused by alacrity in tearing his clothes, and obtuseness in learning the multiplication table. The virtues of all three were of much the same nature—they were well grown, they were genuine children, and they were boisterously fond of Miss Sturch.
To complete the gallery of family portraits, an outline, at the least, must be attempted of the vicar himself. Doctor Chennery was, in a physical point of view, a credit to the establishment to which he was attached.

===Chinese===

牧师宅里任凭出什么事，都休想把斯特奇小姐从她那条光溜的辙里颠出来——她在里面恒跑恒速。她若生在内战年代的保王党人家，查理一世伏刑的那天早晨，她照样打铃唤厨娘，吩咐晚膳。莎士比亚若还魂转世，礼拜六傍晚六点登门牧师宅，要向斯特奇小姐详陈他作《哈姆雷特》悲剧的本意，她会含笑说「真有趣极了」，一直到七点钟敲响；届时她会擖下他半句话不管，去监督女仆核对洗衣簿。一位极可敬的年轻女子，这位斯特奇小姐（朗贝克利的太太们惯常这样说）；带孩子那样有分寸，家务那样尽本分；心思那样规整，钢琴指触那样爽利；相貌恰可人，衣着恰得体，谈吐恰不俗；年纪兴许还不够老些，腰身也稍稍过于丰润可拥——但总的说，是位极可敬的年轻女子——当真极可敬。
至于斯特奇小姐学生们的特性，无须多费笔墨。路易莎小姐的痼疾，是落落感冒的老积习。阿米莉亚小姐的主要缺陷，是在未奉核准的时辰加餐加饭、饱口腹之欲的癖性。罗伯特小少爷最显眼的过失，一是撕衣裳的勤快，二是学乘法表的迟钝。三个人的美德大致同类——个头长得好，是地道的孩子，而且闹嚷嚷地喜欢斯特奇小姐。
要补全家门画像的画廊，至少也得给牧师本人描个轮廓。从体格上看，切纳里博士为他所供职的圣公会增光。

===Original===

He stood six feet two in his shooting-shoes; he weighed fifteen stone; he was the best bowler in the Long Beckley cricket-club; he was a strictly orthodox man in the matter of wine and mutton; he never started disagreeable theories about people’s future destinies in the pulpit, never quarreled with anybody out of the pulpit, never buttoned up his pockets when the necessities of his poor brethren (Dissenters included) pleaded with him to open them. His course through the world was a steady march along the high and dry middle of a safe turnpike-road. The serpentine side-paths of controversy might open as alluringly as they pleased on his right hand and on his left, but he kept on his way sturdily, and never regarded them. Innovating young recruits in the Church army might entrappingly open the Thirty-nine Articles under his very nose, but the veteran’s wary eye never looked a hair’s-breadth further than his own signature at the bottom of them. He knew as little as possible of theology, he had never given the Privy Council a minute’s trouble in the whole course of his life, he was innocent of all meddling with the reading or writing of pamphlets, and he was quite incapable of finding his way to the platform of Exeter Hall. In short, he was the most unclerical of clergymen—but, for all that, he had such a figure for a surplice as is seldom seen. Fifteen stone weight of upright muscular flesh, without an angry spot or sore place in any part of it, has the merit of suggesting stability, at any rate—an excellent virtue in pillars of all kinds, but an especially precious quality, at the present time, in a pillar of the Church.
As soon as the vicar entered the breakfast-parlor, the children assailed him with a chorus of shouts. He was a severe disciplinarian in the observance of punctuality at mealtimes; and he now stood convicted by the clock of being too late for breakfast by a quarter of an hour.
“Sorry to have kept you waiting, Miss Sturch,” said the vicar; “but I have a good excuse for being late this morning.”
“Pray don’t mention it, Sir,” said Miss Sturch, blandly rubbing her plump little hands one over the other. “A beautiful morning. I fear we shall have another warm day.—Robert, my love, your elbow is on the table.—A beautiful morning, indeed!”
“Stomach still out of order—eh, Phippen?” asked the vicar, beginning to carve the ham.
Mr.

===Chinese===

他穿猎装鞋身高六英尺二；体重十五斯通；是朗贝克利板球会的头号投手；在酒与羊肉之事上是个严正的正统派；他在讲坛上从不发为难人的高论、论人身后命运，下了讲坛从不与人拌嘴，穷教友（含异宗）的急需叩他的钱袋，他从不扣紧袋口。他行世的路径，是沿着一条安全收费大道高而干的中线稳步行军。争论的蛇曲小径尽可在他左右妩媚地岔开，他只顾壮实地赶路，看也不看。教会大军里维新的年轻新兵，尽可诱哄着把《三十九条信纲》在他鼻子底下翻开，而老将警觉的眼睛，从不多看信纲底页他自己签名一根发丝宽的地方。神学他懂得越少越好；枢密院在有生之年从未受过他一分钟的烦扰；他清白得不曾染指小册子的读与写；要找到去埃克塞特会堂讲台的路，他全然无此本事。总之，他是教士里最不像教士的教士——可是话虽如此，他那一副穿白法衣的身架，世间少有。十五斯通笔直的筋肉，通体没有一处火气、没有一处疮痛，至少有一样好处：叫人望之觉得牢靠——这是各类柱石共有的美德，而在今日，于教会的柱石，尤为可贵。
牧师一进早餐间，孩子们便齐声喊嚷着围攻他。在守用饭时刻一事上，他是个严纪律者；如今钟表作证，他迟到了一刻钟。
「抱歉叫诸位久等了，斯特奇小姐，」牧师说；「不过今早迟到，我有个极好的理由。」
「请千万别提，先生，」斯特奇小姐说，把两只丰润的小手温文地搓着。「早晨真美。只怕又是一个热天。——罗伯特，宝贝，你的胳膊肘上桌了。——早晨真真是美！」
「胃还是不舒服——呃，菲平？」牧师问，动手切火腿。

===Original===

Phippen shook his large head dolefully, placed his yellow forefinger, ornamented with a large turquoise ring, on the centre check of his light-green summer waistcoat—looked piteously at Doctor Chennery, and sighed—removed the finger, and produced from the breast pocket of his wrapper a little mahogany case—took out of it a neat pair of apothecary’s scales, with the accompanying weights, a morsel of ginger, and a highly polished silver nutmeg-grater. “Dear Miss Sturch will pardon an invalid?” said Mr. Phippen, beginning to grate the ginger feebly into the nearest teacup.
“Guess what has made me a quarter of an hour late this morning,” said the vicar, looking mysteriously all round the table.
“Lying in bed, papa,” cried the three children, clapping their hands in triumph.
“What do *you* say, Miss Sturch?” asked Doctor Chennery.
Miss Sturch smiled as usual, rubbed her hands as usual, cleared her throat softly as usual, looked at the tea-urn, and begged, with the most graceful politeness, to be excused if she said nothing.
“Your turn now, Phippen,” said the vicar. “Come, guess what has kept me late this morning.”
“My dear friend,” said Mr. Phippen, giving the Doctor a brotherly squeeze of the hand, “don’t ask me to guess—I know! I saw what you eat at dinner yesterday—I saw what you drank after dinner. No digestion could stand it—not even yours. Guess what has made you late this morning? Pooh! pooh! I know. You dear, good soul, you have been taking physic!”
“Haven’t touched a drop, thank God, for the last ten years!” said Doctor Chennery, with a look of devout gratitude. “No, no; you’re all wrong. The fact is, I have been to church; and what do you think I have been doing there? Listen, Miss Sturch—listen, girls, with all your ears. Poor blind young Frankland is a happy man at last—I have married him to our dear Rosamond Treverton this very morning!”
“Without telling us, papa!” cried the two girls together in their shrillest tones of vexation and surprise. “Without telling us, when you know how we should have liked to see it!”
“That was the very reason why I did not tell you, my dears,” answered the vicar. “Young Frankland has not got so used to his affliction yet, poor fellow, as to bear being publicly pitied and stared at in the character of a blind bridegroom.

===Chinese===

菲平先生凄然摇了摇那颗大脑袋，把戴着大绿松石戒指的黄手指，按在浅绿夏马甲正中那方格上——可怜巴巴地望着切纳里博士，叹了口气——挪开手指，从晨袍胸袋里掏出一只小小的红木匣——匣里取出一副精巧的药戥子、成套的砝码、一小块姜，和一只擦得锃亮的银制肉豆蔻擦。「亲爱的斯特奇小姐肯体谅一个病人罢？」菲平先生说，开始有气无力地把姜擦进手边最近的茶杯里。
「猜猜看，今早是什么叫我迟了一刻钟？」牧师说，神秘地环视全桌。
「赖床，爸爸！」三个孩子嚷，得意地拍手。
「*您*怎么说，斯特奇小姐？」切纳里博士问。
斯特奇小姐照常微笑，照常搓手，照常轻轻清嗓，望了望茶瓮，而后以最优雅的礼貌，请求恕她不作声。
「该你了，菲平，」牧师说。「来，猜猜今早什么绊住了我。」
「我亲爱的朋友，」菲平先生说着，兄弟般紧握了握博士的手，「别叫我猜——我知道！我看见了您昨天正餐吃的什么——看见了您餐后喝的什么。什么消化也禁不住——您的也禁不住。猜猜今早什么让您迟到？呸！呸！我知道。您这位亲爱的大好人，您服药来着！」
「托上帝的福，十年没沾过一滴了！」切纳里博士说，一脸虔诚的感恩。「不对，不对；你们全猜错。实情是，我去了教堂；你们猜我在那里做了什么？听着，斯特奇小姐——姑娘们，竖起耳朵听。可怜的盲青年弗兰克兰，到底成了幸福的人——就在今早，我把我们亲爱的罗莎蒙德·特雷弗顿嫁给了他！」
「都不告诉我们，爸爸！」两个姑娘一齐用最尖利的恼惊声嚷。「都不告诉我们，您明知道我们多想去看！」
「那正是我不告诉你们的缘故哪，宝贝们，」牧师答道。「可怜的孩子，弗兰克兰还没习惯他的不幸到那个地步，受不住当着众人、以一个盲新郎的身份被怜惜、被盯看。

He had such a nervous horror of being an object of curiosity on his wedding-day, and Rosamond, like a kindhearted girl as she is, was so anxious that his slightest caprices should be humored, that we settled to have the wedding at an hour in the morning when no idlers were likely to be lounging about the neighborhood of the church. I was bound over to the strictest secrecy about the day, and so was my clerk Thomas. Excepting us two, and the bride and bridegroom, and the bride’s father, Captain Treverton, nobody knew—”
“Treverton!” exclaimed Mr. Phippen, holding his teacup, with the grated ginger in the bottom of it, to be filled by Miss Sturch. “Treverton! (No more tea, dear Miss Sturch.) How very remarkable! I know the name. (Fill up with water, if you please.) Tell me, my dear doctor (many, many thanks; no sugar—it turns acid on the stomach), is this Miss Treverton whom you have been marrying (many thanks again; no milk, either) one of the Cornish Trevertons?”
“To be sure she is!” rejoined the vicar. “Her father, Captain Treverton, is the head of the family. Not that there’s much family to speak of now. The Captain, and Rosamond, and that whimsical old brute of an uncle of hers, Andrew Treverton, are the last left now of the old stock—a rich family, and a fine family, in former times—good friends to Church and State, you know, and all that—”
“Do you approve, Sir, of Amelia having a second helping of bread and marmalade?” asked Miss Sturch, appealing to Doctor Chennery, with the most perfect unconsciousness of interrupting him. Having no spare room in her mind for putting things away in until the appropriate time came for bringing them out, Miss Sturch always asked questions and made remarks the moment they occurred to her, without waiting for the beginning, middle, or end of any conversations that might be proceeding in her presence. She invariably looked the part of a listener to perfection, but she never acted it except in the case of talk that was aimed point-blank at her own ears.
“Oh, give her a second helping, by all means!” said the vicar, carelessly; “if she must overeat herself, she may as well do it on bread and marmalade as on anything else.”
“My dear, good soul,” exclaimed Mr. Phippen, “look what a wreck I am, and don’t talk in that shockingly thoughtless way of letting our sweet Amelia overeat herself.

他对在婚日里被当成稀奇物这一点，怀着那样一种神经质的恐惧，罗莎蒙德又是那样一个好心肠的姑娘，急切着要他最细微的任性都得到迁就，于是我们商定：婚礼放在清早一个游手好闲之辈不大可能在教堂左近闲荡的时辰。日期的事我被勒令严守秘密，我的执事托马斯也一样。除我们两个、新娘新郎、新娘的父亲特雷弗顿舰长，谁都不知道——」
「特雷弗顿！」菲平先生叫道，举着那杯底沉着姜屑的茶，请斯特奇小姐续水。「特雷弗顿！（茶不要再添了，亲爱的斯特奇小姐。）真是奇了！这名字我知道。（劳驾兑点水。）告诉我，亲爱的博士（多谢多谢；不要糖——到了胃里就返酸），您今早嫁的这位特雷弗顿小姐（再谢一声；牛奶也不要），是康沃尔的特雷弗顿家的人么？」
「当然是！」牧师接道。「她父亲特雷弗顿舰长，是一族的家长。只是如今也谈不上什么一族了。舰长、罗莎蒙德、还有她那位乖僻的老怪物叔叔安德鲁·特雷弗顿（Andrew Treverton），便是老根子剩下的最后几位——从前可是个富贵之族、体面之族——教会与国家的良友，你知道，诸如此类——」
「先生允许么，阿米莉亚再吃一份面包果酱？」斯特奇小姐问切纳里博士，浑然不觉自己打断了话头。她的头脑里没有多余的空屋，可把事情收存起来等该拿出的时机再拿；所以念头一冒，问题评语便即刻出囗，绝不等人前谈话的起、中、结。她看上去永远是一副十全十美的听客，但除非那话是直冲她自家耳朵说的，她从不演这角色。
「哦，再给一份，尽管给！」牧师漫不经心地说；「她既然非要吃撑，吃面包果酱同吃别的还不一样。」

===Original===

Load the stomach in youth, and what becomes of the digestion in age? The thing which vulgar people call the inside—I appeal to Miss Sturch’s interest in her charming pupil as an excuse for going into physiological particulars—is, in point of fact, an Apparatus. Digestively considered, Miss Sturch, even the fairest and youngest of us is an Apparatus. Oil our wheels, if you like; but clog them at your peril. Farinaceous puddings and mutton-chops; mutton-chops and farinaceous puddings—those should be the parents’ watchwords, if I had my way, from one end of England to the other. Look here, my sweet child—look at me. There is no fun, dear, about these little scales, but dreadful earnest. See! I put in the balance on one side dry bread (stale, dry bread, Amelia!), and on the other some ounce weights. ‘Mr. Phippen, eat by weight. Mr. Phippen! eat the same quantity, day by day, to a hair’s-breadth. Mr. Phippen! exceed your allowance (though it is only stale, dry bread) if you dare!’ Amelia, love, this is not fun—this is what the doctors tell me—the doctors, my child, who have been searching my Apparatus through and through for thirty years past with little pills, and have not found out where my wheels are clogged yet. Think of that, Amelia—think of Mr. Phippen’s clogged Apparatus—and say ‘No, thank you,’ next time. Miss Sturch, I beg a thousand pardons for intruding on your province; but my interest in that sweet child—Chennery, you dear, good soul, what were we talking about? Ah! the bride—the interesting bride! And so she is one of the Cornish Trevertons? I knew something of Andrew years ago. He was a bachelor, like myself, Miss Sturch. His Apparatus was out of order, like mine, dear Amelia. Not at all like his brother, the Captain, I should suppose? And so she is married? A charming girl, I have no doubt. A charming girl!”
“No better, truer, prettier girl in the world,” said the vicar.
“A very lively, energetic person,” remarked Miss Sturch.
“How I shall miss her!” cried Miss Louisa. “Nobody else amused me as Rosamond did, when I was laid up with that last bad cold of mine.”
“She used to give us such nice little early supper-parties,” said Miss Amelia.
“She was the only girl I ever saw who was fit to play with boys,” said Master Robert. “She could catch a ball, Mr. Phippen, Sir, with one hand, and go down a slide with both her legs together.”
“Bless me!”

年少时把胃塞满，年长时消化可怎么办？俗人叫做「肚子里」的那样东西——我援引斯特奇小姐对这位可爱学生的关切，恕我细谈生理——其实是一件器械（Apparatus）。从消化的角度看，斯特奇小姐，我们当中最白最嫩的，也是一件器械。愿意的话，给咱们的轮子上上油；可要叫轮子淤住了，后果自负。谷粉布丁配羊排；羊排配谷粉布丁——这该做父母的囗令，若依我，从英格兰这头喊到那头。听着，我的甜甜的孩子——看着我。这副小戥子里没有玩笑，亲爱的，有的是可怕的认真。看！我在天平一头放干面包（不新鲜的干面包，阿米莉亚！），另一头放几枚盎司砝码。『菲平先生，按重量吃。菲平先生！每天吃同等的量，一丝不差。菲平先生！超量试试（哪怕只是不新鲜的干面包）！』阿米莉亚，宝贝，这不是玩笑——这是大夫们告诉我的——大夫们，孩子，三十年来拿小药丸把我这器械探了又探，至今没探出我的轮子淤在哪里。想想这个，阿米莉亚——想想菲平先生淤住的器械——下回就说「不用了，谢谢」。斯特奇小姐，我千乞恕罪，僭越了您的职分；可我对那甜甜的孩子的关切——切纳里，我亲爱的大好人，咱们方才说什么来着？啊！新娘——那位有趣的新娘！这么说，她是康沃尔特雷弗顿家的人？安德鲁我多年前有几分熟。他是独身，同我一样，斯特奇小姐。他的器械也失了调，同我一样，亲爱的阿米莉亚。完全不像他哥哥舰长，我料想？这么说她嫁了？一位可人的姑娘，我毫不怀疑。一位可人的姑娘！」
「天下再没有更好、更真、更美的姑娘了，」牧师说。
「一个很活泼、很有生气的人，」斯特奇小姐评道。
「我会多想她呀！」路易莎小姐嚷。「上回重感冒躺倒的时候，谁也哄我不乐，只有罗莎蒙德。」
「她常请我们吃多好的小小早夜宵呢，」阿米莉亚小姐说。
「她是我见过的唯一一个配同男孩子玩的姑娘，」罗伯特小少爷说。「她能单手接球，菲平先生，阁下；还能两条腿并拢滑冰道。」
「天哪！」

===Original===

said Mr. Phippen. “What an extraordinary wife for a blind man! You said he was blind from his birth, my dear doctor, did you not? Let me see, what was his name? You will not bear too hardly on my loss of memory, Miss Sturch? When indigestion has done with the body, it begins to prey on the mind. Mr. Frank Something, was it not?”
“No, no—Frankland,” answered the vicar, “Leonard Frankland. And not blind from his birth by any means. It is not much more than a year ago since he could see almost as well as any of us.”
“An accident, I suppose!” said Mr. Phippen. “You will excuse me if I take the armchair?—a partially reclining posture is of great assistance to me after meals. So an accident happened to his eyes? Ah, what a delightfully easy chair to sit in!”
“Scarcely an accident,” said Doctor Chennery. “Leonard Frankland was a difficult child to bring up: great constitutional weakness, you know, at first. He seemed to get over that with time, and grew into a quiet, sedate, orderly sort of boy—as unlike my son there as possible—very amiable, and what you call easy to deal with. Well, he had a turn for mechanics (I am telling you all this to make you understand about his blindness), and, after veering from one occupation of that sort to another, he took at last to watch-making. Curious amusement for a boy; but anything that required delicacy of touch, and plenty of patience and perseverance, was just the thing to amuse and occupy Leonard. I always said to his father and mother, ‘Get him off that stool, break his magnifying-glasses, send him to me, and I’ll give him a back at leapfrog, and teach him the use of a bat.’ But it was no use. His parents knew best, I suppose, and they said he must be humored. Well, things went on smoothly enough for some time, till he got another long illness—as I believe, from not taking exercise enough. As soon as he began to get round, back he went to his old watch-making occupations again. But the bad end of it all was coming. About the last work he did, poor fellow, was the repairing of my watch—here it is; goes as regular as a steam-engine. I hadn’t got it back into my fob very long before I heard that he was getting a bad pain at the back of his head, and that he saw all sorts of moving spots before his eyes. ‘String him up with lots of port wine, and give him three hours a day on the back of a quiet pony’—that was my advice.

菲平先生说。「给一个盲人做太太，多么离奇的一位！您方才说，他是生来就盲，亲爱的博士，是不是？让我想想，他叫什么来着？您不会太怪我这记性的荒疏罢，斯特奇小姐？消化不良对付完了身子，就开始啮心智。弗兰什么先生，是不是？」
「不对，不对——弗兰克兰，」牧师答，「伦纳德·弗兰克兰（Leonard Frankland）。也绝非生来就盲。不到一年多以前，他还看得同我们哪个都差不多一样好。」
「一场意外，我料想！」菲平先生说。「恕我坐这张扶手椅罢？——饭后半躺的姿势，于我大有裨益。这么说，意外出在了他眼睛上？啊，这椅子坐起来多称心！」
「算不得什么意外，」切纳里博士说。「伦纳德·弗兰克兰是个难拉扯的孩子：起初体质孱弱得厉害，你知道。后来似乎熬过来了，长成一个安静、稳重、规矩的少年——同我那边那个儿子尽可能不像——非常和善，属于你们说的好对付。嗯，他偏爱机械一途（我讲这些，为的是叫你明白他的失明），从一门这样的营生漂到另一门，末了定在了制钟表上。男孩子家的一份古怪消遣；可凡要指头灵巧、又要耐性和恒久的活计，恰是哄伦纳德、占住伦纳德的妙品。我总对他父母说：『把他从凳子上拽下来，摔了他的放大镜，把他送到我这儿来，我给他当跳山羊的马，教他使板球拍。』可没有用。他父母最清楚，想必，说孩子得顺着他。嗯，事情顺顺当当地过了一阵，直到他又生了一场长病——照我看，是锻炼太少闹的。他一见好，又回他那套制表的营生去了。可坏结局在来了。他做的最后一件活计，可怜的孩子，恰好是修我这块表——瞧，在这儿；走得同蒸汽机一样规整。表回到我表袋里没多久，我就听说他后脑勺子发痛，眼前面晃着各样会动的黑斑。『拿上好的波尔特酒给他提气，再叫他一天三个钟头骑匹安静的小马』——这是我出的方子。

Instead of taking it, they sent for doctors from London, and blistered him behind the ears and between the shoulders, and drenched the lad with mercury, and moped him up in a dark room. No use. The sight got worse and worse, flickered and flickered, and went out at last like the flame of a candle. His mother died—luckily for her, poor soul—before that happened. His father was half out of his mind: took him to oculists in London and oculists in Paris. All they did was to call the blindness by a long Latin name, and to say that it was hopeless and useless to try an operation. Some of them said it was the result of the long weaknesses from which he had twice suffered after illness. Some said it was an apoplectic effusion in his brain. All of them shook their heads when they heard of the watch-making. So they brought him back home, blind; blind he is now; and blind he will remain, poor dear fellow, for the rest of his life.”
“You shock me; my dear Chennery, you shock me dreadfully,” said Mr. Phippen. “Especially when you state that theory about long weakness after illness. Good Heavens! Why, *I* have had long weaknesses—I have got them now. Spots did he see before his eyes? I see spots, black spots, dancing black spots, dancing black bilious spots. Upon my word of honor, Chennery, this comes home to me—my sympathies are painfully acute—I feel this blind story in every nerve of my body; I do, indeed!”
“You would hardly know that Leonard was blind, to look at him,” said Miss Louisa, striking into the conversation with a view to restoring Mr. Phippen’s equanimity. “Except that his eyes look quieter than other people’s, there seems no difference in them now. Who was that famous character you told us about, Miss Sturch, who was blind, and didn’t show it any more than Leonard Frankland?”
“Milton, my love. I begged you to remember that he was the most famous of British epic poets,” answered Miss Sturch with suavity. “He poetically describes his blindness as being caused by ‘so thick a drop serene.’ You shall read about it, Louisa. After we have had a little French, we will have a little Milton, this morning. Hush, love, your papa is speaking.”
“Poor young Frankland!” said the vicar, warmly. “That good, tender, noble creature I married him to this morning seems sent as a consolation to him in his affliction.

他们没听，反倒从伦敦请来大夫，在他耳后和两肩之间发泡，给这孩子灌水银，把他闷在一间黑屋里。没用。视力越来越坏，闪了又闪，末了像烛火一样灭了。他母亲赶在那之前死了——于她倒是万幸，可怜的人。他父亲急得半疯：带他去伦敦看眼科名医，又去巴黎看。他们所能做的，只是给这失明起一个长长的拉丁名字，再说动手术既无望也无用。有几位说，那是他病后两度缠绵的长期虚弱所致；有几位说，是脑卒中渗血。一听说制表的事，个个摇头。于是他们把他带回了家，盲的；如今他还是盲的；往后一生，他也将盲下去，可怜的好孩子。」
「你吓坏我了；亲爱的切纳里，你真吓坏我了，」菲平先生说。「尤其是你说到病后长期虚弱那个说法的时候。天哪！怎么，*我*就有过长期虚弱——如今还带着。他眼前见黑斑？我也见黑斑，黑斑，乱舞的黑斑，乱舞的黑胆汁斑。凭我的名誉起誓，切纳里，这话正说在我身上——我的同情锐敏得发痛——这个盲人的故事，我全身上下每一根神经都感觉得到；真的，真的！」
「看他的样子，你几乎想不到伦纳德是盲的，」路易莎小姐插进话来，想让菲平先生恢复平静。「只是眼睛显得比旁人的静些，如今看着并无分别。斯特奇小姐，您从前同我们讲的那位著名人物是谁来着——也是盲的，也同伦纳德·弗兰克兰一样叫人看不出来？」
「弥尔顿，宝贝。我请你记过，他是英伦史诗诗人里最著名的一位，」斯特奇小姐温文地答。「他用诗句描写他的失明，说是因为「一层如此浓重的翳」（so thick a drop serene）。你回头去读罢，路易莎。今早咱们念过一小段法文，再念一小段弥尔顿。嘘，宝贝，你爸爸说话呢。」
「可怜的弗兰克兰！」牧师热切地说。「今早我给他娶的那位善良、温柔、高贵的造物，仿佛是上天送来慰他于不幸的。

===Original===

If any human being can make him happy for the rest of his life, Rosamond Treverton is the girl to do it.”
“She has made a sacrifice,” said Mr. Phippen; “but I like her for that, having made a sacrifice myself in remaining single. It seems indispensable, indeed, on the score of humanity, that I should do so. How could I conscientiously inflict such a digestion as mine on a member of the fairer portion of creation? No; I am a sacrifice in my own proper person, and I have a fellow-feeling for others who are like me. Did she cry much, Chennery, when you were marrying her?”
“Cry!” exclaimed the vicar, contemptuously. “Rosamond Treverton is not one of the puling, sentimental sort, I can tell you. A fine, buxom, warmhearted, quick-tempered girl, who looks what she means when she tells a man she is going to marry him. And, mind you, she has been tried. If she hadn’t loved him with all her heart and soul, she might have been free months ago to marry anybody she pleased. They were engaged long before this cruel affliction befell young Frankland—the fathers, on both sides, having lived as near neighbors in these parts for years. Well, when the blindness came, Leonard at once offered to release Rosamond from her engagement. You should have read the letter she wrote to him, Phippen, upon that. I don’t mind confessing that I blubbered like a baby over it when they showed it to me. I should have married them at once the instant I read it, but old Frankland was a fidgety, punctilious kind of man, and he insisted on a six months’ probation, so that she might be certain of knowing her own mind. He died before the term was out, and that caused the marriage to be put off again. But no delays could alter Rosamond—six years, instead of six months, would not have changed her. There she was this morning as fond of that poor, patient blind fellow as she was the first day they were engaged. ‘You shall never know a sad moment, Lenny, if I can help it, as long as you live’—these were the first words she said to him when we all came out of church. ‘I hear you, Rosamond,’ said I. ‘And you shall judge me, too, Doctor,’ says she, quick as lightning. ‘We will come back to Long Beckley, and you shall ask Lenny if I have not kept my word.’ With that she gave me a kiss that you might have heard down here at the vicarage, bless her heart!

===Chinese===

「若说世上还有谁能叫他后半生幸福，罗莎蒙德·特雷弗顿正是那一个。」
「她是做了一件牺牲，」菲平先生说；「可我正因此喜欢她——我自己独身至今，也是做了一件牺牲。论人道，我似乎确乎非如此不可。我怎能凭良心，把我这样一副消化，加给造化中较柔美的那一半中的一位？不；我本人便是一件牺牲，对同我一样的人，我怀有同病之怜。她哭得厉害么，切纳里，你替她们行礼的时候？」
「哭！」牧师不屑地嚷。「罗莎蒙德·特雷弗顿可不是那等哭哭啼啼、伤春悲秋的料，我可以告诉你。一个健美丰润、热心热肠、急性快嘴的姑娘，她对一个男人说要嫁他时，脸上的神情就是她的心意。而且你听着，她是受过考验的。若不是倾心倾魂地爱他，几个月前她尽可以自由自在，爱嫁谁嫁谁。他们订婚早在弗兰克兰遭这场残酷的不幸之前许久——两家的父亲，做近邻在这一带住了多年。嗯，失明来了，伦纳德立刻提出解除罗莎蒙德的婚约。她为此给他写的那封信，菲平，你真该读读。我不怕承认，他们拿给我看时，我哭得像个娃娃。读完那封信我便想立刻给他们成婚，可老弗兰克兰是个毛毛躁躁、拘泥小节的人物，坚持要六个月的试用期，好叫她确知自家心意。期限未满他死了，婚事便又耽搁下来。可什么耽搁也改不了罗莎蒙德——六年，而不是六个月，也不会叫她变。今天早晨她在那里，同订婚头一天一样，疼那个可怜的、坚忍的盲孩子。『伦尼，只要我办得到，你一辈子也别想认得一个愁字』——我们大家走出教堂时，她对他说的是这句。『我听见了，罗莎蒙德，』我说。『那您也来评判我，博士，』她闪电似的答。『我们会回朗贝克利来，您去问伦尼，看我守没守约。』说着她给了我一个吻，那吻响得，牧师宅这边都听得见，上帝保佑她！」

===Original===

We’ll drink her health after dinner, Miss Sturch—we’ll drink both their healths, Phippen, in a bottle of the best wine I have in my cellar.”
“In a glass of toast-and-water, so far as I am concerned, if you will allow me,” said Mr. Phippen, mournfully. “But, my dear Chennery, when you were talking of the fathers of these two interesting young people, you spoke of their living as near neighbors here, at Long Beckley. My memory is impaired, as I am painfully aware; but I thought Captain Treverton was the eldest of the two brothers, and that he always lived, when he was on shore, at the family place in Cornwall?”
“So he did,” returned the vicar, “in his wife’s lifetime. But since her death, which happened as long ago as the year ’twenty-nine—let me see, we are now in the year ’forty-four—and that makes—”
The vicar stopped for an instant to calculate, and looked at Miss Sturch.
“Fifteen years ago, Sir,” said Miss Sturch, offering the accommodation of a little simple subtraction to the vicar, with her blandest smile.
“Of course,” continued Doctor Chennery. “Well, since Mrs. Treverton died, fifteen years ago, Captain Treverton has never been near Porthgenna Tower. And, what is more, Phippen, at the first opportunity he could get, he sold the place—sold it, out and out, mine, fisheries, and all—for forty thousand pounds.”
“You don’t say so!” exclaimed Mr. Phippen. “Did he find the air unhealthy? I should think the local produce, in the way of food, must be coarse now, in those barbarous regions? Who bought the place?”
“Leonard Frankland’s father,” said the vicar. “It is rather a long story, that sale of Porthgenna Tower, with some curious circumstances involved in it. Suppose we take a turn in the garden, Phippen? I’ll tell you all about it over my morning cigar. Miss Sturch, if you want me, I shall be on the lawn somewhere. Girls! mind you know your lessons. Bob! remember that I’ve got a cane in the hall, and a birch-rod in my dressing-room. Come, Phippen, rouse up out of that armchair. You won’t say no to a turn in the garden?”
“My dear fellow, I will say yes—if you will kindly lend me an umbrella, and allow me to carry my campstool in my hand,” said Mr. Phippen. “I am too weak to encounter the sun, and I can’t go far without sitting down.

===Chinese===

「晚饭后我们要祝她健康，斯特奇小姐——祝他们两个健康，菲平，拿我地窖里最好的一瓶酒。」
「至于我，承您允许的话，还是一杯烤面包水罢，」菲平先生凄然说。「不过，亲爱的切纳里，方才你讲这两位有趣年轻人的父亲时，说他们在此地朗贝克利做近邻。我的记性坏损了，这我痛切知道；可我记得特雷弗顿舰长是两兄弟中的长兄，上岸时一向住在康沃尔的老家宅？」
「正是，」牧师答，「在他太太在世时。可自她去世——那是远在二九年——我算算，如今是四四年——那就是——」
牧师顿了一顿算数，望着斯特奇小姐。
「十五年前，先生，」斯特奇小姐说，以她最温和的微笑，向牧师提供了一点小小减法的方便。
「正是，」切纳里博士续道。「那么，自特雷弗顿太太十五年前去世后，特雷弗顿舰长就再没走近过波斯根纳塔楼一步。而且，菲平，一得着机会，他就把那份产业卖了——整个儿卖断，矿、渔场，一概在内——卖了四万镑。」
「不会吧！」菲平先生叫道。「他是嫌那边空气不卫生？我想那蛮荒地方，本地吃食如今必定粗粝？产业是谁买的？」
「伦纳德·弗兰克兰的父亲，」牧师说。「卖波斯根纳塔楼这桩事说来话长，里头还夹着些古怪情形。咱们到花园里遛遛如何，菲平？抽着我的晨雪茄，我一五一十讲给你。斯特奇小姐，要找我的话，我在草坪哪里。姑娘们！功课用心。博布！记住，门厅里我有手杖，更衣室里我有桦条。来，菲平，从那扶手椅里振作起来。花园遛一圈，你不至于回绝罢？」
「亲爱的伙计，我答应——只求你行行好借我一把伞，再许我手里拎上我的折叠凳，」菲平先生说。「我虚弱得禁不起太阳，不坐下歇脚也走不了多远。

===Original===

—The moment I feel fatigued, Miss Sturch, I open my campstool, and sit down anywhere, without the slightest regard for appearances.—I am ready, Chennery, whenever you are—equally ready, my good friend, for the garden and the story about the sale of Porthgenna Tower. You said it was a curious story, did you not?”
“I said there was some curious circumstances connected with it,” replied the vicar. “And when you hear about them, I think you will say so too. Come along! you will find your campstool, and a choice of all the umbrellas in the house, in the hall.”
With those words, Doctor Chennery opened his cigar-case, and led the way out of the breakfast-parlor.
