我改不了它；我比先前更确信，那可怜人一定神智有失——可一想起她那样好心好意地来帮我，那样急着要派上用场，我又忍不住为自己的疑心害臊；忍不住自责：昨晚是我惹得她被辞退。奥里奇先生，您可曾在贾泽夫太太的脸或举止里，注意到什么叫您疑心她的神智有失健全？」
「绝没有，弗兰克兰太太；有我就不会带她来了。听说她突然病倒、或羊癫疯发作、或一件吓不着旁人的小事偏偏吓着了她——这些我都不会惊讶；可说她心智有什么近似错乱之处——这，我承认，当真叫我想不通。」
「难道是我弄错了！」罗莎蒙德叫道，从奥里奇先生望到丈夫，又慌又疑。「伦尼！伦尼！若是我弄错了，我永不饶我自己。」
「何不告诉我们，亲爱的，你是凭什么疑心她疯的？」弗兰克兰先生提说。
罗莎蒙德迟疑了。「一个人心里天大的事，」她说，「一变成话，就缩得那样小。我几乎不指望你们能明白：我那受惊受得多有道理——而且我怕，想对自家公平，倒要对产婆不公了。」
「用你自家的路子讲你自家的故事，爱人，保管错不了，」弗兰克兰先生说。
「还请记住，」奥里奇先生添说，「我对贾泽夫太太的看法，并无真正的分量。我还来不及形成它。您观察她的机会，比我多得多。」
得了这样的鼓励，罗莎蒙德便平平实实地把昨夜她房里发生的一切讲了一遍——讲到她阖眼、听见产婆向床边逼近为止。复述贾泽夫太太附耳吐出的那句离奇话之前，她停了停，恳切地望住丈夫的脸。
「为什么停住？」弗兰克兰先生问。
「一想那几句话，我就还发慌，伦尼——就在摇铃之前，她对我说的那几句。

===Original===

“What did she say? Was it something you would rather not repeat?”
“No! no! I am most anxious to repeat it, and to hear what you think it means. As I have just told you, Lenny, we had been talking of Porthgenna, and of my project of exploring the north rooms as soon as I got there; and she had been asking many questions about the old house; appearing, I must say, to be unaccountably interested in it, considering she was a stranger.”
“Yes?”
“Well, when she came to the bedside, she knelt down close at my ear, and whispered all on a sudden—‘When you go to Porthgenna, keep out of the Myrtle Room!’ ”
Mr. Frankland started. “Is there such a room at Porthgenna?” he asked, eagerly.
“I never heard of it,” said Rosamond.
“Are you sure of that?” inquired Mr. Orridge. Up to this moment the doctor had privately suspected that Mrs. Frankland must have fallen asleep soon after he left her the evening before; and that the narrative which she was now relating, with the sincerest conviction of its reality, was actually derived from nothing but a series of vivid impressions produced by a dream.
“I am certain I never heard of such a room,” said Rosamond. “I left Porthgenna at five years old; and I had never heard of it then. My father often talked of the house in after-years; but I am certain that he never spoke of any of the rooms by any particular names; and I can say the same of your father, Lenny, whenever I was in his company after he had bought the place. Besides, don’t you remember, when the builder we sent down to survey the house wrote you that letter, he complained that there were no names of the rooms on the different keys to guide him in opening the doors, and that he could get no information from anybody at Porthgenna on the subject? How could I ever have heard of the Myrtle Room? Who was there to tell me?”
Mr. Orridge began to look perplexed; it seemed by no means so certain that Mrs. Frankland had been dreaming, after all.
“I have thought of nothing else,” said Rosamond to her husband, in low, whispering tones. “I can’t get those mysterious words off my mind. Feel my heart, Lenny—it is beating quicker than usual only with saying them over to you. They are such very strange, startling words. What do you think they mean?”
“Who is the woman who spoke them?—that is the most important question,” said Mr. Frankland.
“But why did she say the words to *me*?

===Chinese===

」
「她说了什么？是不便复述的话么？」
「不！不！我急着想复述，急着想听听你们断它什么意思。方才我同你说了，伦尼，咱们一直在谈波斯根纳，谈我一到就要搜遍北屋的盘算；她呢，也一直在问那老宅的许多事——我须得承认，一个生人，问得那样没来由地关心。」
「后来呢？」
「后来，她走到床边，跪下来贴着我的耳朵，蓦地低声说——『去了波斯根纳，千万莫进桃金娘房！』」
弗兰克兰先生一惊。「波斯根纳有那样一间屋么？」他急问。
「从没听说过，」罗莎蒙德说。
「您拿得准？」奥里奇先生问。直到此刻，医生私下一直疑心：昨晚他离开后，弗兰克兰太太多半很快睡着了；她此刻以最诚实的信心讲述的这段经历，多半只是梦的一系列鲜明印象。
「拿得准从没听说过，」罗莎蒙德说。「我五岁上离开波斯根纳；那时就没听说过。后来我父亲常谈起那宅子；可他从不曾叫哪间屋子什么特别的名字，这个我拿得准；你父亲那边也一样，伦尼，他买下那地方之后，凡我同他在一起的时候。再说，你可记得，咱们打发去勘宅的营造商写给你的那封信？他抱怨钥匙上没有房名指引他开门，说在波斯根纳向谁都问不出个所以然。我怎么可能听说过桃金娘房？谁告诉我来着？」
奥里奇先生开始露出迷惘之色；看来弗兰克兰太太是做梦一说，到底不那么靠得住了。
「旁的我什么也不想，」罗莎蒙德对丈夫说，声调又低又促。「心里撂不下那几句神秘的话。摸摸我的心，伦尼——只在嘴里向你复述一遍，它就跳得比平时快。那几句话太怪、太震人了。你说它们是什么意思？」
「说这话的女人是谁——那才是顶要紧的一问，」弗兰克兰先生说。
「可她为什么单对我说？」

===Original===

That is what I want to know—that is what I must know, if I am ever to feel easy in my mind again!”
“Gently, Mrs. Frankland, gently!” said Mr. Orridge. “For your child’s sake, as well as for your own, pray try to be calm, and to look at this very mysterious event as composedly as you can. If any exertions of mine can throw light upon this strange woman and her still stranger conduct, I will not spare them. I am going today to her mistress’s house to see one of the children; and, depend upon it, I will manage in some way to make Mrs. Jazeph explain herself. Her mistress shall hear every word that you have told me; and I can assure you she is just the sort of downright, resolute woman who will insist on having the whole mystery instantly cleared up.”
Rosamond’s weary eyes brightened at the doctor’s proposal. “Oh, go at once, Mr. Orridge!” she exclaimed—“go at once!”
“I have a great deal of medical work to do in the town first,” said the doctor, smiling at Mrs. Frankland’s impatience.
“Begin it, then, without losing another instant,” said Rosamond. “The baby is quite well, and I am quite well—we need not detain you a moment. And, Mr. Orridge, pray be as gentle and considerate as possible with the poor woman; and tell her that I never should have thought of sending her away if I had not been too frightened to know what I was about. And say how sorry I am this morning, and say—”
“My dear, if Mrs. Jazeph is really not in her right senses, what would be the use of overwhelming her with all these excuses?” interposed Mr. Frankland. “It will be more to the purpose if Mr. Orridge will kindly explain and apologize for us to her mistress.”
“Go! Don’t stop to talk—pray go at once!” cried Rosamond, as the doctor attempted to reply to Mr. Frankland.
“Don’t be afraid; no time shall be lost,” said Mr. Orridge, opening the door. “But remember, Mrs. Frankland, I shall expect you to reward your embassador, when he returns from his mission, by showing him that you are a little more quiet and composed than I find you this morning.” With that parting hint, the doctor took his leave.
“ ‘When you go to Porthgenna, keep out of the Myrtle Room,’ ” repeated Mr. Frankland, thoughtfully. “Those are very strange words, Rosamond. Who can this woman really be?

===Chinese===

这正是我想知道的——也正是我必须知道的，不然我的心永不得安宁！」
「缓一缓，弗兰克兰太太，缓一缓！」奥里奇先生说。「为您孩子，也为您自己，求您定一定，尽量平静地看这桩极神秘的事。若我出把力能照亮这个古怪的女人同她更古怪的行径，我绝不惜力。今天我要去她主母宅上看一个孩子；您放心，我总有法子叫贾泽夫太太把话说明白。您告诉我的一字一句，都会传到她主母耳中；我可以担保，她正是那种直肚直肠、说一不二的女人，非把这桩疑案当场问个水落石出不可。」
医生这一议，罗莎蒙德倦极的眼睛亮了。「哦，这就去，奥里奇先生！」她叫道——「这就去！」
「城里还有一大摊医务要先办呢，」医生说，对弗兰克兰太太的性急一笑。
「那就一瞬别耽搁，即刻办去，」罗莎蒙德说。「孩子全好，我也全好——半刻不必留您。还有，奥里奇先生，对那可怜的女人，千万尽量温柔、尽量体谅；告诉她，若不是我吓昏了头，我断想不到打发她走；再替我说今早的抱歉，再说——」
「亲爱的，贾泽夫太太若真是神智不清，这一堆告罪堆到她头上何用？」弗兰克兰先生插道。「不如请奥里奇先生代咱们向她主母剖白致歉，更在点上。」
「去！别站着说话了——求你即刻去！」罗莎蒙德叫道，医生正要答复弗兰克兰先生。
「放心；一点时间也不误，」奥里奇先生说着开门。「只是记住，弗兰克兰太太，等您的使臣办完差回来，我可指望您给他一点犒赏——让我见到您比今早所见的更安稳些。」丢下这句临别赠言，医生告辞而去。
「『去了波斯根纳，千万莫进桃金娘房』，」弗兰克兰先生若有所思地重说。「这几句话太怪了，罗莎蒙德。这女人究竟是谁？

===Original===

She is a perfect stranger to both of us; we are brought into contact with her by the merest accident; and we find that she knows something about our own house of which we were both perfectly ignorant until she chose to speak!”
“But the warning, Lenny—the warning, so pointedly and mysteriously addressed to me? Oh, if I could only go to sleep at once, and not wake again till the doctor comes back!”
“My love, try not to count too certainly on our being enlightened, even then. The woman may refuse to explain herself to anybody.”
“Don’t even hint at such a disappointment as that, Lenny—or I shall be wanting to get up, and go and question her myself!”
“Even if you could get up and question her, Rosamond, you might find it impossible to make her answer. She may be afraid of certain consequences which we can not foresee; and, in that case, I can only repeat that it is more than probable she will explain nothing—or, perhaps, still more likely that she will coolly deny her own words altogether.”
“Then, Lenny, we will put them to the proof for ourselves.”
“And how can we do that?”
“By continuing our journey to Porthgenna the moment I am allowed to travel, and by leaving no stone unturned when we get there until we have discovered whether there is or is not any room in the old house that ever was known, at any time of its existence, by the name of the Myrtle Room.”
“And suppose it should turn out that there is such a room?” asked Mr. Frankland, beginning to feel the influence of his wife’s enthusiasm.
“If it does turn out so,” said Rosamond, her voice rising, and her face lighting up with its accustomed vivacity, “how can you doubt what will happen next? Am I not a woman? And have I not been forbidden to enter the Myrtle Room? Lenny! Lenny! Do you know so little of my half of humanity as to doubt what I should do the moment the room was discovered? My darling, as a matter of course, I should walk into it immediately.”

她于你我是个十足的生人；我们同她相识，纯出最偶然的巧遇；可她竟知道我们自家宅子里的一件事——她不开口，你我到今天还一概不知！」
「可那警告，伦尼——那样指着名儿、神秘地冲我来的警告？哦，若能立刻睡着、一觉睡到大夫回来才醒，多好！」
「爱人，别把指望下得太死，就算到那时，也未必就能水落石出。那女人兴许对谁也不肯剖白。」
「连这样的失望都不许提，伦尼——不然我就要爬起来，亲自去盘她！」
「就算你爬得起来去盘她，罗莎蒙德，你也未必问得出她的答话。她也许畏惧某种咱们逆料不到的后果；那样的话，我只能说：她多半什么也不解释——或者，更可能的是，索性冷静地把自家的话一并抵赖掉。」
「那，伦尼，咱们就自家把这话验证到底。」
「怎么个验证法？」
「等我一准旅行，就即刻续程去波斯根纳；到了那里，不翻个底朝天不罢休，直到查个明白：老宅里到底有没有哪间屋子，在它存在过的任何时候，被叫做过桃金娘房。」
「倘若当真有这样一间房呢？」弗兰克兰先生问，开始受妻子热劲的感染。
「若当真有，」罗莎蒙德说，嗓门扬起来，脸上恢复了惯常的鲜活，「往后会怎样，你还疑心么？我不是个女人么？人家不是明令禁止我进桃金娘房么？伦尼！伦尼！你对我这半边人类知道得这样少，少到会疑心那间屋子一被发现，我头一件事做什么？亲爱的，理所当然，我抬腿就进去。」」

## VI Another Surprise / 六 又一桩意外
===Original===

With all the haste he could make, it was one o’clock in the afternoon before Mr. Orridge’s professional avocations allowed him to set forth in his gig for Mrs. Norbury’s house. He drove there with such goodwill that he accomplished the half-hour’s journey in twenty minutes. The footman having heard the rapid approach of the gig, opened the hall door the instant the horse was pulled up before it, and confronted the doctor with a smile of malicious satisfaction.
“Well,” said Mr. Orridge, bustling into the hall, “you were all rather surprised last night when the housekeeper came back, I suppose?”
“Yes, Sir, we certainly were surprised when she came back last night,” answered the footman; “but we were still more surprised when she went away again this morning.”
“Went away! You don’t mean to say she is gone?”
“Yes, I do, Sir—she has lost her place, and gone for good.” The footman smiled again, as he made that reply; and the housemaid, who happened to be on her way downstairs while he was speaking, and to hear what he said, smiled too. Mrs. Jazeph had evidently been no favorite in the servants’ hall.
Amazement prevented Mr. Orridge from uttering another word. Hearing no more questions asked, the footman threw open the door of the breakfast-parlor, and the doctor followed him into the room. Mrs. Norbury was sitting near the window in a rigidly upright attitude, inflexibly watching the proceedings of her invalid child over a basin of beef-tea.
“I know what you are going to talk about before you open your lips,” said the outspoken lady. “But just look to the child first, and say what you have to say on that subject, if you please, before you enter on any other.”
The child was examined, was pronounced to be improving rapidly, and was carried away by the nurse to lie down and rest a little. As soon as the door of the room had closed, Mrs. Norbury abruptly addressed the doctor, interrupting him, for the second time, just as he was about to speak.
“Now, Mr. Orridge,” she said, “I want to tell you something at the outset. I am a remarkably just woman, and I have no quarrel with *you*. You are the cause of my having been treated with the most audacious insolence by three people—but you are the innocent cause, and, therefore, I don’t blame you.”
“I am really at a loss,” Mr. Orridge began—“quite at a loss, I assure you—”
“To know what I mean?” said Mrs. Norbury.

===Chinese===

奥里奇先生的医务任他怎样赶，到下午一点才腾出工夫，驾他的双轮马车上诺伯里太太宅去。他赶得那殷勤，半个钟头的路，二十分钟便到了。男仆听得马车疾驰而至，马刚勒在门前，便拉开正门，迎着医生露出一脸含讥带快的笑。
「怎么样，」奥里奇先生奔进厅堂说，「昨晚管家婆回来，你们都相当意外罢？」
「是，先生，昨晚她回来，我们确实意外，」男仆答；「今早她又走了，我们更意外。」
「走了！别是说她走了？」
「正是，先生——她丢了差事，一去不回了。」男仆答话时又笑了；恰好这时女仆下楼，听得他的话，也笑了。下人们堆房里，贾泽夫太太分明从来不得人心。
惊讶塞住了奥里奇先生的嘴。听得不再发问，男仆推开早餐间的门，医生随他进屋。诺伯里太太坐在窗边，腰身挺得笔直，寸步不让地盯着她那病孩对着盆牛肉茶的动作。
「你嘴还没张，我就知道你要谈什么了，」这位心囗如一的女太太说。「只是先看孩子，那一摊有什么话，请先说完，再开别的。」
孩子验过，断为迅速见好，被保姆抱去躺下歇息。房门一关，诺伯里太太便冲着医生开了腔——这已是第二回抢在他开口之前。
「如今，奥里奇先生，」她说，「我要先告诉你一件事。我是个出奇公道的女人，同*你*没有争执。我受三个人的无礼顶撞，根子在你——可根子是无辜的，所以我不怪你。」
「我实在摸不着头脑，」奥里奇先生开口——「实在摸不着，我担保——」
「摸不着我说什么？」诺伯里太太说。

===Original===

“I will soon tell you. Were you not the original cause of my sending my housekeeper to nurse Mrs. Frankland?”
“Yes.” Mr. Orridge could not hesitate to acknowledge that.
“Well,” pursued Mrs. Norbury, “and the consequence of my sending her is, as I said before, that I am treated with unparalleled insolence by no less than three people. Mrs. Frankland takes an insolent whim into her head, and affects to be frightened by my housekeeper. Mr. Frankland shows an insolent readiness to humor that whim, and hands me back my housekeeper as if she was a bad shilling; and last, and worst of all, my housekeeper herself insults me to my face as soon as she comes back—insults me, Mr. Orridge, to that degree that I give her twelve hours’ notice to leave the place. Don’t begin to defend yourself! I know all about it; I know you had nothing to do with sending her back; I never said you had. All the mischief you have done is innocent mischief. I don’t blame you, remember that—whatever you do, Mr. Orridge, remember that!”
“I had no idea of defending myself,” said the doctor, “for I have no reason to do so. But you surprise me beyond all power of expression when you tell me that Mrs. Jazeph treated you with incivility.”
“Incivility!” exclaimed Mrs. Norbury. “Don’t talk about incivility—it’s not the word. Impudence is the word—brazen impudence. The only charitable thing to say of Mrs. Jazeph is that she is not right in her head. I never noticed anything odd about her myself; but the servants used to laugh at her for being as timid in the dark as a child, and for often running away to her candle in her own room when they declined to light the lamps before the night had fairly set in. I never troubled my head about this before; but I thought of it last night, I can tell you, when I found her looking me fiercely in the face, and contradicting me flatly the moment I spoke to her.”
“I should have thought she was the very last woman in the world to misbehave herself in that way,” answered the doctor.
“Very well. Now hear what happened when she came back last night,” said Mrs. Norbury. “She got here just as we were going upstairs to bed. Of course, I was astonished; and, of course, I called her into the drawing-room for an explanation. There was nothing very unnatural in that course of proceeding, I suppose?

===Chinese===

「我这就告诉你。当初打发我家管家婆去照看弗兰克兰太太，起头的不正是你？」
「是。」这个奥里奇先生无可迟疑。
「嗯，」诺伯里太太续道，「我打发她的后果，方才说了：我受了三个人的无礼，空前未有。弗兰克兰太太起了个无礼的怪念头，假装被我家管家婆吓着。弗兰克兰先生呢，无礼地巴不得迁就那怪念头，把管家婆退还给我，像退一枚坏先令。最末也最糟的，是我那管家婆本人，一进门就当面侮辱我——侮辱到那个份上，奥里奇先生，我下了十二个钟头的通知叫她走。先别忙着替你自家分辩！我全知道；我知道送她回去与你无干；我也没说过有干。你闯的祸是清白的祸。我不怪你——记住这个——无论怎的，奥里奇先生，记住这个！」
「我没想分辩，」医生说，「因为我没有分辩的理由。只是您告诉我贾泽夫太太对您失敬，我惊讶得无以言表。」
「失敬！」诺伯里太太叫道。「别提失敬——不是那个词。是放肆——厚脸皮的放肆。对贾泽夫太太唯一慈善的说法，是她脑不清。她那点古怪，我自家从没留意过；可下人们惯于笑话她：怕黑，怕得同孩子一样；天还没黑透，他们不肯点灯，她就常常逃回自屋，守着她的蜡烛。这我先前从不放在心上；可昨晚我想起来了，千真万确想起来了——那时我看她正狠狠盯住我的脸，我一开口，便直挺挺地顶回我。」
「我还当她正是天下最后一个会这样失仪的女人，」医生应道。
「很好。那就听听她昨晚回来后出了什么事，」诺伯里太太说。「她到家时，我们正要上楼安置。我当然惊讶；当然把她叫进客厅问情由。这一着，总不见得有什么大悖常理罢？」

===Original===

Well, I noticed that her eyes were swollen and red, and that her looks were remarkably wild and queer; but I said nothing, and waited for the explanation. All that she had to tell me was that something she had unintentionally said or done had frightened Mrs. Frankland, and that Mrs. Frankland’s husband had sent her away on the spot. I disbelieved this at first—and very naturally, I think—but she persisted in the story, and answered all my questions by declaring that she could tell me nothing more. ‘So then,’ I said, ‘I am to believe that, after I have inconvenienced myself by sparing you, and after you have inconvenienced yourself by undertaking the business of nurse, I am to be insulted, and you are to be insulted, by your being sent away from Mrs. Frankland on the very day when you get to her, because she chooses to take a whim into her head?’ ‘I never accused Mrs. Frankland of taking a whim into her head,’ said Mrs. Jazeph, and stares me straight in the face, with such a look as I never saw in her eyes before, after all my five years’ experience of her. ‘What do you mean?’ I asked, giving her back her look, I can promise you. ‘Are you base enough to take the treatment you have received in the light of a favor?’ ‘I am just enough,’ said Mrs. Jazeph, as sharp as lightning, and still with that same stare straight at me—‘I am just enough not to blame Mrs. Frankland.’ ‘Oh, you are, are you?’ I said. ‘Then all I can tell you is, that I feel this insult, if you don’t; and that I consider Mrs. Frankland’s conduct to be the conduct of an ill-bred, impudent, capricious, unfeeling woman.’ Mrs. Jazeph takes a step up to me—takes a step, I give you my word of honor—and says distinctly, in so many words, ‘Mrs. Frankland is neither ill-bred, impudent, capricious, nor unfeeling.’ ‘Do you mean to contradict me, Mrs. Jazeph?’ I asked. ‘I mean to defend Mrs. Frankland from unjust imputations,’ says she.
