“It is hard for me to say,” he answered. “I have not your vivid imagination, my dear. I have no power of putting myself, at a moment’s notice, into a position that is not my own, and of knowing how I should act in it.”
“But suppose your wife was close to you—as close as I am now?

===Chinese===

你这故事，要哄得读者去疼一个后来证明是冒牌的假女人。」
「不！」罗丝蒙德热热地嚷道。「一个真女人——一个从不肯屈身作伪的女人——一个满是毛病短处的女人，可不管多大的险、多大的牺牲，口口声声只说真话。伦尼，你先听完再断。」热泪涌进她的眼；她把泪狠狠一挥，接着往下说。「那妻子长成大人，出了嫁，一无所知——记住这个！——对自己真正的身世一无所知。真相猝然揭发，把她压垮——她发觉自己横遭大祸，而这场祸，她的手没有染过一分。这个揭发要把她的神志震得摇摇欲坠；它劈头打下来的时候，她除了自己，再没有一个可倚靠的人；她满可以把真相瞒住丈夫，毫发无损；她要受一场试炼，在她血肉的软弱里摇动，经一夜可怖的诱惑；她胜过了它，出于自己的自由意志，她把自己所知道的一切，亲口告诉了丈夫。如今你说，伦尼，这样的女人叫什么？一个冒牌的假女人么？」
「不：一个牺牲者。」
「自己走向祭坛的牺牲者？要被献上祭坛的又是谁？」
「我可没有这样说。」
「伦尼，这故事若叫你来写，你拿她怎么办？我是说，你教她的丈夫怎么待她？这一问，牵涉的是男人的天性，女人的裁断做不得数。故事的结尾我想不出。你怎样结尾，亲爱的？」话音落处，她的嗓音低下去，低成她最柔的哀恳。她偎到他跟前，亲亲热热把手指插进他的头发。「你怎样结尾，亲爱的？」她又说一遍，低下头去，颤抖的嘴唇恰恰碰到他的额头。
他在椅子里不安地动，答道——「我不是写小说的人，罗丝蒙德。」
「可你若是那个丈夫，伦尼，你怎么办？」
「这话难说，」他答。「亲爱的，我没有你那副活灵活现的想象。别人临时把我安在一个不是我的位置上，叫我揣想我在那个位置上怎么办，我没有这个本事。」
「可倘若你的妻子就在你跟前——同我此刻一样近呢？

===Original===

Suppose she had just told you the dreadful secret, and was standing before you—as I am standing now—with the happiness of her whole life to come depending on one kind word from your lips? Oh, Lenny, you would not let her drop brokenhearted at your feet? You would know, let her birth be what it might, that she was still the same faithful creature who had cherished and served and trusted and worshipped you since her marriage-day, and who asked nothing in return but to lay her head on your bosom, and to hear you say that you loved her? You would know that she had nerved herself to tell the fatal secret, because, in her loyalty and love to her husband, she would rather die forsaken and despised, than live, deceiving him? You would know all this, and you would open your arms to the mother of your child, to the wife of your first love, though she was the lowliest of all lowly born women in the estimation of the world? Oh, you would, Lenny, I know you would!”
“Rosamond! how your hands tremble; how your voice alters! You are agitating yourself about this supposed story of yours, as if you were talking of real events.”
“You would take her to your heart, Lenny? You would open your arms to her without an instant of unworthy doubt?”
“Hush! hush! I hope I should.”
“Hope? only hope? Oh, think again, love, think again; and say you *know* you should!”
“Must I, Rosamond? Then I do say it.”
She drew back as the words passed his lips, and took the letter from the table.
“You have not yet asked me, Lenny, to read the letter that I found in the Myrtle Room. I offer to read it now of my own accord.”
She trembled a little as she spoke those few decisive words, but her utterance of them was clear and steady, as if her consciousness of being now irrevocably pledged to make the disclosure had strengthened her at last to dare all hazards and end all suspense.
Her husband turned toward the place from which the sound of her voice had reached him, with a mixed expression of perplexity and surprise in his face. “You pass so suddenly from one subject to another,” he said, “that I hardly know how to follow you. What in the world, Rosamond, takes you, at one jump, from a romantic argument about a situation in a novel, to the plain, practical business of reading an old letter?”
“Perhaps there is a closer connection between the two than you suspect,” she answered.
“A closer connection?

===Chinese===

倘若她方才把那个可怕的秘密告诉了你，正立在你面前——同我此刻立着一样——她一生将来的幸福，全押在你嘴唇上的一句好话？啊，伦尼，你不肯叫她心碎着倒在你脚前吧？她出身高低由它，你总知道，她还是那个忠忠实实的人，从成婚的日子起，疼你、伺候你、信你、敬你，别的什么都不求，只求把头枕上你的胸，听你说一句你爱她？你总知道，她鼓足了胆子把那致命的秘密说出来，正因为她对丈夫的忠和爱：她宁可被弃绝、被轻贱着死，不肯欺骗着他活？这一切你都明白，你还是会张开怀抱，去抱你孩子的母亲、你初恋情人的妻子，哪怕照世人的眼光，她是低贱出身里最低贱的一个？啊，你肯的，伦尼，我知道你肯！」
「罗丝蒙德！你的手抖得多厉害；你的嗓音都变了！你拿你那个假想的故事，把自己搅成这个样子，倒像你在谈真事。」
「你会把她搂进心里去么，伦尼？你会朝她张开怀抱，一瞬也不存一点见不得人的疑心么？」
「嘘！嘘！我盼着我肯。」
「盼？只盼？啊，再想一想，亲爱的，再想一想；说你知道你肯！」
「罗丝蒙德，非说不可么？那我就说。」
这句话出了他的口，她退开了，从桌上拿起那封信。
「伦尼，我找着的那封信，你还一直没有叫我念呢。如今我自己情愿念。」
这几句斩钉截铁的话出口时，她微微发抖；可话音清楚、稳当，仿佛她意识到自己已经把揭发认下，再不能反悔，这一层到底把她壮起胆来，叫她敢担一切的风险，断了一切的悬望。
她丈夫朝她声音来的方向转过头去，脸上疑和惊混作一团。「你从这一个题目跳到那一个题目，跳得这样急，」他说，「我都跟不上你了。罗丝蒙德，到底是什么，叫你一蹦从小说里一场假想的争辩，蹦到念一封旧信这桩实实在在的正事上来？」
「也许两件事当中牵着的线，比你想的紧，」她答。
「牵着的线？

===Original===

What connection? I don’t understand.”
“The letter will explain.”
“Why the letter? Why should *you* not explain?”
She stole one anxious look at his face, and saw that a sense of something serious to come was now overshadowing his mind for the first time.
“Rosamond!” he exclaimed, “there is some mystery—”
“There are no mysteries between us two,” she interposed quickly. “There never have been any, love; there never shall be.” She moved a little nearer to him to take her old favorite place on his knee, then checked herself, and drew back again to the table. Warning tears in her eyes bade her distrust her own firmness, and read the letter where she could not feel the beating of his heart.
“Did I tell you,” she resumed, after waiting an instant to compose herself, “where I found the folded piece of paper which I put into your hand in the Myrtle Room?”
“No,” he replied, “I think not.”
“I found it at the back of the frame of that picture—the picture of the ghostly woman with the wicked face. I opened it immediately, and saw that it was a letter. The address inside, the first line under it, and one of the two signatures which it contained, were in a handwriting that I knew.”
“Whose!”
“The handwriting of the late Mrs. Treverton.”
“Of your mother?”
“Of the late Mrs. Treverton.”
“Gracious God, Rosamond! why do you speak of her in that way?”
“Let me read, and you will know. You have seen, with my eyes, what the Myrtle Room is like; you have seen, with my eyes, every object which the search through it brought to light; you must now see, with my eyes, what this letter contains. It is the Secret of the Myrtle Room.”
She bent close over the faint, faded writing, and read these words:

> “We have parted, Arthur, forever, and I have not had the courage to embitter our farewell by confessing that I have deceived you—cruelly and basely deceived you. But a few minutes since, you were weeping by my bedside and speaking of our child. My wronged, my beloved husband, the little daughter of your heart is not yours, is not mine. She is a love-child, whom I have imposed on you for mine. Her father was a miner at Porthgenna; her mother is my maid, Sarah Leeson.”

===Chinese===

什么线？我不懂。」
「信会说明。」
「为什么是信？为什么*你*不说明？」
她偷偷朝他脸上不安地望了一眼，看出：有什么严重的事要来的预感，头一回罩住了他的心。
「罗丝蒙德！」他嚷道，「这里头有玄虚——」
「我们两个之间没有玄虚，」她赶忙插进来。「从来就没有，亲爱的；将来也决不有。」她朝他挪近一点，要照老习惯坐上他的膝，随即止住自己，又退回桌边。眼里含着的、预先示警的泪，叫她别信自己那份硬撑的坚决；她要在听不见他心跳的地方念这封信。
「我告诉你过没有，」她顿了一顿，定一定神，接着说，「我放进你手里那片叠着的纸，是在桃金娘房间哪一处找着的？」
「没有，」他答。「我想没有。」
「是在那张画的框子背后找着的——那张画着鬼妇人的画，那张邪脸的画。我立刻拆开，看出是一封信。信里的称呼，称呼底下的头一行，还有它签着的两个名字里的一个，那笔字我认得。」
「谁的！」
「故去的特雷弗顿夫人的手笔。」
「你母亲？」
「故去的特雷弗顿夫人。」
「仁慈的上帝，罗丝蒙德！你怎么这样称呼她？」
「容我念，你便知道了。我的眼睛已经叫你看见了桃金娘房间什么样；我的眼睛已经叫你看见了搜查它时每一件露出来的东西；如今你必须再用我的眼睛，看这封信里写的是什么。这就是桃金娘房间的秘密。」
她俯下身去，凑近那片淡淡的、褪了色的字，念出这几行：

> 「我们从此永别了，阿瑟，我却连一点勇气也没有，不肯拿认罪的话来苦我们的别——我欺瞒了你，残忍地、卑鄙地欺瞒了你。几分钟之前，你还在我床边流泪，说我们的孩子。我受屈的、我心爱的丈夫：你心尖上的这个小女儿，不是你的，也不是我的。她是一个私生的孩子，我强安在我名下，充作我生的。她的父亲是波思根纳一个矿工；她的母亲是我的使女，萨拉·利森。」

===Original===

Rosamond paused, but never raised her head from the letter. She heard her husband lay his hand suddenly on the table; she heard him start to his feet; she heard him draw his breath heavily in one quick gasp; she heard him whisper to himself the instant after—“A love-child!” With a fearful, painful distinctness she heard those three words. The tone in which he whispered them turned her cold. But she never moved, for there was more to read; and while more remained, if her life had depended on it, she could not have looked up.
In a moment more she went on, and read these lines next:

> “I have many heavy sins to answer for, but this one sin you must pardon, Arthur, for I committed it through fondness for you. That fondness told me a secret which you sought to hide from me. That fondness told me that your barren wife would never make your heart all her own until she had borne you a child; and your lips proved it true. Your first words, when you came back from sea, and when the infant was placed in your arms, were—‘I have never loved you, Rosamond, as I love you now.’ If you had not said that, I should never have kept my guilty secret.
> “I can add no more, for death is very near me. How the fraud was committed, and what my other motives were, I must leave you to discover from the mother of the child, who writes this under my dictation, and who is charged to give it to you when I am no more. You will be merciful to the poor little creature who bears my name. Be merciful also to her unhappy parent: she is only guilty of too blindly obeying me. If there is anything that mitigates the bitterness of my remorse, it is the remembrance that my act of deceit saved the most faithful and the most affectionate of women from shame that she had not deserved. Remember me forgivingly, Arthur—words may tell how I have sinned against you; no words can tell how I have loved you!”

===Chinese===

罗丝蒙德停了停，可头始终没有从信上抬起。她听见丈夫的手猛地按在桌上；听见他从座上跳起；听见他急急抽了一口气，重重地吸进去；随即又听见他低低自语——「一个私生的孩子！」这三个字，她听得清清楚楚，怕人、痛心。那声气叫她浑身冰凉。可她一动不敢动，还有下文要念；还有下文在，纵然拿性命作押，她也不敢抬头。
片刻之后她接着往下念，念的是这几行：

> 「我背着的重罪多得很，可这一桩罪你必要赦，阿瑟，因为我是疼你才犯的。那份疼教会了我一个秘密，你原想瞒着我的一个秘密。那份疼告诉我：你那无出的妻子，不替你生个孩子，便休想占住你整颗的心；你的嘴唇证明了这话不假。你出海回来，婴儿抱在你怀里，你的头一句话是——『罗丝蒙德，我从前爱你，从没有像如今爱得这样。』你若没说这句话，我这桩罪孽的秘密，我决计瞒不到底。
> 「我添不出了，死已近在眼前。那场欺瞒怎样做下的，我另外的心思又是些什么，只好留给你自己去问孩子的母亲——她此刻正听我口授写这封信，我咽了气，她受命把它交给你。顶着我姓氏的那个可怜小生命，你要慈悲。她那苦命的生母，你也要慈悲：她的罪，不过是把我的话盲从得过了头。我的悔恨苦到十分，若有一件事减得它一分苦，便是想起：我这一场欺瞒，救下了一个顶忠、顶爱的女人，免掉她不该受的羞辱。阿瑟，饶恕着我记着我——言语说得出我怎样得罪了你；言语说不出我怎样爱你！」

===Original===

She had struggled on thus far, and had reached the last line on the second page of the letter, when she paused again, and then tried to read the first of the two signatures—“Rosamond Treverton.” She faintly repeated two syllables of that familiar Christian name—the name that was on her husband’s lips every hour of the day!—and strove to articulate the third, but her voice failed her. All the sacred household memories which that ruthless letter had profaned forever seemed to tear themselves away from her heart at the same moment. With a low, moaning cry she dropped her arms on the table, and laid her head down on them, and hid her face.
She heard nothing, she was conscious of nothing, until she felt a touch on her shoulder—a light touch from a hand that trembled. Every pulse in her body bounded in answer to it, and she looked up.
Her husband had guided himself near to her by the table. The tears were glistening in his dim, sightless eyes. As she rose and touched him, his arms opened, and closed fast around her.
“My own Rosamond!” he said, “come to me and be comforted!”

===Chinese===

她挣扎到这里，念到信第二页的末一行，又停住了，随后想念两个签名里头的一个——「罗丝蒙德·特雷弗顿」。那个熟熟的教名，她轻轻念出两个音节——那个名字，她丈夫一天到晚挂在嘴边！——第三个音节，她竭力想说，嗓子却哑了。那封无情的手书永远亵渎了的那一切神圣的家常记忆，仿佛在同一瞬，全要从她心里撕走。她低低一声呻吟似的叫，两臂伏在桌上，头枕下去，把脸藏了。
她什么也没有听见，什么也没有知觉，直到肩上觉出轻轻的一触——一只手的轻轻的一触，那只手在抖。她全身上下每一根脉，都应着这一触跳起来；她抬起了头。
她丈夫扶着桌子，摸索着到了她近旁。他昏茫无光的两眼里，闪着泪。她立起来一触他，他的两条臂膀张开，把她紧紧箍住了。
「我自己的罗丝蒙德！」他说，「到我怀里来，受安慰吧！」

# Book VI / 第六部
===Original===



===Chinese===

第六部

## I Uncle Joseph / 一 约瑟夫舅舅
===Original===

The day and the night had passed, and the new morning had come, before the husband and wife could trust themselves to speak calmly of the Secret, and to face resignedly the duties and the sacrifices which the discovery of it imposed on them.
Leonard’s first question referred to those lines in the letter which Rosamond had informed him were in a handwriting that she knew. Finding that he was at a loss to understand what means she could have of forming an opinion on this point, she explained that, after Captain Treverton’s death, many letters had naturally fallen into her possession which had been written by Mrs. Treverton to her husband. They treated of ordinary domestic subjects, and she had read them often enough to become thoroughly acquainted with the peculiarities of Mrs. Treverton’s handwriting. It was remarkably large, firm, and masculine in character; and the address, the line under it, and the uppermost of the two signatures in the letter which had been found in the Myrtle Room, exactly resembled it in every particular.
The next question related to the body of the letter. The writing of this, of the second signature (“Sarah Leeson”), and of the additional lines on the third page, also signed by Sarah Leeson, proclaimed itself in each case to be the production of the same person. While stating that fact to her husband, Rosamond did not forget to explain to him that, while reading the letter on the previous day, her strength and courage had failed her before she got to the end of it. She added that the postscript which she had thus omitted to read was of importance, because it mentioned the circumstances under which the Secret had been hidden; and begged that he would listen while she made him acquainted with its contents without any further delay.
Sitting as close to his side, now, as if they were enjoying their first honeymoon days over again, she read these last lines—the lines which her mother had written sixteen years before, on the morning when she fled from Porthgenna Tower:

===Chinese===

丈夫和妻子过了一个白天一个黑夜，才敢放心拿平稳的声气谈那个秘密，才肯顺顺当当地面对它的揭发给两人加下的责任和牺牲。
伦纳德头一个问题，问的是信里那几行罗丝蒙德告诉过他、说她认得那笔字的文字。他一时想不明白她凭什么能下这个判断；她便解释：特雷弗顿舰长去世之后，特雷弗顿夫人写给她丈夫的许多书信，自然都落到她手里。那些信谈的都是家常，她读得熟了，特雷弗顿夫人笔迹的特点，她体察得透熟。那笔字出奇地大、出奇地有劲、出奇地有男子气；桃金娘房间找出的那封信里的称呼、称呼底下一行、两个签名里上头那个，样样同它一般无二。
下一个问题问到信的正文。这一部分，同第二个签名（「萨拉·利森」）、同第三页上另添的几行——也署着萨拉·利森——笔迹件件证明，出的是同一个人的手。罗丝蒙德向丈夫陈述这个事实时，没有忘记解释：前天读那封信，读到末尾之前，她的力气和勇气先尽了。她又添上说：她因此漏掉没念的那段附言要紧，因为它说了秘密藏起来的情形；求他听她把附言的内容立刻说给他，一刻也不必耽搁。
如今她紧偎在他身边坐着，仿佛重过初度的蜜月；她念出那末了几行——她母亲十六年前写下、在逃离波思根纳塔楼那个早晨写下的几行：

===Original===

> “If this paper should ever be found (which I pray with my whole heart it never may be), I wish to say that I have come to the resolution of hiding it, because I dare not show the writing that it contains to my master, to whom it is addressed. In doing what I now propose to do, though I am acting against my mistress’s last wishes, I am not breaking the solemn engagement which she obliged me to make before her on her deathbed. That engagement forbids me to destroy this letter, or to take it away with me if I leave the house. I shall do neither—my purpose is to conceal it in the place, of all others, where I think there is least chance of its ever being found again. Any hardship or misfortune which may follow as a consequence of this deceitful proceeding on my part, will fall on myself. Others, I believe, in my conscience, will be the happier for the hiding of the dreadful Secret which this letter contains.”

===Chinese===

> 「倘这张纸日后叫人找着（我全心祷告但愿永不叫人找着），我想说一句：我拿定主意把它藏起来，因为我不敢把它里头的文字拿给我东家看，信是写给他的。我此刻要做的这件事，虽然违背主母临终的心愿，却没有破她在病榻上逼我立下的那个庄严的信约。那信约禁我毁掉这封信，也禁我离家时把它带走。这两样我都不做——我的意思是把它藏在天下万处我以为顶不容易再寻着的地方。我这桩欺瞒的行径往后结出什么艰难祸殃，都落在我一个人头上。旁的人，我凭良心相信，倒会因这封可怕的密信藏起来，反得到快活。」

===Original===

“There can be no doubt, now,” said Leonard, when his wife had read to the end; “Mrs. Jazeph, Sarah Leeson, and the servant who disappeared from Porthgenna Tower, are one and the same person.”
“Poor creature!” said Rosamond, sighing as she put down the letter. “We know now why she warned me so anxiously not to go into the Myrtle Room. Who can say what she must have suffered when she came as a stranger to my bedside? Oh, what would I not give if I had been less hasty with her! It is dreadful to remember that I spoke to her as a servant whom I expected to obey me; it is worse still to feel that I can not, even now, think of her as a child should think of a mother. How can I ever tell her that I know the Secret? how—” She paused, with a heartsick consciousness of the slur that was cast on her birth; she paused, shrinking as she thought of the name that her husband had given to her, and of her own parentage, which the laws of society disdained to recognize.
“Why do you stop?” asked Leonard.
“I was afraid—” she began, and paused again.
“Afraid,” he said, finishing the sentence for her, “that words of pity for that unhappy woman might wound my sensitive pride by reminding me of the circumstances of your birth? Rosamond! I should be unworthy of your matchless truthfulness toward me, if I, on my side, did not acknowledge that this discovery *has* wounded me as only a proud man can be wounded. My pride has been born and bred in me. My pride, even while I am now speaking to you, takes advantage of my first moments of composure, and deludes me into doubting, in face of all probability, whether the words you have read to me can, after all, be words of truth. But, strong as that inborn and inbred feeling is—hard as it may be for me to discipline and master it as I ought, and must and will—there is another feeling in my heart that is stronger yet.” He felt for her hand, and took it in his; then added—“From the hour when you first devoted your life to your blind husband—from the hour when you won all his gratitude, as you had already won all his love, you took a place in his heart, Rosamond, from which nothing, not even such a shock as has now assailed us, can move you! High as I have always held the worth of rank in my estimation, I have learned, even before the event of yesterday, to hold the worth of my wife, let her parentage be what it may, higher still.”

===Chinese===

「如今再没有可疑的了，」妻子念到末尾，伦纳德说。「贾泽夫太太、萨拉·利森、波思根纳塔楼里失踪的那个使女，是同一个人。」
「可怜的人！」罗丝蒙德说，放下信，叹息。「如今我们知道她为什么那样苦苦警告我，不叫我进桃金娘房间。她乔装生客来到我床边的时候，受过怎样的煎熬，谁说得尽？啊，我当初对她少急躁一点，叫我拿什么谢天都肯！想起来可怕：我把她当仆人使唤，只当她该听我的；想起来更可怕：直到此刻，我心里还不能够拿孩子对母亲的心思去想她。我怎么开口告诉她，我知道了那个秘密？我怎么——」她停住了，心头一阵沉痛，觉出自己出身上蒙着的那层玷污；她停住了，想起丈夫给她的那个名目，想起社会法律不屑认的那点出身，畏缩了。
「为什么停住？」伦纳德问。
「我怕——」她起头，又停住。
「怕，」他替她把话接完，「怕你为那苦命女人抱怜惜，一提你出身的情形，便伤了我那点骄傲的自尊？罗丝蒙德！我若不也照样承认——这个揭发*确然*伤了我，伤得只有一个骄傲的人受伤的那等伤法——我便配不上你对我的那份无匹的真诚。我的骄傲生在我身上，长在我身上。我的骄傲，趁我这会儿同你说话的当口，趁我心神初定的头几刻，又在作祟，哄着我逆着一切情理去疑惑：你念给我听的那些话，到底当真是真话不是。可是，这份与生俱来、长养在身的情结再强——我要管束它、降伏它，照我该当的、必得做的、下决心做的去做，再难——我心里另有一种情结，比它更强。」他摸索着找到她的手，握在手里；又添上——「打你头一回把一生献给瞎眼的丈夫那一刻起——打你赢尽他全部的感激、如你早已赢尽他全部的爱情那一刻起——罗丝蒙德，你便在他心里占下了一个位置，什么也动不得你，连如今这样劈头打来的一记震动也动不得！门第的价值，我一向看得再高不过；可早在昨天那件事之前，我便学会了把它看低——我妻子的价值，她出身由它是什么，我看得更高。」

===Original===

“Oh, Lenny, Lenny, I can’t hear you praise me, if you talk in the same breath as if I had made a sacrifice in marrying you! But for my blind husband I might never have deserved what you have just said of me. When I first read that fearful letter, I had one moment of vile, ungrateful doubt if your love for me would hold out against the discovery of the Secret. I had one moment of horrible temptation, that drew me away from you when I ought to have put the letter into your hand. It was the sight of you, waiting for me to speak again, so innocent of all knowledge of what happened close by you, that brought me back to my senses, and told me what I ought to do. It was the sight of my blind husband that made me conquer the temptation to destroy that letter in the first hour of discovering it. Oh, if I had been the hardest-hearted of women, could I have ever taken your hand again—could I kiss you, could I lie down by your side, and hear you fall asleep, night after night, feeling that I had abused your blind dependence on me to serve my own selfish interests? knowing that I had only succeeded in my deceit because your affliction made you incapable of suspecting deception? No, no; I can hardly believe that the basest of women could be guilty of such baseness as that; and I can claim nothing more for myself than the credit of having been true to my trust. You said yesterday, love, in the Myrtle Room, that the one faithful friend to you in your blindness, who never failed, was your wife. It is reward enough and consolation enough for me, now that the worst is over, to know that you can say so still.”
“Yes, Rosamond, the worst is over; but we must not forget that there may be hard trials still to meet.”
“Hard trials, love? To what trials do you refer?”
“Perhaps, Rosamond, I overrate the courage that the sacrifice demands; but, to *me* at least, it will be a hard sacrifice of my own feelings to make strangers partakers in the knowledge that we now possess.”
Rosamond looked at her husband in astonishment. “Why need we tell the Secret to anyone?” she asked.
“Assuming that we can satisfy ourselves of the genuineness of that letter,” he answered, “we shall have no choice but to tell it to strangers. You can not forget the circumstances under which your father—under which Captain Treverton—”
“Call him my father,” said Rosamond, sadly.

===Chinese===

「啊，伦尼，伦尼，你一口气里既说我嫁你是牺牲，我就听不得你夸我！要不是这个瞎眼的丈夫，你说我的这些好处，我也许一辈子当不起。头一回读那封可怕的信的时候，我有一瞬卑污的、忘恩的疑惑：你的爱经不经得起秘密揭开的这一击。我有一瞬可怕的诱惑：那封信原该放到你手里，它偏偏把我从你身边拉开。是你唤醒了我——你在等我往下说，对你身边出的事一无所知，那份天真无辜——把我唤回了神志，教我知道我该做什么。是我那瞎眼的丈夫的模样，教我胜过了毁掉那封信的诱惑——发现它后的头一个时辰里。啊，我若是世上心肠最狠的女人，我后来还敢握你的手么——还敢亲你么，还敢躺在你的身边，一夜一夜听你睡去，心里记着我滥用了你对我瞎眼的信赖，去遂我自己自私的私图么？记着我欺瞒得逞，全靠你的苦难叫你疑心不到欺瞒么？不，不；天下最贱的女人犯这样贱的事，我几乎不肯信；我自己能领的，不过守住了信托这一点功。你昨天在桃金娘房间说，你瞎了眼，永不误事的那一个忠实朋友，是你的妻子。如今最难的一关过去了，我知道这句话你还说得出，这便是足够的酬报，足够的安慰。」
「不错，罗丝蒙德，最难的一关过去了；可我们不要忘，前头也许还有难挨的试炼。」
「难挨的试炼，亲爱的？你指什么试炼？」
「罗丝蒙德，也许我把这份牺牲要的胆量估得过高；可至少对*我*，叫外人同享我们如今才知道的事，要牺牲我自己的感情，是难牺牲的。」
罗丝蒙德惊愕地望她的丈夫。「何必把秘密告诉谁呢？」她问。
「就算我们验得明那封信是真的，」他答，「我们便没有选择，只好告诉外人。你忘不了你父亲——忘不了特雷弗顿舰长——」
「叫他我的父亲，」罗丝蒙德凄凄地说。

===Original===

“Remember how he loved me, and how I loved him, and say ‘my father’ still.”
“I am afraid I must say ‘Captain Treverton’ now,” returned Leonard, “or I shall hardly be able to explain simply and plainly what it is very necessary that you should know. Captain Treverton died without leaving a will. His only property was the purchase-money of this house and estate; and you inherited it, as his next of kin—”
Rosamond started back in her chair and clasped her hands in dismay. “Oh, Lenny,” she said simply, “I have thought so much of you, since I found the letter, that I never remembered this!”
“It is time to remember it, my love. If you are not Captain Treverton’s daughter, you have no right to one farthing of the fortune that you possess; and it must be restored at once to the person who *is* Captain Treverton’s next of kin—or, in other words, to his brother.”
“To that man!” exclaimed Rosamond. “To that man who is a stranger to us, who holds our very name in contempt! Are we to be made poor that he may be made rich?—”
“We are to do what is honorable and just, at any sacrifice of our own interests and ourselves,” said Leonard, firmly. “I believe, Rosamond, that my consent, as your husband, is necessary, according to the law, to effect this restitution. If Mr. Andrew Treverton was the bitterest enemy I had on earth, and if the restoring of this money utterly ruined us both in our worldly circumstances, I would give it back of my own accord to the last farthing—and so would you!”
The blood mantled in his cheeks as he spoke. Rosamond looked at him admiringly in silence. “Who would have him less proud,” she thought, fondly, “when his pride speaks in such words as those!”
“You understand now,” continued Leonard, “that we have duties to perform which will oblige us to seek help from others, and which will therefore render it impossible to keep the Secret to ourselves? If we search all England for her, Sarah Leeson must be found. Our future actions depend upon her answers to our inquiries, upon her testimony to the genuineness of that letter. Although I am resolved beforehand to shield myself behind no technical quibbles and delays—although I want nothing but evidence that is morally conclusive, however legally imperfect it may be—it is still impossible to proceed without seeking advice immediately.

===Chinese===

「记着他怎样疼我，我怎样疼他，还是叫他『我的父亲』。」
「如今我怕我只能说『特雷弗顿舰长』了，」伦纳德答，「不然有件顶要紧你该知道的事，我讲不简单、讲不明白。特雷弗顿舰长去世没有立遗嘱。他唯一的产业，是这座宅子、这片田产的买价；你是他最近的血亲，承了它——」
罗丝蒙德从椅子上惊得往后一缩，两手绝望地合拢。「啊，伦尼，」她直直地说，「找到那封信之后，我一直想着你，这个我竟全没想起！」
「如今该想起了，我的爱。你若不是特雷弗顿舰长的女儿，你手里的家私，一法新的名分也没有；必须立刻归还给真正算特雷弗顿舰长最近血亲的那个人——换句话说，还给他的兄弟。」
「给那个人！」罗丝蒙德嚷道。「给那个同我们不相识的人，给那个把我们姓氏都轻贱的人！要叫我们穷，好叫他富么？——」
「我们要做的是体面公道的事，我们自己的利益、我们自己的身家，牺牲多少都由它，」伦纳德说，斩钉截铁。「罗丝蒙德，照法律，这事要办成，必须有你丈夫我点头。安德鲁·特雷弗顿先生便是我世上头号的死敌，还这笔钱便把我们俩的家境全毁了，我也要自愿把它一个法新不剩地还回去——你也要！」
说这话时，他腮上血色涌起。罗丝蒙德默默地欣赏地望着他。「他的骄傲说得出这样的话，谁还嫌他骄傲呢！」她痴痴地想。
「如今你明白了，」伦纳德接着说，「我们有责任要尽，要尽便须求人相助，因此这秘密便再瞒不住人？我们搜遍英格兰，也要把萨拉·利森寻出来。我们将来的举动，全看她对我们盘问的答话，全看她证那封信的真。我虽已拿定主意，绝不肯躲在律条的诡辩和拖延背后——我要的证据只要道义上确凿，法理上有瑕疵由它——可要是不同时立刻请教，事情还是办不动。

===Original===

The lawyer who always managed Captain Treverton’s affairs, and who now manages ours, is the proper person to direct us in instituting a search, and to assist us, if necessary, in making the restitution.”
“How quietly and firmly you speak of it, Lenny! Will not the abandoning of my fortune be a dreadful loss to us?”
“We must think of it as a gain to our consciences, Rosamond, and must alter our way of life resignedly to suit our altered means. But we need speak no more of that until we are assured of the necessity of restoring the money. My immediate anxiety, and your immediate anxiety, must turn now on the discovery of Sarah Leeson—no! on the discovery of your mother; I must learn to call her by that name, or I shall not learn to pity and forgive her.”
Rosamond nestled closer to her husband’s side. “Every word you say, love, does my heart good,” she whispered, laying her head on his shoulder. “You will help me and strengthen me, when the time comes, to meet my mother as I ought? Oh, how pale and worn and weary she was when she stood by my bedside, and looked at me and my child! Will it be long before we find her? Is she far away from us, I wonder? or nearer, much nearer than we think?”
Before Leonard could answer, he was interrupted by a knock at the door, and Rosamond was surprised by the appearance of the maidservant. Betsey was flushed, excited, and out of breath; but she contrived to deliver intelligibly a brief message from Mr. Munder, the steward, requesting permission to speak to Mr. Frankland, or to Mrs. Frankland, on business of importance.
“What is it? What does he want?” asked Rosamond.
“I think, ma’am, he wants to know whether he had better send for the constable or not,” answered Betsey.
“Send for the constable!” repeated Rosamond. “Are there thieves in the house in broad daylight?”
“Mr. Munder says he don’t know but what it may be worse than thieves,” replied Betsey. “It’s the foreigner again, if you please, ma’am. He come up and rung at the door as bold as brass, and asked if he could see Mrs. Frankland.”
“The foreigner!” exclaimed Rosamond, laying her hand eagerly on her husband’s arm.
“Yes, ma’am,” said Betsey. “Him as come here to go over the house along with the lady—”
Rosamond, with characteristic impulsiveness, started to her feet. “Let me go down!” she began.
“Wait,” interposed Leonard, catching her by the hand.

===Chinese===

从前经手特雷弗顿舰长事务、如今经手我们事务的那位律师，正是给我们指点怎样着手寻人的正主，必要的时候，归还的事，也须他襄助。」
「伦尼，你说得这样安静、这样坚定！放弃我的家私，对我们要不是可怕的损失么？」
「罗丝蒙德，我们要把它当作良心的进项看，日子要顺顺当当地跟着家私的改样去改。不过必要归还的话验实之前，这一层不必多谈。我眼前的心事，你眼前的心事，如今都在寻萨拉·利森——不！都在寻你的母亲；这个名字我必须学着叫，不然怜惜她、饶恕她，我也学不会。」
罗丝蒙德把身子朝丈夫那边偎得更紧。「你说的每一个字，亲爱的，都治我心的病，」她低低地说，头枕上他的肩。「到时候来了，你会帮我、壮我的胆，教我照该当的样子去见我母亲？啊，她站在我床边、望着我和孩子的时候，多苍白、多憔悴、多困乏！找着她，要多久？她离我们远么？我纳闷？还是近，比我们想的近得多？」
伦纳德还没来得及答，门上一声敲，打断了他；女仆的出现又叫罗丝蒙德吃了一惊。贝琪满脸涨红，慌慌张张，喘着气；可她到底把蒙德先生——管家公——托她的一句短短的话说得明明白白：求见弗兰克兰先生或弗兰克兰太太，有要紧的公事。
「什么事？他要什么？」罗丝蒙德问。
「太太，我想他是想知道，要不要去请巡捕，」贝琪答。
「请巡捕！」罗丝蒙德跟着念。「青天白日，宅里有贼？」
「蒙德先生说他不知道，兴许比贼还坏，」贝琪答。「又是那个洋人，太太，就是他。他大摇大摆上来按铃，说要见弗兰克兰太太。」
「洋人！」罗丝蒙德嚷道，急急把手按在丈夫的胳膊上。
「是，太太，」贝琪说。「就是陪着那位太太来看房子的那位——」
罗丝蒙德照她那说动就动的急性子，霍地立起。「让我下去！」她起头。
「等一等，」伦纳德插进来，一把抓住她的手。

===Original===

“There is not the least need for you to go downstairs.—Show the foreigner up here,” he continued, addressing himself to Betsey, “and tell Mr. Munder that we will take the management of this business into our own hands.”
Rosamond sat down again by her husband’s side. “This is a very strange accident,” she said, in a low, serious tone. “It must be something more than mere chance that puts the clue into our hands, at the moment when we least expected to find it.”
The door opened for the second time, and there appeared, modestly, on the threshold, a little old man, with rosy cheeks and long white hair. A small leather case was slung by a strap at his side, and the stem of a pipe peeped out of the breast pocket of his coat. He advanced one step into the room, stopped, raised both his hands, with his felt hat crumpled up in them, to his heart, and made five fantastic bows in quick succession—two to Mrs. Frankland, two to her husband, and one to Mrs. Frankland again, as an act of separate and special homage to the lady. Never had Rosamond seen a more complete embodiment in human form of perfect innocence and perfect harmlessness than the foreigner who was described in the housekeeper’s letter as an audacious vagabond, and who was dreaded by Mr. Munder as something worse than a thief!
“Madam and good Sir,” said the old man, advancing a little nearer at Mrs. Frankland’s invitation, “I ask your pardon for intruding myself. My name is Joseph Buschmann. I live in the town of Truro, where I work in cabinets and tea-caddies, and other shining woods. I am also, if you please, the same little foreign man who was scolded by the big majordomo when I came to see the house. All that I ask of your kindness is, that you will let me say for my errand here and for myself, and for another person who is very near to my love—one little word. I will be but few minutes, Madam and good Sir, and then I will go my ways again, with my best wishes and my best thanks.”
“Pray consider, Mr. Buschmann, that our time is your time,” said Leonard. “We have no engagement whatever which need oblige you to shorten your visit. I must tell you beforehand, in order to prevent any embarrassment on either side, that I have the misfortune to be blind. I can promise you, however, my best attention as far as listening goes. Rosamond, is Mr. Buschmann seated?”
Mr.

===Chinese===

「你全不必下楼。——请洋人上来，」他接着对贝琪说，「告诉蒙德先生，这桩事我们自己料理。」
罗丝蒙德重新在丈夫身边坐下。「这真是一桩古怪的巧事，」她说，低低的、庄重的声气。「恰在我们最不指望寻着线索的时刻，线索偏偏递到我们手里，单靠凑巧，说不下去。」
门第二次开了；门限上，端端地现出一个小小的老头儿，红喷喷的腮，长长的白发。一只小皮匣用皮带挎在身边，外衣胸袋里探出半截烟斗柄。他朝屋里进了一步，站住，举起两手——毡帽攥在手里——按在胸口，一连串五个古怪的躬：两个朝弗兰克兰太太，两个朝她丈夫，再一个又朝弗兰克兰太太——算是对太太单另的、特别的敬礼。装成人的形状的十足的清白、十足的无害，罗丝蒙德从没见过比这更完备的化身——管家婆信里写的那个放肆的流氓，蒙德先生疑心比贼还坏的那个家伙！
「太太，好先生，」老人说，得了弗兰克兰太太的邀请，又上前一点。「冒昧搅扰，先请恕罪。我姓布什曼，名约瑟夫。我住特鲁罗城，做的是橱柜、茶叶匣，别的发亮的木头活。我便是，你若不嫌，那个先前来看房子、叫大管家骂了一场的又矮又小的洋人。我求您二位开恩的，不过许我为我来此的差事、为我自家、为另一个同我疼爱贴得极近的人，说一句小小的话。几分钟就完，太太，好先生，随后我便走了，带着我最诚的祝愿、最诚的谢意。」
「布什曼先生，请把我们的工夫当作你的工夫，」伦纳德说。「我们毫无旁的约，用不着你把来访缩短。我先说明一句，省得彼此拘束：我不幸瞎了眼。不过就听这一层说，我担保尽力留心。罗丝蒙德，布什曼先生坐下了么？」

===Original===

Buschmann was still standing near the door, and was expressing sympathy by bowing to Mr. Frankland again, and crumpling his felt hat once more over his heart.
“Pray come nearer, and sit down,” said Rosamond. “And don’t imagine for one moment that any opinion of the steward’s has the least influence on us, or that we feel it at all necessary for you to apologize for what took place the last time you came to this house. We have an interest—a very great interest,” she added, with her usual hearty frankness, “in hearing anything that you have to tell us. You are the person of all others whom we are, just at this time—” She stopped, feeling her foot touched by her husband’s, and rightly interpreting the action as a warning not to speak too unrestrainedly to the visitor before he had explained his object in coming to the house.
Looking very much pleased, and a little surprised also, when he heard Rosamond’s last words, Uncle Joseph drew a chair near to the table by which Mr. and Mrs. Frankland were sitting, crumpled his felt hat up smaller than ever, and put it in one of his side pockets, drew from the other a little packet of letters, placed them on his knees as he sat down, patted them gently with both hands, and entered on his explanation in these terms:
“Madam and good Sir,” he began, “before I can say comfortably my little word, I must, with your leave, travel backward to the last time when I came to this house in company with my niece.”
“Your niece!” exclaimed Rosamond and Leonard, both speaking together.
“My niece, Sarah,” said Uncle Joseph, “the only child of my sister Agatha. It is for the love of Sarah, if you please, that I am here now. She is the one last morsel of my flesh and blood that is left to me in the world. The rest, they are all gone! My wife, my little Joseph, my brother Max, my sister Agatha and the husband she married, the good and noble Englishman, Leeson—they are all, all gone!”
“Leeson,” said Rosamond, pressing her husband’s hand significantly under the table. “Your niece’s name is Sarah Leeson?”
Uncle Joseph sighed and shook his head. “One day,” he said, “of all the days in the year the evilmost for Sarah, she changed that name. Of the man she married—who is dead now, Madam—it is little or nothing that I know but this: His name was Jazeph, and he used her ill, for which I think him the First Scoundrel!

===Chinese===

布什曼先生还立在门边，又朝弗兰克兰先生躬了一躬，又把毡帽在胸口攥了一攥，表示怜惜。
「请走近些，坐下，」罗丝蒙德说。「还有，一刻也不要想管家公的意见对我们有一丝影响，也不要想你上回到这宅子里出的事，还须道歉。我们有一份利害——一份顶大的利害，」她照往常那副爽直的性子添上说，「要听你告诉我们的每一句话。我们此刻恰恰要找的人——」她停住了，觉出丈夫的脚碰了碰她的脚，领会那是个警告：客人还没说明来意之前，对他说话别太放。
听见罗丝蒙德末了那句话，老人满面欢喜，又带几分诧异。约瑟夫舅舅拉过一把椅子，到弗兰克兰夫妇坐着的桌边，把毡帽攥得比先前更小，塞进一边口袋；从另一边掏出一小包信，坐下来搁在膝头，两只手轻轻拍了拍，随后这样开讲：
「太太，好先生，」他起头，「我这句小小的话要说得安稳，先得蒙你们允许，往回走一程，走到我同我侄女上回来这宅子的那一天。」
「你的侄女！」罗丝蒙德和伦纳德同声嚷。
「我侄女萨拉，」约瑟夫舅舅说。「我姐姐阿加莎的独生女。我今日到这里来，若蒙你问，全是为疼萨拉。她是世上剩给我的骨血末了的一小口。其余的，全去了！我的妻子，我的小约瑟夫，我的兄弟马克斯，我姐姐阿加莎同她嫁的那位又好又高贵的英国人利森——全去了，全去了！」
「利森，」罗丝蒙德说，在桌下有意地紧攥丈夫的手。「你侄女叫萨拉·利森？」
约瑟夫舅舅叹了口气，摇头。「一年里头，」他说，「顶害萨拉的一个日子，她改了这个姓。她嫁的那个男人——如今死了，太太——我知之甚少，知道的只有这一点：他姓贾泽夫，他把她作践了；为这个，我认定他是天下头一号的恶棍！

===Original===

Yes,” exclaimed Uncle Joseph, with the nearest approach to anger and bitterness which his nature was capable of making, and with an idea that he was using one of the strongest superlatives in the language—“Yes! if he was to come to life again at this very moment of time, I would say it of him to his face—Englishman Jazeph, you are the First Scoundrel!”
Rosamond pressed her husband’s hand for the second time. If their own convictions had not already identified Mrs. Jazeph with Sarah Leeson, the old man’s last words must have amply sufficed to assure them that both names had been borne by the same person.
“Well, then, I shall now travel backward to the time when I was here with Sarah, my niece,” resumed Uncle Joseph. “I must, if you please, speak the truth in this business, or, now that I am already backward where I want to be, I shall stick fast in my place, and get on no more for the rest of my life. Sir and good Madam, will you have the great kindness to forgive me and Sarah, my niece, if I confess that it was not to see the house that we came here and rang at the bell, and gave deal of trouble, and wasted much breath of the big majordomo’s with the scolding that we got. It was only to do one curious little thing that we came together to this place—or, no, it was all about a secret of Sarah’s, which is still as black and dark to me as the middle of the blackest and darkest night that ever was in the world—and as I nothing knew about it, except that there was no harm in it to anybody or anything, and that Sarah was determined to go, and that I could not let her go by herself; as also for the good reason that she told me she had the best right of anybody to take the letter and to hide it again, seeing that she was afraid of its being found if longer in that room she left it, which was the room where she had hidden it before—why, so it happened that I—no, that she—no, no, that I—*Ach Gott!*” cried Uncle Joseph, striking his forehead in despair, and relieving himself by an invocation in his own language. “I am lost in my own muddlement; and whereabouts the right place is, and how I am to get myself back into it, as I am a living sinner, is more than I know!”
“There is not the least need to go back on our account,” said Rosamond, forgetting all caution and self-restraint in her anxiety to restore the old man’s confidence and composure.

===Chinese===

不错，」约瑟夫舅舅嚷道，拿出他天性里所能有的最近于恼怒、最狠的一副声气，自以为用的是这一国语言里顶狠的一个头名——「不错！他这一刻死而复生，我也要当面说他——英国人贾泽夫，你是天下头一号的恶棍！」
罗丝蒙德第二次攥紧丈夫的手。若不是他们自己的信念早把贾泽夫太太同萨拉·利森合在一个人身上，老人末了这几句话，也尽够教他们拿稳：两个名字同归一身。
「好，那么，如今我往回走，走到我同我侄女萨拉在这里的那一天，」约瑟夫舅舅接着说。「这桩事里，我若蒙你允许，必得说实话；不然，我既然已经退到我要退的地方，我便会钉死在原地，往后一辈子再挪不动一步。先生，好太太，我招认一件事，求你们大发慈悲饶了我同我侄女萨拉：我们到这里来按铃，给大管家添那些麻烦、费他那些唾沫挨那些骂，不是为了看房子。我们来这里，只为办一桩古怪的小事——不，全是为萨拉的一桩秘密，那秘密在我，还同世上顶黑顶黑的夜的黑心一样黑，一样暗——我除了知道它于谁、于什么都不含恶意，知道萨拉打定了主意要去，知道我不能放她独自去；还有一层顶好的缘故：她告诉我，她顶有权利去拿那封信、去重新藏起它，因为那间屋子——搁得久了她怕叫人找着——正是她从前藏它的那间——于是，事情便成了这样：是我——不，是她——不，不，是我——*我的天哪！*」约瑟夫舅舅嚷道，绝望地拍着额头，用家乡话喊了一声，松一松心里的疙瘩。「我埋在我自己搅的糊涂里了；对的地位在哪里，我又怎么爬回去，我是个活着的罪人，全然不知！」
「为我们，你全不必往回退，」罗丝蒙德说，一心只忙着安老人的心、保他的从容，把谨慎、把自制全忘了。

===Original===

“Pray don’t try to repeat your explanations. We know already—”
“We will suppose,” said Leonard, interposing abruptly before his wife could add another word, “that we know already everything you can desire to tell us in relation to your niece’s secret, and to your motives for desiring to see the house.”
“You will suppose that!” exclaimed Uncle Joseph, looking greatly relieved. “Ah! thank you, Sir, and you, good Madam, a thousand times for helping me out of my own muddlement with a ‘Suppose.’ I am all over confusion from my tops to my toes; but I can go on now, I think, and lose myself no more. So! Let us say it in this way: I and Sarah, my niece, are *in* the house—that is the first ‘Suppose.’ I and Sarah, my niece, are *out* of the house—that is the second ‘Suppose.’ Good! now we go on once more. On my way back to my own home at Truro, I am frightened for Sarah, because of the faint she fell into on your stairs here, and because of a look in her face that it makes me heavy at my heart to see. Also, I am sorry for her sake, because she has not done that one curious little thing which she came into the house to do. I fret about these same matters, but I console myself too; and my comfort is that Sarah will stop with me in my house at Truro, and that I shall make her happy and well again, as soon as we are settled in our life together. Judge, then, Sir, what a blow falls on me when I hear that she will not make her home where I make mine. Judge you, also, good Madam, what my surprise must be, when I ask for her reason, and she tells me she must leave Uncle Joseph, because she is afraid of being found out by *you*.” He stopped, and looking anxiously at Rosamond’s face, saw it sadden and turn away from him after he had spoken his last words. “Are you sorry, Madam, for Sarah, my niece? do you pity her?” he asked, with a little hesitation and trembling in his voice.
“I pity her with my whole heart,” said Rosamond, warmly.
“And with my whole heart, for that pity I thank you!” rejoined Uncle Joseph. “Ah, Madam, your kindness gives me the courage to go on, and to tell you that we parted from each other on the day of our getting back to Truro! When she came to see me this time, it was years and years, long and lonely and very many, since we two had met. I was afraid that many more would pass again, and I tried to make her stop with me to the very last.

===Chinese===

「求你别再费神重讲你的解释。我们已经知道——」
「我们只当，」伦纳德插进来，不等妻子再添一个字，便斩截地说，「凡是关于你侄女的秘密、关于你想看这宅子的缘由，你愿意告诉我们的，我们已经全知道了。」
「你们只当知道了！」约瑟夫舅舅嚷道，一脸大大的宽慰。「啊！谢谢你，先生，也谢谢你，好太太，一千遍一万遍，拿一个『只当』救我出我自己搅的糊涂。我从头顶到脚底全是乱麻；可如今我想我能往下讲了，再不会迷路。这样！让我们这样说：我和我侄女萨拉*在*宅子里——这是头一个『只当』。我和我侄女萨拉*出了*宅子——这是第二个『只当』。好！如今再往下走。我回特鲁罗自己家里的路上，我为萨拉悬心：为她在这里你们楼梯上跌的那一晕，为她脸上的那点神气——我一看见，心就往下沉。再者，我也替她惋惜：她进宅要办的那桩古怪小事，没有办成。这两桩事我愁；可我也自宽自解；我的安慰是：萨拉会同我在特鲁罗家里住下，等我们安下同过的日子，我便叫她又快活、又康健。那么先生，你判断判断，随后听见她说：她不肯在我安家的地方安家，这一记打在我头上，多重！好太太，你也判断判断：我问她缘故，她告诉我，她必得离开约瑟夫舅舅，因为她怕叫*你们*寻出来、盘问出来——我这惊诧该有多大！」他停住，不安地望罗丝蒙德的脸，见那脸在他末了的话音落处黯下去，别开了。「太太，你为我侄女萨拉难过么？你怜惜她么？」他问，声气里带一点迟疑，一点发抖。
「我全心全意怜惜她，」罗丝蒙德热烈地说。

===Original===

But she had still the same fear to drive her away—the fear of being found and put to the question by you. So, with the tears in her eyes (and in mine), and the grief at her heart (and at mine), she went away to hide herself in the empty bigness of the great city, London, which swallows up all people and all things that pour into it, and which has now swallowed up Sarah, my niece, with the rest. ‘My child, you will write sometimes to Uncle Joseph,’ I said, and she answered me, ‘I will write often.’ It is three weeks now since that time, and here, on my knee, are four letters she has written to me. I shall ask your leave to put them down open before you, because they will help me to get on further yet with what I must say, and because I see in your face, Madam, that you are indeed sorry for Sarah, my niece, from your heart.”
He untied the packet of letters, opened them, kissed them one by one, and put them down in a row on the table, smoothing them out carefully with his hand, and taking great pains to arrange them all in a perfectly straight line. A glance at the first of the little series showed Rosamond that the handwriting in it was the same as the handwriting in the body of the letter which had been found in the Myrtle Room.
“There is not much to read,” said Uncle Joseph. “But if you will look through them first, Madam, I can tell you after all the reason for showing them that I have.”
The old man was right. There was very little to read in the letters, and they grew progressively shorter as they became more recent in date. All four were written in the formal, conventionally correct style of a person taking up the pen with a fear of making mistakes in spelling and grammar, and were equally destitute of any personal particulars relative to the writer; all four anxiously entreated that Uncle Joseph would not be uneasy, inquired after his health, and expressed gratitude and love for him as warmly as their timid restraints of style would permit; all four contained these two questions relating to Rosamond—First, had Mrs. Frankland arrived yet at Porthgenna Tower? Second, if she had arrived, what had Uncle Joseph heard about her? And, finally, all four gave the same instructions for addressing an answer—“Please direct to me, ‘S. J.

===Chinese===

「我全心全意怜惜她，还嫌不够！为这一份怜惜，我谢你！」约瑟夫舅舅答。「啊，太太，你的慈悲给了我胆量，容我往下讲：我们回到特鲁罗那天，就彼此分了手！她这一回来看我，离我们上回相见，一年一年，又长又孤单，数不清的年头。我怕往后又是一年一年白过，便千方百计留她，留到底。可她心里还是那一份怕，赶着她走——怕叫你们寻出来、盘问出来。于是，她眼里噙着泪（我眼里也噙着），心里怀着愁（我心里也怀着），走了，把自己藏进那座空空洞洞的大城伦敦里去——那座城吞得下一切涌进去的人、一切涌进去的东西，如今把我侄女萨拉同其余的一齐吞了。「我的孩子，你有时要给约瑟夫舅舅写信，」我说；她答我：「我要常写。」这话到如今三个星期了，这里，在我膝上，是她写给我的四封信。我求你们允许，把它们拆开摊在你们面前，因为它们帮我往下说我要说的话；还因为我在你脸上看见，太太，你当真打心底里怜惜我侄女萨拉。」
他解开那一小包信，拆开，一封一封亲过，在桌上摆成一行，用手细细抚平，费尽心思把它们摆成一条笔直的线。罗丝蒙德朝这一小串的第一封一瞥，看出里头的笔迹，同桃金娘房间找出的那封信正文的笔迹，是同一支手。
「可读的不多，」约瑟夫舅舅说。「不过太太，你先把它们通看一遍，我随后再告诉你我叫你看它们的缘故。」
老人说得不差。信里可读的实在少，而且越近期的越短。四封全是拘拘谨谨、循规蹈矩的笔墨，一个人提笔怕写错了字、坏了文法的那一路；写的人自身的细情，一概不见；四封全都急急恳求约瑟夫舅舅千万别不安，问他的安，把感激和疼爱热热地写给他，笔墨的胆怯拘束容到多少便给多少；四封全都问着关于罗丝蒙德的两句话——第一句：弗兰克兰太太到波思根纳塔楼了没有？第二句：到了的话，约瑟夫舅舅听见她什么没有？末了，四封给的回信地址一模一样——「请写『寄给我：「S. J.」

===Original===

, Post-office, Smith Street, London’ ”—followed by the same apology, “Excuse my not giving my address, in case of accidents; for even in London I am still afraid of being followed and found out. I send every morning for letters; so I am sure to get your answer.”
“I told you, Madam,” said the old man, when Rosamond raised her head from the letters, “that I was frightened and sorry for Sarah when she left me. Now see, if you please, why I got more frightened and more sorry yet, when I have all the four letters that she writes to me. They begin here, with the first, at my left hand; and they grow shorter, and shorter, and shorter, as they get nearer to my right, till the last is but eight little lines. Again, see, if you please. The writing of the first letter, here, at my left hand, is very fine—I mean it is very fine to me, because I love Sarah, and because I write very badly myself; but it is not so good in the second letter—it shakes a little, it blots a little, it crooks itself a little in the last lines. In the third it is worse—more shake, more blot, more crook. In the fourth, where there is least to do, there is still more shake, still more blot, still more crook, than in all the other three put together. I see this; I remember that she was weak and worn and weary when she left me, and I say to myself, ‘She is ill, though she will not tell it, for the writing betrays her!’ ”
Rosamond looked down again at the letters, and followed the significant changes for the worse in the handwriting, line by line, as the old man pointed them out.
“I say to myself that,” he continued; “I wait, and think a little; and I hear my own heart whisper to me, ‘Go you, Uncle Joseph, to London, and, while there is yet time, bring her back to be cured and comforted and made happy in your own home!’ After that I wait, and think a little again—not about leaving my business; I would leave it forever sooner than Sarah should come to harm—but about what I am to do to get her to come back. That thought makes me look at the letters again; the letters show me always the same questions about Mistress Frankland; I see it plainly as my own hand before me that I shall never get Sarah, my niece, back, unless I can make easy her mind about those questions of Mistress Frankland’s that she dreads as if there was death to her in every one of them. I see it!

===Chinese===

，伦敦史密斯街邮局』」——底下又是同一句道歉：「恕我不写住址，防着出事；便在伦敦，我还是怕人跟着、找着。我每天早上叫人去取信；你的回信我一定收得到。」
「我告诉过你，太太，」罗丝蒙德从信上抬起头来，老人说。「萨拉走的时候，我害怕，我难过。如今请你看一看：她写给我的这四封信都到齐了，我为什么越发害怕、越发难过。信打我的左手这里起，头一封起头；往我的右手去，一封比一封短，短，短，短到末一封，不过小小的八行。再看，劳驾。左手头一封信的字，极清秀——我是说，在我眼里极清秀，因为我疼萨拉，又因为我自己写得极坏；第二封便差些——微微地抖，微微地污，末几行微微地歪。第三封更坏——更抖，更污，更歪。第四封，要写的最少，抖、污、歪，却比前头三封加在一起还多。我看见了；我记得她走的时候又弱又憔悴又困乏，我便对自己说：『她病着，只是不肯说，字迹把她招出来了！』」
罗丝蒙德又低头看那些信，照老人指点的，一行一行追那笔迹里要紧的越来越坏的变故。
「我便这样对自己说，」他接着说；「我等着，略想一想；听见自己的心对我低语：『你去吧，约瑟夫舅舅，上伦敦去，趁着还来得及，把她带回来，在你自己家里调养她、安慰她、叫她快活！』随后我又等，又略想一想——不是想丢买卖；萨拉若有闪失，我永远丢掉也甘心——想的是：我怎么办，才叫她肯回来。这个念头教我再去看那些信；信里始终是问弗兰克兰太太的那几句话；我明明看见，如同看见我眼前的这只手：除非我把萨拉为弗兰克兰太太那几句话悬着的心放宽——她怕那几句话，怕得字字都含着死——我永远不能叫我侄女萨拉回来。我看见了！

===Original===

it makes my pipe go out; it drives me up from my chair; it puts my hat on my head; it brings me here, where I have once intruded myself already, and where I have no right, I know, to intrude myself again; it makes me beg and pray now, of your compassion for my niece and of your goodness for me, that you will not deny me the means of bringing Sarah back. If I may only say to her, I have seen Mistress Frankland, and she has told me with her own lips that she will ask none of those questions that you fear so much—if I may only say that, Sarah will come back with me, and I shall thank you every day of my life for making me a happy man!”
The simple eloquence of his words, the innocent earnestness of his manner, touched Rosamond to the heart. “I will do anything, I will promise anything,” she answered eagerly, “to help you to bring her back! If she will only let me see her, I promise not to say one word that she would not wish me to say; I promise not to ask one question—no, not one—that it will pain her to answer. Oh, what comforting message can I send besides? what can I say—?” She stopped confusedly, feeling her husband’s foot touching hers again.
“Ah, say no more! say no more!” cried Uncle Joseph, tying up his little packet of letters, with his eyes sparkling and his ruddy face all in a glow. “Enough said to bring Sarah back! enough said to make me grateful for all my life! Oh, I am so happy, so happy, so happy—my skin is too small to hold me!” He tossed up the packet of letters into the air, caught it, kissed it, and put it back again in his pocket, all in an instant.
“You are not going?” said Rosamond. “Surely you are not going yet?”
“It is my loss to go away from here, which I must put up with, because it is also my gain to get sooner to Sarah,” replied Uncle Joseph. “For that reason only, I shall ask your pardon if I take my leave with my heart full of thanks, and go my ways home again.”
“When do you propose to start for London, Mr. Buschmann?” inquired Leonard.
“Tomorrow, in the morning early, Sir,” replied Uncle Joseph. “I shall finish the work that I must do tonight, and shall leave the rest to Samuel (who is my very good friend, and my shopman too), and shall then go to Sarah by the first coach.”
“May I ask for your niece’s address in London, in case we wish to write to you?”

===Chinese===

这一个看见，叫我的烟斗灭了火；把我从椅子上拔起来；把帽子给我扣上头；把我带到这里——我先前已经冒昧来过一趟的地方，我明知道没有权利再来冒昧的地方；叫我如今向你二位哀告，求你们疼我侄女的心、待我好的心，别拒绝我领萨拉回去的门路。我只消能对她说：我见过弗兰克兰太太，她亲口告诉我，那些你怕得要命的话，她一句也不问——我只消能说这一句，萨拉便同我回来了；叫我做了一世快活人的这份恩，我一天谢你们一天！」
他这番话里朴朴实实的雄辩，他那份天真无邪的恳切，把罗丝蒙德的心都感动了。「我什么都肯做，什么都肯应承，」她急急地答，「帮你把她带回来！她只要肯见我一面，我应承不说一个她不愿意我说的一个字；我应承不问一句——不，一句也不问——她答起来心痛的话。啊，此外我还能捎什么宽心的话呢？我还能说什么——」她慌慌地停住，觉出丈夫的脚又碰了碰她的脚。
「啊，不必说了！不必说了！」约瑟夫舅舅嚷道，一面捆他那一小包信，眼里放光，红脸涨得通红。「说这些，尽够把萨拉领回来！尽够叫我感激一辈子！啊，我快活，快活，快活——我这张皮都包不住我了！」他把那一包信往空里一抛，接住，亲了亲，霎时塞回口袋。
「你要走了？」罗丝蒙德说。「你不会这样快就走吧？」
「离了这里是我的损失，我只好受着，因为早点到萨拉身边也是我的得着，」约瑟夫舅舅答。「单为这个缘故，我心头装满谢意告辞，走回我家去，还要求你们恕罪。」
「你打算几时动身去伦敦，布什曼先生？」伦纳德问。
「明天一大早，先生，」约瑟夫舅舅答。「今晚我必得做的活做完，余下的交给塞缪尔——他是我的极好的朋友，也是我铺子里的伙计——随后赶头班马车去萨拉那里。」
「你的侄女在伦敦的住址，可否见告？万一我们要写信给你们。」

===Original===

“She gives me no address, Sir, but the post-office; for even at the great distance of London, the same fear that she had all the way from this house still sticks to her. But here is the place where I shall get my own bed,” continued the old man, producing a small shop card. “It is the house of a countryman of my own, a fine baker of buns, Sir, and a very good man indeed.”
“Have you thought of any plan for finding out your niece’s address?” inquired Rosamond, copying the direction on the card while she spoke.
“Ah, yes—for I am always quick at making my plans,” said Uncle Joseph. “I shall present myself to the master of the post, and to him I shall say just this and no more—‘Good morning, Sir. I am the man who writes the letters to S. J. She is my niece, if you please; and all that I want to know is—Where does she live?’ There is something like a plan, I think? Aha!” He spread out both his hands interrogatively, and looked at Mrs. Frankland with a self-satisfied smile.
“I am afraid,” said Rosamond, partly amused, partly touched by his simplicity, “that the people at the post-office are not at all likely to be trusted with the address. I think you would do better to take a letter with you, directed to ‘S. J.’; to deliver it in the morning when letters are received from the country; to wait near the door, and then to follow the person who is sent by your niece (as she tells you herself) to ask for letters for S. J.”
“You think that is better?” said Uncle Joseph, secretly convinced that his own idea was unquestionably the most ingenious of the two. “Good! The least little word that you say to me, Madam, is a command that I follow with all my heart.” He took the crumpled felt hat out of his pocket, and advanced to say farewell, when Mr. Frankland spoke to him again.
“If you find your niece well, and willing to travel,” said Leonard, “you will bring her back to Truro at once? And you will let us know when you are both at home again?”
“At once, Sir,” said Uncle Joseph. “To both these questions, I say, At once.”
“If a week from this time passes,” continued Leonard, “and we hear nothing from you, we must conclude, then, either that some unforeseen obstacle stands in the way of your return, or that your fears on your niece’s account have been but too well-founded, and that she is not able to travel?”
“Yes, Sir; so let it be.

===Chinese===

「她不给我住址，先生，只给邮局；便在伦敦那样远的地方，她从这宅子起一路揣着的那份怕，还黏着她。不过这是我安床位的地方，」老人接着说，拿出一张小小的铺子名片。「是我同乡的宅子，一位烤甜面包的妙人，先生，一个实实在在的好人。」
「寻你侄女住址的章程，想好了没有？」罗丝蒙德问，一面说，一面照名片抄地址。
「啊，有了——因为我出章程向来快，」约瑟夫舅舅说。「我去见邮局长，对他说，不多不少，只这一句——『早安，先生。我就是写给S. J.的那些信的人。她是我侄女，你若不嫌；我要知道的只有一句——她住在哪里？』这总像个章程了吧，我想？啊哈！」他两手一摊，一副请君裁夺的样子，望住弗兰克兰太太，得意地笑。
「我怕，」罗丝蒙德说，一半觉得有趣，一半叫他的天真感动，「邮局的人靠不住，未必肯把住址告诉他。我想你顶好带一封信去，写明寄『S. J.』；趁早晨收乡来信的时辰投进去；守在门边，随后跟着你侄女打发来取信的那个人走——她信里自己说的，打发人来取S. J.的信。」
「你道这个好？」约瑟夫舅舅说，心里暗暗拿定：他的主意明明比这个巧。「妙！太太，你赏我的字，再小也是吩咐，我全心照办。」他从兜里掏出那顶皱毡帽，上前告别；弗兰克兰先生又同他说起话来。
「你找着侄女，她身子好，肯上路，」伦纳德说，「你便立刻带她回特鲁罗？你们两个到了家，也给我们个信？」
「立刻，先生，」约瑟夫舅舅说。「这两问，我都答：立刻。」
「从今天起过一个星期，」伦纳德接着说，「我们听不见你的音信，那么我们只好断定：或者是料想不到的阻碍挡住你的归程，或者是你为你侄女担的那份心果然应验，她上不了路？」
「是，先生；就这样办。

===Original===

But I hope you will hear from me before the week is out.”
“Oh, so do I! most earnestly, most anxiously!” said Rosamond. “You remember my message?”
“I have got it here, every word of it,” said Uncle Joseph, touching his heart. He raised the hand which Rosamond held out to him to his lips. “I shall try to thank you better when I have come back,” he said. “For all your kindness to me and to my niece, God bless you both, and keep you happy, till we meet again.” With these words, he hastened to the door, waved his hand gayly, with the old crumpled hat in it, and went out.
“Dear, simple, warmhearted old man!” said Rosamond, as the door closed. “I wanted to tell him everything, Lenny. Why did you stop me?”
“My love, it is that very simplicity which you admire, and which I admire, too, that makes me cautious. At the first sound of his voice I felt as warmly toward him as you do; but the more I heard him talk the more convinced I became that it would be rash to trust him, at first, for fear of his disclosing too abruptly to your mother that we know her secret. Our chance of winning her confidence and obtaining an interview with her depends, I can see, upon our own tact in dealing with her exaggerated suspicions and her nervous fears. That good old man, with the best and kindest intentions in the world, might ruin everything. He will have done all that we can hope for, and all that we can wish, if he only succeeds in bringing her back to Truro.”
“But if he fails?—if anything happens?—if she is really ill?”
“Let us wait till the week is over, Rosamond. It will be time enough then to decide what we shall do next.”

===Chinese===

不过我盼着等不到一星期，你们就有我的信。」
「啊，我也盼！最恳切地盼，最悬心地盼！」罗丝蒙德说。「我托你的话，还记得么？」
「都在这里，一个字不缺，」约瑟夫舅舅说，手按着心口。他把罗丝蒙德伸出的手举到唇边。「等我回来，再好好谢你们，」他说。「你们待我、待我侄女的一切恩情，愿上帝祝福你们两位，保你们快活，直到我们再见。」说罢，他急急奔到门口，手里挥着那顶旧毡帽，欢欢喜喜一扬，出门去了。
「又亲、又天真、又热心的老人家！」门一关上，罗丝蒙德说。「我想把样样都告诉他，伦尼。你为什么拦我？」
「我的爱，恰恰是你欣赏的那份天真——我也欣赏——教我谨慎。他一开口，我对他的热心便同你一样；可他越往下讲，我越拿定：起初便信托他，太轻率，怕他把我们知道她秘密的事，太直露地捅到你母亲耳朵里。我看得出，我们要赢她的信，求她一见，全凭我们应付她那过分的疑心和神经过敏的怕时的手腕。那位好老人，存着世上顶好顶慈悲的心，可能把一切全毁了。他只消把她带回特鲁罗，便把我们指望的、心里盼的，全办到了。」
「可他若办不成呢？——倘若出了事呢？——倘若她当真病着呢？」
「罗丝蒙德，等一个星期过完再说。那时候再定下一步怎么办，才来得及。」

## II Waiting and Hoping / 二 守候与盼望
===Original===

The week of expectation passed, and no tidings from Uncle Joseph reached Porthgenna Tower.
On the eighth day Mr. Frankland sent a messenger to Truro, with orders to find out the cabinetmaker’s shop kept by Mr. Buschmann, and to inquire of the person left in charge there whether he had received any news from his master. The messenger returned in the afternoon, and brought word that Mr. Buschmann had written one short note to his shopman since his departure, announcing that he had arrived safely toward nightfall in London; that he had met with a hospitable welcome from his countryman, the German baker; and that he had discovered his niece’s address, but had been prevented from seeing her by an obstacle which he hoped would be removed at his next visit. Since the delivery of that note, no further communication had been received from him, and nothing therefore was known of the period at which he might be expected to return.
The one fragment of intelligence thus obtained was not of a nature to relieve the depression of spirits which the doubt and suspense of the past week had produced in Mrs. Frankland. Her husband endeavored to combat the oppression of mind from which she was suffering, by reminding her that the ominous silence of Uncle Joseph might be just as probably occasioned by his niece’s unwillingness as by her inability to return with him to Truro. Remembering the obstacle at which the old man’s letter hinted, and taking also into consideration her excessive sensitiveness and her unreasoning timidity, he declared it to be quite possible that Mrs. Frankland’s message, instead of reassuring her, might only inspire her with fresh apprehensions, and might consequently strengthen her resolution to keep herself out of reach of all communications from Porthgenna Tower.
Rosamond listened patiently while this view of the case was placed before her, and acknowledged that the reasonableness of it was beyond dispute; but her readiness in admitting that her husband might be right and that she might be wrong was accompanied by no change for the better in the condition of her spirits. The interpretation which, the old man had placed upon the alteration for the worse in Mrs. Jazeph’s handwriting had produced a vivid impression on her mind, which had been strengthened by her own recollection of her mother’s pale, worn face when they met as strangers at West Winston.

===Chinese===

翘望的那个星期过去了，约瑟夫舅舅的音信一点没有到波思根纳塔楼。
第八天，弗兰克兰先生打发一个差人去特鲁罗，吩咐他寻出布什曼先生开的那家细木匠铺子，问一问留守铺子的人，可曾得着东家的消息。差人下午回来，带回来的话是：布什曼先生动身之后，给铺里的伙计写过一张短短的字条，报说他安安稳稳，天黑前后到了伦敦；报说他的同乡、那位德国烤面包的，待他殷殷勤勤；报说他已经探出侄女的住址，可有一样拦路的东西，教他见不着她——那东西他指望下一回登门便除得掉。那张字条送到之后，他再无音信；他几时回得来，也就无从知道。
这样零零星星得来的一丁点消息，救不了弗兰克兰太太的心——过去一个星期的疑惑悬望，把她的精神压得沉沉的。丈夫同她心头的郁结相搏，劝她说：约瑟夫舅舅那片不祥的沉默，兴许恰恰是侄女不肯跟他回特鲁罗闹出来的，同她回不去，一样地讲得通。老人信里暗示的那道拦路的东西，又参上她那份过分的敏感、那点不讲道理的怯惧，他宣言：弗兰克兰太太捎的那番话，不但安不了她的心，兴许反倒添她的疑，叫她越发拿定主意，把自己藏到波思根纳塔楼的音信够不着的地方去。

===Original===

Reason, therefore, as convincingly as he might, Mr. Frankland was unable to shake his wife’s conviction that the obstacle mentioned in Uncle Joseph’s letter, and the silence which he had maintained since, were referable alike to the illness of his niece.
The return of the messenger from Truro suggested, besides this topic of discussion, another question of much greater importance. After having waited one day beyond the week that had been appointed, what was the proper course of action for Mr. and Mrs. Frankland now to adopt, in the absence of any information from London or from Truro to decide their future proceedings?
Leonard’s first idea was to write immediately to Uncle Joseph, at the address which he had given on the occasion of his visit to Porthgenna Tower. When this project was communicated to Rosamond, she opposed it, on the ground that the necessary delay before the answer to the letter could arrive would involve a serious waste of time, when it might, for aught they knew to the contrary, be of the last importance to them not to risk the loss of a single day. If illness prevented Mrs. Jazeph from traveling, it would be necessary to see her at once, because that illness might increase. If she were only suspicious of their motives, it was equally important to open personal communications with her before she could find an opportunity of raising some fresh obstacle, and of concealing herself again in some place of refuge which Uncle Joseph himself might not be able to trace.
The truth of these conclusions was obvious, but Leonard hesitated to adopt them, because they involved the necessity of a journey to London. If he went there without his wife, his blindness placed him at the mercy of strangers and servants, in conducting investigations of the most delicate and most private nature. If Rosamond accompanied him, it would be necessary to risk all kinds of delays and inconveniences by taking the child with them on a long and wearisome journey of more than two hundred and fifty miles.
Rosamond met both these difficulties with her usual directness and decision. The idea of her husband traveling anywhere, under any circumstances, in his helpless, dependent state, without having her to attend on him, she dismissed at once as too preposterous for consideration.

===Chinese===

所以，弗兰克兰先生道理讲得再透，也摇不动妻子的那个信念：约瑟夫舅舅信里提的拦路的东西，同他往后的沉默，一概算在他侄女病着的一笔账上。
差人从特鲁罗回来，除了这个话头，又勾起一桩更要紧的问题。比约好的期限多等了一天，伦敦、特鲁罗两边都没有音信来定他们下一步，弗兰克兰夫妇如今该走的路，究竟是哪一条？
伦纳德头一个念头，是照约瑟夫舅舅登波思根纳塔楼时留下的地址，立刻写信给他。这个章程说给罗丝蒙德听，她反对，理由是：等回信来的耽搁，白白糟蹋工夫——说不定，他们那样一天也丢不起，偏偏一天也丢不起。贾泽夫太太若病得起不了身，便须立刻见她，因为病会重。她若不过疑心他们的来意，也同等地要紧，要趁她还没寻着机会再立起新的障碍、再躲进连约瑟夫舅舅自己也未必寻得出的避难的去处，先同她当面把话打开。
这些结论明明白白站得住，伦纳德却踌躇不肯照办，因为办它们，必要跑一趟伦敦。他若撇下妻子独自去，他那一双瞎眼，查访这样细、这样隐秘的事，便全得听陌生人和仆人摆布。罗丝蒙德若陪着去，那孩子也必要带上——二百五十多英里长、累坏人的路，各样的耽搁各样不便，只好一概冒险。
罗丝蒙德照她素来直截、素来果断的性子，把两道难关一齐接住。丈夫那样无助、样样靠人的身子，无论到哪、无论什么情形，身边没有她伺候，这个主意她当场打掉，荒唐得不值一驳。

===Original===

The second objection, of subjecting the child to the chances and fatigues of a long journey, she met by proposing that they should travel to Exeter at their own time and in their own conveyance, and that they should afterward insure plenty of comfort and plenty of room by taking a carriage to themselves when they reached the railroad at Exeter. After thus smoothing away the difficulties which seemed to set themselves in opposition to the journey, she again reverted to the absolute necessity of undertaking it. She reminded Leonard of the serious interest that they both had in immediately obtaining Mrs. Jazeph’s testimony to the genuineness of the letter which had been found in the Myrtle Room, as well as in ascertaining all the details of the extraordinary fraud which had been practiced by Mrs. Treverton on her husband. She pleaded also her own natural anxiety to make all the atonement in her power for the pain she must have unconsciously inflicted, in the bedroom at West Winston, on the person of all others whose failings and sorrows she was most bound to respect; and having thus stated the motives which urged her husband and herself to lose no time in communicating personally with Mrs. Jazeph, she again drew the inevitable conclusion that there was no alternative, in the position in which they were now placed, but to start forthwith on the journey to London.
A little further consideration satisfied Leonard that the emergency was of such a nature as to render all attempts to meet it by half-measures impossible. He felt that his own convictions agreed with his wife’s; and he resolved accordingly to act at once, without further indecision or further delay. Before the evening was over, the servants at Porthgenna were amazed by receiving directions to pack the trunks for traveling, and to order horses at the post-town for an early hour the next morning.
On the first day of the journey, the travelers started as soon as the carriage was ready, rested on the road toward noon, and remained for the night at Liskeard. On the second day they arrived at Exeter, and slept there. On the third day they reached London by the railway, between six and seven o’clock in the evening.

===Chinese===

第二道——叫孩子去冒长途的劳顿和风险——她另出一条计策挡了回去：先照自己的钟点、坐自备的车到埃克塞特；到了埃克塞特上了铁路，再雇一节专车，宽敞舒适，两样都保。这样把挡路的难关一一扫平，她又回到那趟路非走不可的正题。她提醒伦纳德：桃金娘房间找出的那封信，要立刻拿到贾泽夫太太的口供验它是真；特雷弗顿夫人对她丈夫做下的那桩奇异的欺瞒，细情也要一件一件问明——这两桩，他们两个利害都大。她又求告：西温斯顿卧房里，她不知不觉给了那个人疼痛，而天下人的短处和忧苦，她顶该敬惜的偏偏是她——她巴不得尽力把这份亏补全。这样把她催丈夫和她自己一刻不可迟延、要同贾泽夫太太当面相见的种种缘故摆出来，她重新落下那个躲不开的结论：照他们眼下的境地，除了即刻动身去伦敦，别无第二条路。
伦纳德再一想，也就心服：眼前的急事，遮遮掩掩的半吊子办法救不了它，一试也不必试。他觉得自己的信念同妻子的一致，便决意立刻照办，不再三心，不再拖延。天还没黑，波思根纳的仆人们叫吩咐收旅行箱、叫到邮镇定一早的马车，惊得目瞪口呆。
上路头一天，车一备好他们便动身，晌午前后在道上歇了歇，夜里宿在莱斯卡德。第二天到埃克塞特，宿下。第三天傍晚上下六七点钟，坐火车到了伦敦。

===Original===

When they were comfortably settled for the night at their hotel, and when an hour’s rest and quiet had enabled them to recover a little after the fatigues of the journey, Rosamond wrote two notes under her husband’s direction. The first was addressed to Mr. Buschmann: it simply informed him of their arrival, and of their earnest desire to see him at the hotel as early as possible the next morning, and it concluded by cautioning him to wait until he had seen them before he announced their presence in London to his niece.
The second note was addressed to the family solicitor, Mr. Nixon—the same gentleman who, more than a year since, had written, at Mrs. Frankland’s request, the letter which informed Andrew Treverton of his brother’s decease, and of the circumstances under which the captain had died. All that Rosamond now wrote, in her husband’s name and her own, to ask of Mr. Nixon, was that he would endeavor to call at their hotel on his way to business the next morning, to give his opinion on a private matter of great importance, which had obliged them to undertake the journey from Porthgenna to London. This note, and the note to Uncle Joseph, were sent to their respective addresses by a messenger on the evening when they were written.
The first visitor who arrived the next morning was the solicitor—a clearheaded, fluent, polite old gentleman, who had known Captain Treverton and his father before him. He came to the hotel fully expecting to be consulted on some difficulties connected with the Porthgenna estate, which the local agent was perhaps unable to settle, and which might be of too confused and intricate a nature to be easily expressed in writing. When he heard what the emergency really was, and when the letter that had been found in the Myrtle Room was placed in his hands, it is not too much to say that, for the first time in the course of a long life and a varied practice among all sorts and conditions of clients, sheer astonishment utterly paralyzed Mr. Nixon’s faculties, and bereft him for some moments of the power of uttering a single word.
When, however, Mr.

===Chinese===

夜里在旅馆安顿舒齐，歇了一个钟头，把路上的疲乏缓过来一点，罗丝蒙德照丈夫的意思写了两张字条。头一张写给布什曼先生：只报他们到了，报他们切切盼他明早尽早到旅馆一见，末了叮嘱他一句：见了他们，再向他侄女报他们在伦敦，一步也不可先。
第二张写给府上的律师尼克松先生——就是一年多以前，奉弗兰克兰太太之命写信给安德鲁·特雷弗顿、报他兄弟的噩耗和舰长去世情形的那位先生。罗丝蒙德如今以丈夫和自己的名义求尼克松先生的，不过一件：明早上班的道上，劳驾绕到旅馆来一趟，一桩要紧的私事，把他们从波思根纳拉到伦敦来，请他拿个主意。这两张字条，同约瑟夫舅舅那张，写成的当晚，便叫差人分头送去。
第二天早晨头一个到的客人是律师——一位头脑清爽、口齿流利、礼数周全的老绅士，特雷弗顿舰长同舰长的父亲，他都熟识。他来旅馆，满心预备着人家要向他请教波思根纳田产上的难处：本地代理人办不下来的、又乱又缠夹、信里说不清楚的那一等。等他听说急事到底是什么，等那封桃金娘房间找出的信放到他手里——尼可松先生办了一辈子差，什么样主顾没经见过，可不得不这样说：十足的惊骇把他整个人钉住了，有好几分钟，一个字也吐不出。

===Original===

Frankland proceeded from making the disclosure to announcing his resolution to give up the purchase-money of Porthgenna Tower, if the genuineness of the letter could be proved to his own satisfaction, the old lawyer recovered the use of his tongue immediately, and protested against his client’s intention with the sincere warmth of a man who thoroughly understood the advantage of being rich, and who knew what it was to gain and to lose a fortune of forty thousand pounds.
Leonard listened with patient attention while Mr. Nixon argued from his professional point of view against regarding the letter, taken by itself, as a genuine document, and against accepting Mrs. Jazeph’s evidence, taken with it, as decisive on the subject of Mrs. Frankland’s real parentage. He expatiated on the improbability of Mrs. Treverton’s alleged fraud upon her husband having been committed without other persons besides her maid and herself being in the secret. He declared it to be in accordance with all received experience of human nature that one or more of those other persons must have spoken of the secret either from malice or from want of caution, and that the consequent exposure of the truth must, in the course of so long a period as twenty-two years, have come to the knowledge of some among the many people in the West of England, as well as in London, who knew the Treverton family personally or by reputation. From this objection he passed to another, which admitted the possible genuineness of the letter as a written document; but which pleaded the probability of its having been produced under the influence of some mental delusion on Mrs. Treverton’s part, which her maid might have had an interest in humoring at the time, though she might have hesitated, after her mistress’s death, at risking the possible consequences of attempting to profit by the imposture. Having stated this theory, as one which not only explained the writing of the letter, but the hiding of it also, Mr. Nixon further observed, in reference to Mrs. Jazeph, that any evidence she might give was of little or no value in a legal point of view, from the difficulty—or, he might say, the impossibility—of satisfactorily identifying the infant mentioned in the letter with the lady whom he had now the honor of addressing as Mrs.

等到弗兰克兰先生把底细说完，跟着宣告他的决断：那封信的真若验得他自己满意，波思根纳塔楼的买价便交出来——老律师的舌头立刻回来了，奋然抗辩主顾的打算，那份热心出于至诚：发财的好处，他体察入骨；四万英镑的家私怎样挣来、怎样丢掉，他一清二楚。
尼克松先生从行家的立场辩驳，说那封信单拿它自己当真凭据使不得，说连着贾泽夫太太的口供一起算，弗兰克兰太太真正的血统，也还断不死——伦纳德耐着性子听。他铺陈说：特雷弗顿夫人那桩指名的欺瞒，若除她同使女二人之外再无旁人知情，天下没有这样便宜的事。他宣告：照人性万古不易的经验，其余知情的人里头，总有一个两个，或者怀恨，或者不小心，总要把这桩秘密漏出风声；二十二年这样长的岁月，泄漏了的真相，总该传到西英格兰——也传到伦敦——多少亲身相识、或者闻名听过特雷弗顿家的人的耳朵里。这个抗辩说完，他又转到另一个：那封信作为一纸文书，真也说得过去；可他请人们考虑，信也许是特雷弗顿夫人一种神志的迷乱底下写出来的，使女当时也许有利害所在，曲意逢迎，等主母一死，借这场假把戏捞好处会有什么下场，她便不敢冒险了。他把这套理论陈说了一番，说它不但解释了写信，也解释了藏信；尼克松先生又进一步指贾泽夫太太说：她不论作什么证，法理上几乎一文不值——因为要把信里提的那个婴儿，安安稳稳对上他此刻有幸称呼的这位弗兰克兰

===Original===

Frankland, and whom no unsubstantiated document in existence should induce him to believe to be any other than the daughter of his old friend and client, Captain Treverton.
Having heard the lawyer’s objections to the end, Leonard admitted their ingenuity, but acknowledged at the same time that they had produced no alteration in his impression on the subject of the letter, or in his convictions as to the course of duty which he felt bound to follow. He would wait, he said, for Mrs. Jazeph’s testimony before he acted decisively; but if that testimony were of such a nature, and were given in such a manner, as to satisfy him that his wife had no moral right to the fortune that she possessed, he would restore it at once to the person who had—Mr. Andrew Treverton.
Finding that no fresh arguments or suggestions could shake Mr. Frankland’s resolution, and that no separate appeal to Rosamond had the slightest effect in stimulating her to use her influence for the purpose of inducing her husband to alter his determination; and feeling convinced, moreover, from all that he heard, that Mr. Frankland would, if he was opposed by many more objections, either employ another professional adviser, or risk committing some fatal legal error by acting for himself in the matter of restoring the money, Mr. Nixon at last consented, under protest, to give his client what help he needed in case it became necessary to hold communication with Andrew Treverton. He listened with polite resignation to Leonard’s brief statement of the questions that he intended to put to Mrs. Jazeph; and said, with the slightest possible dash of sarcasm, when it came to his turn to speak, that they were excellent questions in a moral point of view, and would doubtless produce answers which would be full of interest of the most romantic kind. “But,” he added, “as you have one child already, Mr. Frankland, and as you may, perhaps, if I may venture on suggesting such a thing, have more in the course of years; and as those children, when they grow up, may hear of the loss of their mother’s fortune, and may wish to know why it was sacrificed, I should recommend—resting the matter on family grounds alone, and not going further to make a legal point of it also—that you procure from Mrs.

太太，难乎其难——或者竟不妨说，办不到；无论如何，没有确凿凭据的一纸文书，休想教他信她是他老友老主顾特雷弗顿舰长的女儿之外的任何人。
律师的抗辩听到底，伦纳德承它们的巧；可他同时招认：他对那封信的看法，他认定该走的那条本分路，一样也没有改。他说，贾泽夫太太的口供到了，他才下断然的举动；可那口供若果真叫他自己信服：妻子占着的那份家私，道义上她没有名分，他立刻把它还给有名分的那个人——安德鲁·特雷弗顿先生。
尼克松先生见新论新策都摇不动弗兰克兰先生的决断，见单对罗丝蒙德下的功夫毫无用处，劝不动她去运用她的影响教丈夫回心；又从听来的种种断定：再多几个人抗辩，弗兰克兰先生便要另请高明，或者索性自己动手去办归还的事，惹出致命的法律大错；尼克松先生到头来让了步，抗着议应承：果真到了要同安德鲁·特雷弗顿打交道的地步，主顾要什么帮助，他便给什么帮助。伦纳德把他要向贾泽夫太太盘问的几个问题简简单单说了一遍，尼克松先生恭恭敬敬、认命似的听完；轮到他开口，只淡淡地带一点讥诮说：这几问，道义上头是问得好极了，问出来的回答，自然也一定满纸奇情，浪漫得很。「可是，」他添上，「弗兰克兰先生，你已经有了一个孩子；多少年里，兴许——恕我冒昧提这样的事——还要添几个；那些孩子长大，听见母亲的家私丢了，要问为什么丢的——我只就家务的一边说，不进一步拿法律的一边说——我劝你从贾泽夫

===Original===

Jazeph, besides the vivâ voce evidence you propose to extract (against the admissibility of which, in this case, I again protest), a written declaration, which you may leave behind you at your death, and which may justify you in the eyes of your children, in case the necessity for such justification should arise at some future period.”
This advice was too plainly valuable to be neglected. At Leonard’s request, Mr. Nixon drew out at once a form of declaration, affirming the genuineness of the letter addressed by the late Mrs. Treverton on her deathbed to her husband, since also deceased, and bearing witness to the truth of the statements therein contained, both as regarded the fraud practiced on Captain Treverton and the asserted parentage of the child. Telling Mr. Frankland that he would do well to have Mrs. Jazeph’s signature to this document attested by the names of two competent witnesses, Mr. Nixon handed the declaration to Rosamond to read aloud to her husband, and, finding that no objection was made to any part of it, and that he could be of no further use in the present early stage of the proceedings, rose to take his leave. Leonard engaged to communicate with him again in the course of the day, if necessary; and he retired, reiterating his protest to the last, and declaring that he had never met with such an extraordinary case and such a self-willed client before in the whole course of his practice.
Nearly an hour elapsed after the departure of the lawyer before any second visitor was announced. At the expiration of that time, the welcome sound of footsteps was heard approaching the door, and Uncle Joseph entered the room.
Rosamond’s observation, stimulated by anxiety, detected a change in his look and manner the moment he appeared. His face was harassed and fatigued, and his gait, as he advanced into the room, had lost the briskness and activity which so quaintly distinguished it when she saw him, for the first time, at Porthgenna Tower. He tried to add to his first words of greeting an apology for being late; but Rosamond interrupted him, in her eagerness to ask the first important question.
“We know that you have discovered her address,” she said, anxiously, “but we know nothing more. Is she as you feared to find her? Is she ill?”
The old man shook his head sadly. “When I showed you her letter,” he said, “what did I tell you?

这条主意明明白白金贵，忽略不得。伦纳德一请，尼克松先生立刻拟出一篇声明的程式：认定故去的特雷弗顿夫人临终写给丈夫——亦已故去——的那封信是真的；信里所载的种种，欺瞒特雷弗顿舰长也好，指称那孩子的血统也好，一概属实，愿为此作证。尼克松先生嘱咐弗兰克兰先生：这纸文书，顶好叫贾泽夫太太签了名，再请两位够格的证人连署。他把声明交罗丝蒙德念给她丈夫听，见没有一字有异议，见眼下事情才起头，自己再无用处，便起身告辞。伦纳德应承必要时当天再同他接头；他退出去，抗议到底，一口咬定：他挂牌以来，这样离奇的案子、这样固执的主顾，他从未遇过。
律师走后，差不多一个钟头，才报进来第二个客人的名。到那个时候，门边才听见可喜的脚步声，约瑟夫舅舅进了屋。
罗丝蒙德的眼睛叫悬心磨快了，他一露面，便看出他的脸色举止变了。他的脸又愁又乏；走进屋来的步态，失了那份利落活泼——头一回在波思根纳塔楼见着他的时候，那份古怪的利落活泼多逗人。他想在开头的道贺话里搭上一句迟到的抱歉；罗丝蒙德急着问头一件要紧的事，把他拦住了。
「我们知道你寻着她的住址了，」她急急地说，「可再多的我们不知道。她照你怕的那个样子么？她病了么？」
老人凄凄地摇头。「我给你看她的信的时候，」他说，「我说过什么来着？

===Original===

She is so ill, Madam, that not even the message your kindness gave to me will do her any good.”
Those few simple words struck Rosamond’s heart with a strange fear, which silenced her against her own will when she tried to speak again. Uncle Joseph understood the anxious look she fixed on him, and the quick sign she made toward the chair standing nearest to the sofa on which she and her husband were sitting. There he took his place, and there he confided to them all that he had to tell.
He had followed, he said, the advice which Rosamond had given to him at Porthgenna, by taking a letter addressed to “S. J.” to the post-office the morning after his arrival in London. The messenger—a maidservant—had called to inquire, as was anticipated, and had left the post-office with his letter in her hand. He had followed her to a lodging-house in a street near, had seen her let herself in at the door, and had then knocked and inquired for Mrs. Jazeph. The door was answered by an old woman, who looked like the landlady; and the reply was that no one of that name lived there. He had then explained that he wished to see the person for whom letters were sent to the neighboring post-office, addressed to “S. J.”; but the old woman had answered, in the surliest way, that they had nothing to do with anonymous people or their friends in that house, and had closed the door in his face. Upon this he had gone back to his friend, the German baker, to get advice; and had been recommended to return, after allowing some little time to elapse, to ask if he could see the servant who waited on the lodgers, to describe his niece’s appearance, and to put half a crown into the girl’s hand to help her to understand what he wanted. He had followed these directions, and had discovered that his niece was lying ill in the house, under the assumed name of “Mrs. James.” A little persuasion (after the present of the half-crown) had induced the girl to go upstairs and announce his name. After that there were no more obstacles to be overcome, and he was conducted immediately to the room occupied by his niece.
He was inexpressibly shocked and startled when he saw her by the violent nervous agitation which she manifested as he approached her bedside. But he did not lose heart and hope until he had communicated Mrs.

===Chinese===

她病得那样，太太，你的慈悲教我捎的那番话，于她半点好处也没有。」
寥寥几个字，打在罗丝蒙德心上，起了说不出的惊惶，她要再开口，自家的话自家管不住。约瑟夫舅舅懂得她钉在他脸上那副着急的眼色，懂得她朝沙发——她同丈夫坐着的那张——近旁那把椅子打的那个急急的手势。他在那里坐了，在那里把他要讲的一切和盘托出。
他说，他照罗丝蒙德在波思根纳出的主意办了：到伦敦的第二天早晨，拿一封写明寄「S. J.」的信送到邮局去。打发来取信的是个使女——不出所料——手里拿着他的信出了邮局。他跟着她到左近街上一家寓所，看见她开门进去，随后上去敲门，问贾泽夫太太。开门的是个老妇人，像房东娘；回话说，那个姓的没有。他又说明：他要见的是往左近邮局寄信、写明「S. J.」的那一位；老妇人恶声恶气答了一句：那宅子里，无名无姓的人同他们的朋友，一概不伺候，当面把门关了。于是他回到他朋友、那德国烤面包的那里讨主意；得了指点：隔开一点工夫再回去，求见伺候房客的使女，把侄女的样子描给她听，往姑娘手里放半个克朗，教她明白他的意思。他依着指点办了，探出侄女果真病在这宅子里，顶着「詹姆斯太太」的假名字。劝了一小阵（半个克朗的礼之后），那姑娘肯上楼去通名。此后再没有拦路的了，他立刻被领到侄女占着的房里。
他走近床边，见她那样神经剧烈地震动起来，吃惊伤心，说不出口。可他直到把弗兰克兰

===Original===

Frankland’s message, and had found that it failed altogether in producing the reassuring effect on her spirits which he had trusted and believed that it would exercise. Instead of soothing, it seemed to excite and alarm her afresh. Among a host of minute inquiries about Mrs. Frankland’s looks, about her manner toward him, about the exact words she had spoken, all of which he was able to answer more or less to her satisfaction, she had addressed two questions to him, to which he was utterly unable to reply. The first of the questions was, Whether Mrs. Frankland had said anything about the Secret? The second was, Whether she had spoken any chance word to lead to the suspicion that she had found out the situation of the Myrtle Room?
The doctor in attendance had come in, the old man added, while he was still sitting by his niece’s bedside, and still trying ineffectually to induce her to accept the friendly and reassuring language of Mrs. Frankland’s message. After making some inquiries and talking a little while on indifferent matters, the doctor had privately taken him aside; had informed him that the pain over the region of the heart and the difficulty in breathing, which were the symptoms of which his niece complained, were more serious in their nature than persons uninstructed in medical matters might be disposed to think; and had begged him to give her no more messages from anyone, unless he felt perfectly sure beforehand that they would have the effect of clearing her mind, at once and forever, from the secret anxieties that now harassed it—anxieties which he might rest assured were aggravating her malady day by day, and rendering all the medical help that could be given of little or no avail.
Upon this, after sitting longer with his niece, and after holding counsel with himself, he had resolved to write privately to Mrs. Frankland that evening, after getting back to his friend’s house. The letter had taken him longer to compose than anyone accustomed to writing would believe. At last, after delays in making a fair copy from many rough drafts, and delays in leaving his task to attend to his niece, he had completed a letter narrating what had happened since his arrival in London, in language which he hoped might be understood. Judging by comparison of dates, this letter must have crossed Mr. and Mrs. Frankland on the road.

===Chinese===

太太的话捎到、见它同他信着盼着的全然两样，在她心上丝毫不起宽慰的效用，这才死了心、灰了志。那番话不抚慰，倒仿佛重新把她撩起来、吓起来。她细细盘问了一大车：弗兰克兰太太的模样、待他的神气、说过的原话，样样他都答得出个大概，她勉强算满意；可有两条，她问到他头上，他一概答不上。头一条：弗兰克兰太太提没提那个秘密？第二条：她可曾说过什么不要紧的话，教人疑心她已经寻出了桃金娘房间在哪里？
伺候的大夫进来了，老人添上说，那时他还坐在侄女床边，还在白费气力劝她领受弗兰克兰太太那番话里友好的宽心。大夫问了几句，说了几句不相干的话，便私下把他拉到一旁；告诉他：侄女叫唤的那些症候，心口疼、喘不上气，性质比不懂医道的人想的重；又恳求他：往后谁的话也不许再传给她，除非他事前拿得十足地稳：那些话能把她心头此刻折磨着的隐忧，一下子、永远地扫干净——那些隐忧，他尽管放一百个心，一天一天加重她的病，把所能给的医药一齐变成无用。
于是他又陪侄女坐了一阵，又同自己合计了一阵，决意当晚回到朋友宅里，私下写信给弗兰克兰太太。那封信，寻常会写字的人不信他要费那样大的工夫。改了不知多少张草稿，誊清誊了不知多少遍，中间还搁下笔去照应侄女，末了总算写成一封：他到伦敦以后出的种种事情，他指望人家看得懂的话，一一写在里头。拿日子一对照，这封信在路上必定同弗兰克兰夫妇错身而过。

===Original===
