她说这些话的时候，眼睛切切地盯着女儿的脸；那双衰弱枯瘦的指头里，爱整洁的天性还在机械地动。罗丝蒙德前一分钟才把手套抛在床上；母亲已经把它们拾起来，细细抚平，齐齐整整叠在一处，嘴里说着，手里做着。
「再叫我一声『妈妈』，」罗丝蒙德把手套接过来，亲了她一下谢她叠好，她说。「你叫我『妈妈』，我这才头一回听见——打你落地的日子起，从来没有，从来没有过！」
罗丝蒙德按住又涌上来的泪，把那个字重说了一遍。
「躺在这里望着你，听你说这个，我要的福分尽在这里了！普天之下，我的爱，可有第二个女人，有一张同你一样美、一样善的脸？」她停了停，微微一笑。「如今我一看见那两张甜的、红红的嘴唇，」她说，「就想起它们欠我多少个亲亲！」
「你若早容我把这笔债还了！」罗丝蒙德说，握起母亲的手——同她平常握孩子的小手一样——搁在自己的颈上。

===Original===

“If you had only spoken the first time we met, when you came to nurse me! How sorrowfully I have thought of that since! Oh, mother, did I distress you much in my ignorance? Did it make you cry when you thought of me after that?”
“Distress me! All my distress, Rosamond, has been of my own making, not of yours. My kind, thoughtful love! you said, ‘Don’t be hard on her’—do you remember? When I was being sent away, deservedly sent away, dear, for frightening you, you said to your husband, ‘Don’t be hard on her!’ Only five words—but, oh, what a comfort it was to me afterward to think that you had said them! I did want to kiss you so, Rosamond, when I was brushing your hair. I had such a hard fight of it to keep from crying out loud when I heard you, behind the bed-curtains, wishing your little child good night. My heart was in my mouth, choking me all that time. I took your part afterward, when I went back to my mistress—I wouldn’t hear her say a harsh word of you. I could have looked a hundred mistresses in the face then, and contradicted them all. Oh, no, no, no! you never distressed me. My worst grief at going away was years and years before I came to nurse you at West Winston. It was when I left my place at Porthgenna; when I stole into your nursery on that dreadful morning, and when I saw you with both your little arms round my master’s neck. The doll you had taken to bed with you was in one of your hands, and your head was resting on the Captain’s bosom, just as mine rests now—oh, so happily, Rosamond!—on yours. I heard the last words he was speaking to you—words you were too young to remember. ‘Hush! Rosie, dear,’ he said, ‘don’t cry any more for poor mamma. Think of poor papa, and try to comfort him!’ There, my love—there was the bitterest distress and the hardest to bear! I, your own mother, standing like a spy, and hearing him say that to the child I dared not own! ‘Think of poor papa!’ My own Rosamond! you know, now, what father *I* thought of when he said those words! How could I tell him the Secret? how could I give him the letter, with his wife dead that morning—with nobody but you to comfort him—with the awful truth crushing down upon my heart, at every word he spoke, as heavily as ever the rock crushed down upon the father you never saw!”
“Don’t speak of it now!” said Rosamond.

===Chinese===

「你若头一回见面就开口——你来伺候我病中的那一次！打那以后，我想起来，多凄凉！啊，妈妈，我那时什么都不懂，可曾叫你十分难受？过后你想起我来，哭过没有？」
「叫我难受！罗丝蒙德，我一切的难受，是我自己造的，不是你造的。我那又善体人意、又疼人的宝贝！你说，『别难为她』——记得么？我因为吓着了你，要被打发走——打发得应当，亲爱的——你对丈夫说：『别难为她！』不过五个字——可是，啊，事后想起你说过这几个字，多大的安慰！罗丝蒙德，我替你梳头发的时候，真想亲你。隔着床帐听见你给小宝宝道晚安，我拼命忍着，才没有哭出声来。那一段工夫，我的心堵在嗓子眼，噎得慌。后来我回到主母身边，我还偏袒你——她骂你一句重话，我不许。那时候，一百个主母的脸我都敢对着望，一百个我都要顶回去。啊，不，不，不！你从来没有叫我难受过。我离宅那一回顶重的哀伤，远在西温斯顿来伺候你之前的许多年。是我辞去波思根纳差事的那一天；那个可怕的日子早晨，我偷偷溜进你的育儿室，看见你两条小胳膊搂着我老爷的脖子。你抱上床去的那个娃娃握在一只手里，头靠在舰长的怀里，同我此刻靠着你的头——啊，多快活，罗丝蒙德！——一样。我听见他对你说末了的话——那些话你太小，记不得。『嘘！罗西，乖，』他说，『别再为可怜的妈咪哭了。想想可怜的爹地，想法子安慰他！』就是那里，我的爱——顶苦、顶捱不过的难受在那里！我，你的亲母亲，像个奸细一样站着，听他对我不敢认的孩子说那些话！『想想可怜的爹地！』我自己的罗丝蒙德！如今你知道，他说那些话的时候，*我*心里想的是哪个父亲！那桩秘密，我怎么开得了口？那封信，我怎么交得出？他妻子那天早晨死了——身边只剩你一个可以安慰他——那句可怕的真话压在我心上，他每说一个字，压得重一分，同那块砸死你从未见过面的父亲的岩石，压得一样重！」
「如今别说这个了！」罗丝蒙德说。

===Original===

“Don’t let us refer again to the past: I know all I ought to know, all I wish to know of it. We will talk of the future, mother, and of happier times to come. Let me tell you about my husband. If any words can praise him as he ought to be praised, and thank him as he ought to be thanked, I am sure mine ought—I am sure yours will! Let me tell you what he said and what he did when I read to him the letter that I found in the Myrtle Room. Yes, yes, do let me!”
Warned by a remembrance of the doctor’s last injunctions; trembling in secret, as she felt under her hand the heavy, toilsome, irregular heaving of her mother’s heart, as she saw the rapid changes of color, from pale to red, and from red to pale again, that fluttered across her mother’s face, she resolved to let no more words pass between them which were of a nature to recall painfully the sorrows and the suffering of the years that were gone. After describing the interview between her husband and herself which ended in the disclosure of the Secret, she led her mother, with compassionate abruptness, to speak of the future, of the time when she would be able to travel again, of the happiness of returning together to Cornwall, of the little festival they might hold on arriving at Uncle Joseph’s house in Truro, and of the time after that, when they might go on still farther to Porthgenna, or perhaps to some other place where new scenes and new faces might help them to forget all sad associations which it was best to think of no more.
Rosamond was still speaking on these topics, her mother was still listening to her with growing interest in every word that she said, when Uncle Joseph returned. He brought in with him a basket of flowers and a basket of fruit, which he held up in triumph at the foot of his niece’s bed.
“I have been walking about, my child, in the fine bright sunshine,” he said, “and waiting to give your face plenty of time to look happy, so that I might see it again as I want to see it always, for the rest of my life. Aha, Sarah! it is I who have brought the right doctor to cure you!” he added gayly, looking at Rosamond. “She has made you better already. Wait but a little while longer, and she shall get you up from your bed again, with your two cheeks as red, and your heart as light, and your tongue as fast to chatter as mine.

===Chinese===

往事咱们再别提了：该知道的、想知道的，我全知道了。咱们谈将来吧，妈妈，谈要来的好日子。让我把我的丈夫讲给你听。若有什么话配夸他、配谢他，我相信我的话配——我相信你的话更配！让我把我在桃金娘房间找到那封信、念给他听的时候，他说了什么、做了什么，讲给你听。是的，是的，让我讲！」
她记起大夫末了的嘱咐；一只手按在母亲心口，觉着那颗心又重、又累、又不匀地起伏，又看见母亲脸上红一阵、白一阵、又从白到红，飞快地变换，心里暗暗发抖，便拿定主意：往后两个人之间，再不许过一句叫人想起往年哀伤苦难的话。把丈夫同她那场以揭开秘密收场的对答描说完，她猛地把话锋一转，径直引母亲谈起将来：谈起她还能上路出门的日子，谈起一同回康沃尔的福分，谈起到了特鲁罗约瑟夫舅舅家里要办的小小庆典，谈起往后更远的行程——再到波思根纳去，或者到旁的什么地方，新的景致、新的面孔，帮她们把那些顶好不想再想的悲伤的旧影，统统忘掉。
罗丝蒙德还在这些题目上说，母亲还在听，她说一个字，母亲的兴味便深一分——约瑟夫舅舅回来了。他带进来一篮鲜花、一篮果子，捧着，得意洋洋，举在侄女床脚。
「我出去走走，我的孩子，在又亮又暖的太阳里，」他说，「等着，给你的脸留足了工夫显出快活来，好叫我再看见它——照我往后一辈子巴望的样子。啊哈，萨拉！是我把治你的好大夫请来了！」他朝罗丝蒙德望一眼，欢欢喜喜添上。「她把你治好些了。你再等一小会儿，她就要叫你下床，两腮红红的，心头轻轻的，嘴巴快快地讲闲话，同我一样。

===Original===

See the fine flowers and the fruit I have bought that is nice to your eyes, and nice to your nose, and nicest of all to put into your mouth! It is festival-time with us today, and we must make the room bright, bright, bright, all over. And then, there is your dinner to come soon; I have seen it on the dish—a cherub among chicken-fowls! And, after that, there is your fine sound sleep, with Mozart to sing the cradle song, and with me to sit for watch, and to go downstairs when you wake up again, and fetch your cup of tea. Ah, my child, my child, what a fine thing it is to have come at last to this festival-day!”
With a bright look at Rosamond, and with both his hands full of flowers, he turned away from his niece to begin decorating the room. Except when she thanked the old man for the presents he had brought, her attention had never wandered, all the while he had been speaking, from her daughter’s face; and her first words, when he was silent again, were addressed to Rosamond alone.
“While I am happy with *my* child,” she said, “I am keeping you from *yours*. I, of all persons, ought to be the last to part you from each other too long. Go back now, my love, to your husband and your child; and leave me to my grateful thoughts and my dreams of better times.”
“If you please, answer Yes to that, for your mother’s sake,” said Uncle Joseph, before Rosamond could reply. “The doctor says she must take her repose in the day as well as her repose in the night. And how shall I get her to close her eyes, so long as she has the temptation to keep them open upon *you*?”
Rosamond felt the truth of those last words, and consented to go back for a few hours to the hotel, on the understanding that she was to resume her place at the bedside in the evening. After making this arrangement, she waited long enough in the room to see the meal brought up which Uncle Joseph had announced, and to aid the old man in encouraging her mother to partake of it. When the tray had been removed, and when the pillows of the bed had been comfortably arranged by her own hands, she at last prevailed on herself to take leave.
Her mother’s arms lingered round her neck; her mother’s cheek nestled fondly against hers. “Go, my dear, go now, or I shall get too selfish to part with you even for a few hours,” murmured the sweet voice, in the lowest, softest tones. “My own Rosamond!

===Chinese===

瞧这些好看的鲜花、这些鲜果，我买的：瞧着舒服，闻着香，放进嘴里顶美！今天是咱们过节的日子，屋里要处处点得亮亮的、亮亮的、亮亮的。还有，你的晚饭就要来了；我在盘子里看见了——鸡群中的一只小天使！再往后，还有你的香香的一觉，莫扎特给你唱摇篮曲，我给你守着，等你醒了再下楼，给你端茶来。啊，我的孩子，我的孩子，到底挨到这个节日的日子，多好啊！」
朝罗丝蒙德欢欢喜喜望了一眼，两手捧满了花，他从侄女身边转过身去，动手装点屋子。他说话的整段工夫，她除了谢他带来的礼物，注意力一刻也没有离开女儿的脸；他重新静下来，她开口的头一句，单对罗丝蒙德说。
「我同*我的*孩子快活，」她说，「倒把*你的*孩子耽搁了。天下人里头，最不该叫你们母子久别的，是我。回去吧，我的爱，回到你的丈夫、你的孩子那里；留我守着我感激的心思、守着我好日子的梦。」
「你若不嫌，替你母亲应一句好，」不等罗丝蒙德答，约瑟夫舅舅先插进话。「大夫说她白日也要歇，同夜里歇一样。她眼皮睁着舍不得挪，挪开*你*身上——她的眼我怎么合得上？」
罗丝蒙德觉出末了那句话在理，应承回旅馆去几个钟头，讲定了傍晚回床边守着。这样安排停当，她又在屋里多待了一会儿，等约瑟夫舅舅报过的那顿饭端上来，帮着老人劝母亲进食。托盘撤了，床上的枕头经她亲手安放舒齐，她到底劝服自己告辞。
母亲的胳膊恋恋地搂着她的脖子；母亲的脸亲亲热热偎着她的脸。「去吧，亲爱的，去吧，不然我自私起来，几个钟头也不肯放你，」甜甜的嗓音低低地、柔柔地咕哝。「我自己的罗丝蒙德！

===Original===

I have no words to bless you that are good enough; no words to thank you that will speak as gratefully for me as they ought! Happiness has been long in reaching me—but, oh, how mercifully it has come at last!”
Before she passed the door, Rosamond stopped and looked back into the room. The table, the mantelpiece, the little framed prints on the wall were bright with flowers; the musical box was just playing the first sweet notes of the air from Mozart; Uncle Joseph was seated already in his accustomed place by the bed, with the basket of fruit on his knees; the pale, worn face on the pillow was tenderly lighted up by a smile; peace and comfort and repose, all mingled together happily in the picture of the sickroom, all joined in leading Rosamond’s thoughts to dwell quietly on the hope of a happier time.
Three hours passed. The last glory of the sun was lighting the long summer day to its rest in the western heaven, when Rosamond returned to her mother’s bedside.
She entered the room softly. The one window in it looked toward the west, and on that side of the bed the chair was placed which Uncle Joseph had occupied when she left him, and in which she now found him still seated on her return. He raised his fingers to his lips, and looked toward the bed, as she opened the door. Her mother was asleep, with her hand resting in the hand of the old man.
As Rosamond noiselessly advanced, she saw that Uncle Joseph’s eyes looked dim and weary. The constraint of the position that he occupied, which made it impossible for him to move without the risk of awakening his niece, seemed to be beginning to fatigue him. Rosamond removed her bonnet and shawl, and made a sign to him to rise and let her take his place.
“Yes, yes!” she whispered, seeing him reply by a shake of the head. “Let me take my turn, while you go out a little and enjoy the cool evening air. There is no fear of waking her; her hand is not clasping yours, but only resting in it—let me steal mine into its place gently, and we shall not disturb her.”
She slipped her hand under her mother’s while she spoke. Uncle Joseph smiled as he rose from his chair, and resigned his place to her. “You will have your way,” he said; “you are too quick and sharp for an old man like me.”
“Has she been long asleep?” asked Rosamond.
“Nearly two hours,” answered Uncle Joseph.

===Chinese===

祝福你的话，我想不出够好的；谢你的话，我想不出够真的！福分来得这样迟——可是，啊，它到底来了，来得多慈悲！」
出门之前，罗丝蒙德停了一步，回头朝屋里望。桌上、壁炉架上、墙上那些小镜框画，都叫花照得亮亮的；音乐盒正奏出莫扎特那支曲子头几缕甜甜的音；约瑟夫舅舅已经在他惯常的位置上坐下，守着床，膝头搁着果篮；枕上那张苍白憔悴的脸，叫一丝微笑温柔地照亮；安宁、舒适、歇息，快快活活融作一幅病房的画图，一齐把罗丝蒙德的心思安安静静引向好日子的指望上去。
三个钟头过去。太阳末了的辉煌正把长长的夏日送进西天安息，罗丝蒙德回到母亲床边。
她轻轻进屋。屋里那一扇窗朝西；她走时约瑟夫舅舅占着的那把椅子，便安在床的那一边——此刻回来，她还见他在那里坐着。她开门时，他把手指按上嘴唇，朝床那边望。母亲睡着，一只手搭在老人手里。
罗丝蒙德悄没声地走近，看见约瑟夫舅舅的眼色黯了，乏了。他那把椅子占的位置处处受拘束，一动便有惊醒侄女的险，看着渐渐叫他熬不住了。罗丝蒙德摘下帽子披肩，朝他打手势，叫他起来，让她换班。
「是，是！」见他摇头作答，她低声说。「让我轮一环，你出去走一走，享一享晚凉的空气。惊不着她；她的手没有攥着你的，不过搭在你的手里——让我把我的手轻轻换进去，我们惊不动她。」
她说着，把手从母亲的手底下轻轻滑进去。约瑟夫舅舅笑着从椅上立起，把位置让给她。「你总有你的道理，」他说。「你这样眼明手快，我这个老头子比不过。」
「她睡得久了么？」罗丝蒙德问。
「差不多两个钟头，」约瑟夫舅舅答。

===Original===

“But it has not been the good sleep I wanted for her—a dreaming, talking, restless sleep. It is only ten little minutes since she has been so quiet as you see her now.”
“Surely you let in too much light?” whispered Rosamond, looking round at the window, through which the glow of the evening sky poured warmly into the room.
“No, no!” he hastily rejoined. “Asleep or awake, she always wants the light. If I go away for a little while, as you tell me, and if it gets on to be dusk before I come back, light both those candles on the chimneypiece. I shall try to be here again before that; but if the time slips by too fast for me, and if it so happens that she wakes and talks strangely, and looks much away from you into that far corner of the room there, remember that the matches and the candles are together on the chimneypiece, and that the sooner you light them after the dim twilight-time, the better it will be.” With those words he stole on tiptoe to the door and went out.
His parting directions recalled Rosamond to a remembrance of what had passed between the doctor and herself that morning. She looked round again anxiously to the window.
The sun was just sinking beyond the distant housetops; the close of day was not far off.
As she turned her head once more toward the bed, a momentary chill crept over her. She trembled a little, partly at the sensation itself, partly at the recollection it aroused of that other chill which had struck her in the solitude of the Myrtle Room.
Stirred by the mysterious sympathies of touch, her mother’s hand at the same instant moved in hers, and over the sad peacefulness of the weary face there fluttered a momentary trouble—the flying shadow of a dream. The pale, parted lips opened, closed, quivered, opened again; the toiling breath came and went quickly and more quickly; the head moved uneasily on the pillow; the eyelids half unclosed themselves; low, faint, moaning sounds poured rapidly from the lips—changed ere long to half-articulated sentences—then merged softly into intelligible speech, and uttered these words:
“Swear that you will not destroy this paper! Swear that you will not take this paper away with you if you leave the house!”
The words that followed these were whispered so rapidly and so low that Rosamond’s ear failed to catch them. They were followed by a short silence.

===Chinese===

可那不是我巴望她有的好觉——做梦、说梦话、翻来覆去的觉。她安静成如今这个样子，不过小小的十分钟。」
「光放进来的怕是太多了？」罗丝蒙德低声说，回头朝窗户望——暮天的红光正暖暖地涌进屋里。
「不，不！」他急忙答。「睡也罢，醒也罢，她总离不得光。我若出去一小会儿，照你说的，若回来之前天已经擦黑，壁炉架上那两支蜡烛，两支都点上。我尽力赶在头里回来；可时辰若从我脚底下溜得太快，若凑巧她醒来说起古怪的话，眼睛老朝那边屋角里望——记住，火柴和蜡烛同在壁炉架上，天光一昏暗便点上，越早越好。」说罢，他踮着脚尖溜到门口，出去了。
他临别的嘱咐，把那天早晨大夫同她的一番对答，唤回罗丝蒙德心里。她又不安地朝窗上望。
太阳正沉到远处的屋脊背后；白昼的尽头不远了。
她把头重新转向床那边，一阵短促的寒意爬过全身。她微微发抖——一半为那感觉本身，一半为它勾起的回忆：桃金娘房间的孤寂里袭击过她的那阵寒意。
肌肤之间神秘的感应，同一个瞬间，母亲的手在她手里动了，困乏的脸上那片哀哀的宁静上，掠过一阵短促的不安——一个梦影飞掠而过。苍白的、微张的嘴唇开了，合了，颤了，又开了；吃力的呼吸来来去去，越去越急；头在枕上不安地挪动；眼皮半睁半闭；低低的、虚虚的、呻吟似的声音，急急从唇边流出来——不多时变成半吐半咽的句子——随后轻轻融成明白的话语，吐出这样几个字：
「发誓你不毁掉这张纸！发誓你离开这宅子，不把这张纸带走！」
跟在后面的话，又急又低，罗丝蒙德的耳朵捉不住。随后是短短的静默。

===Original===

Then the dreaming voice spoke again suddenly, and spoke louder.
“Where? where? where?” it said. “In the bookcase? In the table-drawer?—Stop! stop! In the picture of the ghost—”
The last words struck cold on Rosamond’s heart. She drew back suddenly with a movement of alarm—checked herself the instant after, and bent down over the pillow again. But it was too late. Her hand had moved abruptly when she drew back, and her mother awoke with a start and a faint cry—with vacant, terror-stricken eyes, and with the perspiration standing thick on her forehead.
“Mother!” cried Rosamond, raising her on the pillow. “I have come back. Don’t you know me?”
“Mother?” she repeated, in mournful, questioning tones—“Mother?” At the second repetition of the word a bright flush of delight and surprise broke out on her face, and she clasped both arms suddenly round her daughter’s neck. “Oh, my own Rosamond!” she said. “If I had ever been used to waking up and seeing your dear face look at me, I should have known you sooner, in spite of my dream! Did you wake me, my love? or did I wake myself?”
“I am afraid I awoke you, mother.”
“Don’t say ‘afraid.’ I would wake from the sweetest sleep that ever woman had to see your face and to hear you say ‘mother’ to me. You have delivered me, my love, from the terror of one of my dreadful dreams. Oh, Rosamond! I think I should live to be happy in your love, if I could only get Porthgenna Tower out of my mind—if I could only never remember again the bedchamber where my mistress died, and the room where I hid the letter—”
“We will try and forget Porthgenna Tower now,” said Rosamond. “Shall we talk about other places where I have lived, which you have never seen? Or shall I read to you, mother? Have you got any book here that you are fond of?”
She looked across the bed at the table on the other side. There was nothing on it but some bottles of medicine, a few of Uncle Joseph’s flowers in a glass of water, and a little oblong work-box. She looked round at the chest of drawers behind her—there were no books placed on the top of it. Before she turned toward the bed again, her eyes wandered aside to the window. The sun was lost beyond the distant housetops; the close of day was near at hand.
“If I could forget! Oh, me, if I could only forget!” said her mother, sighing wearily, and beating her hand on the coverlid of the bed.

===Chinese===

随后梦里的话音陡然又起，说得响了。
「哪里？哪里？哪里？」它说。「书柜里？桌子抽屉里？——站住！站住！在鬼的画像里——」
末了那几个字，凉冰冰打在罗丝蒙德心上。她猛地一缩，带着惊惶的举动——随即止住，重新俯向枕头。可已经迟了。她缩身的时候，手猛地一动，母亲惊起，微微一叫——两眼空茫茫的，盛满恐怖，额头上的汗一层层冒着。
「妈妈！」罗丝蒙德叫道，把她从枕上扶起。「我回来了。你不认得我？」
「妈妈？」她跟着念，声气凄凄的，探询似的——「妈妈？」念到第二遍，一团欢喜和惊讶的红光在她脸上迸开，两条胳膊猛地搂住女儿的脖子。「啊，我自己的罗丝蒙德！」她说。「我往常若惯了醒来就看见你的亲脸望着我，管它做梦不做梦，我早就认出你了！是你惊醒了我么，我的爱？还是我自己醒的？」
「是我惊醒了你，妈妈，我怕是。」
「别说『怕』。女人睡得再甜，为见你的脸、听你叫我一声『妈妈』，我情愿醒来。我的爱，你把我从一桩可怕的梦里那个怕字里救出来了。啊，罗丝蒙德！只要能把波思根纳塔楼从心里撵出去——只要能再不想起主母咽气的那间卧房、我藏信的那间屋子——我想，我活得成，能在你的爱里快活地活下去——」
「波思根纳塔楼，咱们如今就把它忘了，」罗丝蒙德说。「说说旁的、我住过、你没见过的那些地方，好不好？要不，妈妈，我念书给你听？这里可有你爱读的书？」
她隔着床朝那边的桌上望。桌上只有几瓶药，几枝约瑟夫舅舅的花插在玻璃杯里，一只小小的长方针线盒。她又回头朝身后的抽屉柜望——柜顶上没有摆书。转身朝床之前，眼睛朝窗上溜了溜。太阳没进远处的屋脊背后了；白昼的尽头近了。
「若忘得掉！啊天哪，若忘得掉才好！」她母亲说，疲惫地叹息，手在床罩上一下一下拍着。

===Original===

“Are you well enough, dear, to amuse yourself with work?” asked Rosamond, pointing to the little oblong box on the table, and trying to lead the conversation to a harmless, everyday topic, by asking questions about it. “What work do you do? May I look at it?”
Her face lost its weary, suffering look, and brightened once more into a smile. “There is no work there,” she said. “All the treasures I had in the world, till you came to see me, are shut up in that one little box. Open it, my love, and look inside.”
Rosamond obeyed, placing the box on the bed where her mother could see it easily. The first object that she discovered inside was a little book, in dark, worn binding. It was an old copy of Wesley’s Hymns. Some withered blades of grass lay between its pages; and on one of its blank leaves was this inscription—“Sarah Leeson, her book. The gift of Hugh Polwheal.”
“Look at it, my dear,” said her mother. “I want you to know it again. When my time comes to leave you, Rosamond, lay it on my bosom with your own dear hands, and put a little morsel of your hair with it, and bury me in the grave in Porthgenna churchyard, where *he* has been waiting for me to come to him so many weary years. The other things in the box, Rosamond, belong to you; they are little stolen keepsakes that used to remind me of my child, when I was alone in the world. Perhaps, years and years hence, when your brown hair begins to grow gray like mine, you may like to show these poor trifles to your children when you talk about me. Don’t mind telling them, Rosamond, how your mother sinned and how she suffered—you can always let these little trifles speak for her at the end. The least of them will show that she always loved you.”
She took out of the box a morsel of neatly folded white paper, which had been placed under the book of Wesley’s Hymns, opened it, and showed her daughter a few faded laburnum leaves that lay inside. “I took these from your bed, Rosamond, when I came, as a stranger, to nurse you at West Winston. I tried to take a ribbon out of your trunk, love, after I had taken the flowers—a ribbon that I knew had been round your neck. But the doctor came near at the time, and frightened me.”
She folded the paper up again, laid it aside on the table, and drew from the box next a small print which had been taken from the illustrations to a pocketbook.

===Chinese===

「亲爱的，身子撑得住么？做点活解解闷？」罗丝蒙德指指桌上那只小小的长方盒，想拿问它的话把话头引到无害的日常题目上去。「你做什么活？容我瞧瞧行么？」
她脸上那副困乏受苦的神气褪了，重新透亮，透出笑来。「那里头没有活，」她说。「你来看我之前，我在世上一切的宝贝，都锁在那只小小的盒子里。打开它，我的爱，瞧瞧里头。」
罗丝蒙德依言，把盒子放在床上，教母亲看得容易。她在里头发现的头一样，是一本小书，深色书皮，磨旧了。是一本旧版的卫斯理《赞美诗》。书页里夹着几茎枯草；一片空页上写着这样几个字——「萨拉·利森的书。休·波尔惠尔赠。」
「瞧着它，亲爱的，」她母亲说。「我要你认得它。罗丝蒙德，到了我离你的那一天，拿你亲爱的两只手把它放在我胸口，再添一小缕你的头发，把我埋在波思根纳教堂墓地的那个坟里——*他*在那底下等我，等了这许多困乏的年头。盒子里旁的东西，罗丝蒙德，都是你的；都是些偷来收着的小纪念，我孤零零在世上的年月，它们替我想起我的孩子。兴许许多许多年之后，你的黑头发像我的开始变白了，你同孩子们谈起我，兴许肯把这些可怜的小东西拿给他们看。罗丝蒙德，别怕告诉他们，你们的母亲怎样犯了罪、怎样受了苦——到末了，你总可以把这些小东西叫出来替她说话。顶小的一样也能证明：她始终疼你。」
她从盒里取出一角折得整整齐齐的白纸——搁在卫斯理《赞美诗》底下的——打开，给女儿看里头的几片褪色的金链花叶。「这是我从你床上摘的，罗丝蒙德，我乔装生客到西温斯顿伺候你的那一天。摘了花，我还想从你箱子里拿一条缎带，亲爱的——一条我记得围过你脖子的。可大夫正走近来，把我吓退了。」
她把纸重新折起，搁在桌上，接着又从盒里取出一幅小画，是从一本袖珍记事册的插图里揭下来的。

===Original===

It represented a little girl, in gypsy-hat, sitting by the waterside, and weaving a daisy chain. As a design, it was worthless; as a print, it had not even the mechanical merit of being a good impression. Underneath it a line was written in faintly pencilled letters—“Rosamond when I last saw her.”
“It was never pretty enough for you,” she said. “But still there was something in it that helped me to remember what my own love was like when she was a little girl.”
She put the engraving aside with the laburnum leaves, and took from the box a leaf of a copybook, folded in two, out of which there dropped a tiny strip of paper, covered with small printed letters. She looked at the strip of paper first. “The advertisement of your marriage, Rosamond,” she said. “I used to be fond of reading it over and over again to myself when I was alone, and trying to fancy how you looked and what dress you wore. If I had only known when you were going to be married, I would have ventured into the church, my love, to look at you and at your husband. But that was not to be—and perhaps it was best so, for the seeing you in that stolen way might only have made my trials harder to bear afterward. I have had no other keepsake to remind me of you, Rosamond, except this leaf out of your first copybook. The nursemaid at Porthgenna tore up the rest one day to light the fire, and I took this leaf when she was not looking. See! you had not got as far as words then—you could only do upstrokes and downstrokes. Oh me! how many times I have sat looking at this one leaf of paper, and trying to fancy that I saw your small child’s hand traveling over it, with the pen held tight in the rosy little fingers. I think I have cried oftener, my darling, over that first copy of yours than over all my other keepsakes put together.”
Rosamond turned aside her face toward the window to hide the tears which she could restrain no longer.
As she wiped them away, the first sight of the darkening sky warned her that the twilight dimness was coming soon. How dull and faint the glow in the west looked now! how near it was to the close of day!
When she turned toward the bed again, her mother was still looking at the leaf of the copybook.
“That nursemaid who tore up all the rest of it to light the fire,” she said, “was a kind friend to me in those early days at Porthgenna.

===Chinese===

画上是个小姑娘，戴着吉普赛式的宽檐帽，坐在水边，编一串雏菊花环。论画意，一文不值；论版印，连印工齐整这点长处也没有。底下有一行淡淡的铅笔字——「罗丝蒙德，我末一回见她的时候。」
「它从来不配你好看，」她说。「可它里头总有一点东西，帮我想起我自己的爱做小姑娘的时候的模样。」
她把那张版画同金链花叶搁在一处，又从盒里取出一页习字帖，对折着——里头掉出一小条纸，印满了小字。她先看那条纸。「你成亲的启事，罗丝蒙德。」她说。「我独自一个人的时候，爱把它拿来一遍一遍念，设想你的模样、你穿的衣裳。我若知道你成亲的日子，亲爱的，我准敢上教堂去，远远地望一望你，望一望你的丈夫。可那办不到——兴许不办倒好，那样偷看你一眼，兴许反倒教往后的煎熬更难捱。除了你头一本习字帖的这一页，罗丝蒙德，我再没有旁的纪念，替我想起你。波思根纳那个看孩子的奶妈有一天把其余的撕了生火，我趁她不留神，抢下这一页。瞧！你那时候还没写到字呢——只会一横一竖地画道道。天哪！有多少回，我对着这一页纸呆坐，只管设想看见你小孩子的小手在纸上爬，笔杆紧紧攥在粉红的小指头里。我的宝贝，为你的头一页习字，我流的泪，恐怕比我旁的纪念加在一起流的还多。」
罗丝蒙德把脸转向窗口，遮一遮再也按捺不住的眼泪。
拭泪的当口，天色暗下来了的头一眼警告她：黄昏的昏暗快到了。西边那片红光，如今看着多暗、多淡！离白昼的尽头多近！
她重新转向床那边，母亲还在望着那页习字帖。
「撕了它拿去生火的那个奶妈，」她说，「在波思根纳起头的那些日子，是我的一个好心的朋友。

===Original===

She used sometimes to let me put you to bed, Rosamond; and never asked questions, or teased me, as the rest of them did. She risked the loss of her place by being so good to me. My mistress was afraid of my betraying myself and betraying her if I was much in the nursery, and she gave orders that I was not to go there, because it was not my place. None of the other women-servants were so often stopped from playing with you and kissing you, Rosamond, as I was. But the nursemaid—God bless and prosper her for it!—stood my friend. I often lifted you into your little cot, my love, and wished you good night, when my mistress thought I was at work in her room. You used to say you liked your nurse better than you liked me, but you never told me so fretfully; and you always put your laughing lips up to mine whenever I asked you for a kiss!”
Rosamond laid her head gently on the pillow by the side of her mother’s. “Try to think less of the past, dear, and more of the future,” she whispered pleadingly; “try to think of the time when my child will help you to recall those old days without their sorrow—the time when you will teach him to put his lips up to yours, as I used to put mine.”
“I will try, Rosamond—but my only thoughts of the future, for years and years past, have been thoughts of meeting you in heaven. If my sins are forgiven, how shall we meet there? Shall you be like my little child to me—the child I never saw again after she was five years old? I wonder if the mercy of God will recompense me for our long separation on earth? I wonder if you will first appear to me in the happy world with your child’s face, and be what you should have been to me on earth, my little angel that I can carry in my arms? If we pray in heaven, shall I teach you your prayers there, as some comfort to me for never having taught them to you here?”
She paused, smiled sadly, and, closing her eyes, gave herself in silence to the dream-thoughts that were still floating in her mind. Thinking that she might sink to rest again if she was left undisturbed, Rosamond neither moved nor spoke. After watching the peaceful face for some time, she became conscious that the light was fading on it slowly. As that conviction impressed itself on her, she looked round at the window once more.
The western clouds wore their quiet twilight colors already: the close of day had come.

===Chinese===

她有时候容我安顿你上床，罗丝蒙德；从来不多问，也不来搅我，不像她们其余的人。她待我这样好，冒着丢差事的险。我若多往育儿室里去，主母怕我自己露了馅、也带累她，吩咐过不许我去——那里不是我的差事地界。旁的使女，罗丝蒙德，谁也不像我那样，常叫人拦着，不许同你玩、不许亲你。可那个奶妈——上帝保佑她、赐福她！——偏做了我的朋友。我常常把你抱进你的小摇篮，我的爱，给你道晚安——那时候主母只当我在她屋里做活。你从前说，你喜欢奶妈多过喜欢我，可你从来不气鼓鼓地说；我一讨亲亲，你总把笑嘻嘻的嘴唇送上来！」
罗丝蒙德把头轻轻枕在母亲头边的枕上。「少想一点过去，多想一点将来，亲爱的，」她恳求地低声说；「想一想那样的日子：我的孩子帮你想起那些旧年月，想起它们却不带它们的哀伤——想一想你教他把嘴唇送上来亲你，同我从前一样。」
「我试着想，罗丝蒙德——可这些年，我对将来唯一的心思，是天上一处相见的念头。我的罪若得饶恕，我们在那里怎样相见呢？你还像我的小孩子么——五岁以后我再没见着的那个孩子？上帝的慈悲，会不会偿我在地上这一场长别？在极乐的世界里，你会不会先带着孩子的脸面来见我，做你在地上该做给我的——我做在怀里的小天使？我们在天上祷告，我可不可以在那里教你祷告，稍稍补一补地上从来没教你祷告的亏？」
她停住，惨然一笑，闭上眼，静静把自己交给还在心里飘着的梦念。罗丝蒙德想：不去搅她，她兴许又睡得着，便一动不动，一言不发。望着那张安恬的脸望了许久，她觉出脸上的光在慢慢暗下去。这个感觉一上心，她又朝窗上望。
西天的云已经披上安恬的暮色：白昼的尽头到了。

===Original===

The moment she moved the chair, she felt her mother’s hand on her shoulder. When she turned again toward the bed, she saw her mother’s eyes open and looking at her—looking at her, as she thought, with a change in their expression, a change to vacancy.
“Why do I talk of heaven?” she said, turning her face suddenly toward the darkening sky, and speaking in low, muttering tones. “How do I know I am fit to go there? And yet, Rosamond, I am not guilty of breaking my oath to my mistress. You can say for me that I never destroyed the letter, and that I never took it away with me when I left the house. I tried to get it out of the Myrtle Room; but I only wanted to hide it somewhere else. I never thought to take it away from the house: I never meant to break my oath.”
“It will be dark soon, mother. Let me get up for one moment to light the candles.”
Her hand crept softly upward, and clung fast round Rosamond’s neck.
“I never swore to give him the letter,” she said. “There was no crime in the hiding of it. You found it in a picture, Rosamond? They used to call it a picture of the Porthgenna ghost. Nobody knew how old it was, or when it came into the house. My mistress hated it, because the painted face had a strange likeness to hers. She told me, when first I lived at Porthgenna, to take it down from the wall and destroy it. I was afraid to do that; so I hid it away, before ever you were born, in the Myrtle Room. You found the letter at the back of the picture, Rosamond? And yet that was a likely place to hide it in. Nobody had ever found the picture. Why should anybody find the letter that was hid in it?”
“Let me get a light, mother! I am sure you would like to have a light!”
“No! no light now. Give the darkness time to gather down there in the corner of the room. Lift me up close to you, and let me whisper.”
The clinging arm tightened its grasp as Rosamond raised her in the bed. The fading light from the window fell full on her face, and was reflected dimly in her vacant eyes.
“I am waiting for something that comes at dusk, before the candles are lit,” she whispered in low, breathless tones. “My mistress!—down there!” And she pointed away to the farthest corner of the room near the door.
“Mother! for God’s sake, what is it! what has changed you so?”
“That’s right! say ‘mother.’

===Chinese===

她一挪椅子，觉出母亲的手搭上她的肩。她重新转向床那边，见母亲两眼睁着，望着她——照她看，眼里的神气变了，变空了。
「我为什么谈天上？」她说，脸猛地转向昏暗的天，声气低低的、咕哝似的。「我配不配去那里，我怎么知道？可是，罗丝蒙德，我对主母立下的誓，我没有破。你替我说：那封信我没有毁掉，我离开宅子的时候没有把它带走。我想把它带出桃金娘房间；可我只想把它另藏一个地方。我从来没想过把它带出宅子：我从来没存心破我的誓。」
「快黑了，妈妈。容我起来一下，把蜡烛点上。」
她的手悄悄往上爬，紧紧攀住罗丝蒙德的脖子。
「我从来没发誓要把信给他，」她说。「藏起它来，不是罪。你是在一张画里找着它的，罗丝蒙德？他们从前管它叫波思根纳鬼的画像。它有多大年头，几时进的宅，谁也不知道。我主母恨它，因为画上那张脸同她出奇地像。我才到波思根纳住下的时候，她就吩咐我把它从墙上摘下来毁掉。我不敢；便在你出世以前把它藏进桃金娘房间。你是在画背后找着信的，罗丝蒙德？那也本是藏信顶相宜的地方。那幅画从来没有人找着。藏在画里的信，谁找得着呢？」
「容我拿个亮来，妈妈！我信你愿意有个亮！」
「不！如今不要亮。容黑暗先在屋子那一头角落里聚起来。扶我起来，挨着你，容我说句悄悄话。」
罗丝蒙德把她扶起，那条攀着的胳膊把搂得更紧。窗里那片将灭的光，满满落在她脸上，又隐隐映在她空茫的眼睛里。
「我在等一样东西，黄昏里来的，蜡烛点上之前来的，」她压着嗓子、屏着气低声说。「我的主母！——在那底下！」她指着靠门最远的那个角落。
「妈妈！看上帝的份上，怎么了！什么把你变成这样！」
「这就对了！叫『妈妈』。

===Original===

*If she does come*, she can’t stop when she hears you call me ‘mother,’ when she sees us together at last, loving and knowing each other in spite of her. Oh, my kind, tender, pitying child! if you can only deliver me from her, how long may I live yet!—how happy we may both be!”
“Don’t talk so! don’t look so! Tell me quietly—dear, dear mother, tell me quietly—”
“Hush! hush! I am going to tell you. She threatened me on her deathbed, if I thwarted her—she said she would come to me from the other world. Rosamond! I *have* thwarted her and she has kept her promise—all my life since, she has kept her promise! Look! Down there!”
Her left arm was still clasped round Rosamond’s neck. She stretched her right arm out toward the far corner of the room, and shook her hand slowly at the empty air.
“Look!” she said. “There she is as she always comes to me at the close of day—with the coarse, black dress on, that my guilty hands made for her—with the smile that there was on her face when she asked me if she looked like a servant. Mistress! mistress! Oh, rest at last! the Secret is ours no longer! Rest at last! my child is my own again! Rest, at last; and come between us no more!”
She ceased, panting for breath; and laid her hot, throbbing cheek against the cheek of her daughter. “Call me ‘mother’ again!” she whispered. “Say it loud; and send her away from me forever!”
Rosamond mastered the terror that shook her in every limb, and pronounced the word.
Her mother leaned forward a little, still gasping heavily for breath, and looked with straining eyes into the quiet twilight dimness at the lower end of the room.
“*Gone!!!*” she cried suddenly, with a scream of exultation. “Oh, merciful, merciful God! gone at last!”
The next instant she sprang up on her knees in the bed. For one awful moment her eyes shone in the gray twilight with a radiant, unearthly beauty, as they fastened their last look of fondness on her daughter’s face. “Oh, my love! my angel!” she murmured, “how happy we shall be together now!” As she said the words, she twined her arms round Rosamond’s neck, and pressed her lips rapturously on the lips of her child.
The kiss lingered till her head sank forward gently on Rosamond’s bosom—lingered, till the time of God’s mercy came, and the weary heart rested at last.

===Chinese===

*她若果真来*，听你叫我『妈妈』，看见我们到底在一处了、彼此疼着、彼此认着，偏偏绕过了她——她便站不住了。啊，我的善心的、柔情的、怜惜我的孩子！你只消把我从她手里救出来，我还能活多久都行！——我们两个还能多快活！」
「别这样说！别这样望！安安静静告诉我——我亲爱的、亲爱的妈妈，安安静静告诉我——」
「嘘！嘘！我正要说。她在病榻上恐吓过我，倘若我逆她的意——她说她要从那一边的世界来找我。罗丝蒙德！我*偏偏*逆了她的意，她守着她的约——打那以后我一辈子，她守着她的约！瞧！在那底下！」
她的左臂还搂着罗丝蒙德的脖子。右臂朝屋子那头的远角伸出去，对着空无一物的空中，慢慢摇手。
「瞧！」她说。「她在那里，同每天黄昏到我这里来的一个样——穿着那身粗黑衣裳，我这双有罪的手给她缝的——带着那副笑，她问我像不像一个使女时候的那副笑。太太！太太！啊，安息吧，到底安息！秘密再不是我们的了！安息吧，到底安息！我的孩子又归我自己了！安息吧，到底；再不要到我们当中来！」
她住了口，喘不上气，把滚烫的、突突跳的腮贴上女儿的腮。「再叫我一声『妈妈』！」她低声说。「大声说；把她从我身边永远撵走！」
罗丝蒙德把那摇撼着她四肢百骸的恐怖压下去，念出那个字。
她母亲微微前倾，还重重地喘着气，眼睛竭力朝屋子下首那片安安静静的黄昏的昏暗里望。
「*去了！！！*」她猛地喊出来，狂喜的尖叫。「啊，慈悲的、慈悲的上帝！到底去了！」
下一瞬，她在床上跪着直起身来。灰蒙蒙的暮色里，那双眼睛亮了一瞬，放射出非凡的、超凡的美，把末了的一望，恋恋地钉在女儿脸上。「啊，我的爱！我的天使！」她低声说，「往后我们两个同在一处，多快活！」话音里，她两条胳膊搂住罗丝蒙德的脖子，狂喜地把嘴唇压上孩子的嘴唇。
那一枚亲亲流连着，直到她的头轻轻垂进罗丝蒙德怀里——流连着，直到上帝慈悲的时辰到来，那颗困乏的心，到底安息了。

## V Forty Thousand Pounds / 五 四万英镑
===Original===

No popular saying is more commonly accepted than the maxim which asserts that Time is the great consoler; and, probably, no popular saying more imperfectly expresses the truth. The work that we must do, the responsibilities that we must undertake, the example that we must set to others—these are the great consolers, for these apply the first remedies to the malady of grief. Time possesses nothing but the negative virtue of helping it to wear itself out. Who that has observed at all, has not perceived that those among us who soonest recover from the shock of a great grief for the dead are those who have the most duties to perform toward the living? When the shadow of calamity rests on our houses, the question with us is not how much time will suffice to bring back the sunshine to us again, but how much occupation have we got to force us forward into the place where the sunshine is waiting for us to come? Time may claim many victories, but not the victory over grief. The great consolation for the loss of the dead who are gone is to be found in the great necessity of thinking of the living who remain.
The history of Rosamond’s daily life, now that the darkness of a heavy affliction had fallen on it, was in itself the sufficient illustration of this truth. It was not the slow lapse of time that helped to raise her up again, but the necessity which would not wait for time—the necessity which made her remember what was due to the husband who sorrowed with her, to the child whose young life was linked to hers, and to the old man whose helpless grief found no support but in the comfort she could give, learned no lesson of resignation but from the example she could set.
From the first the responsibility of sustaining him had rested on her shoulders alone. Before the close of day had been counted out by the first hour of the night, she had been torn from the bedside by the necessity of meeting him at the door, and preparing him to know that he was entering the chamber of death. To guide the dreadful truth gradually and gently, till it stood face to face with him, to support him under the shock of recognizing it, to help his mind to recover after the inevitable blow had struck it at last—these were the sacred duties which claimed all the devotion that Rosamond had to give, and which forbade her heart, for his sake, to dwell selfishly on its own grief.

===Chinese===

世上流行的俗话，没有比「时间是顶大的安慰者」这一句更被大家信从的；兴许也没有一句俗话，把真情表达得更不完全。我们不得不做的事，我们不得不担的责任，我们不得不立给他人的榜样——这些才是顶大的安慰者，因为哀伤的病，头一副药便是它们下的。时间一无所有，只有一种消极的德行：帮哀伤自己耗尽自己。但凡留心过的人都看见：我们当中顶快从丧亡大恸的震撼里缓过来的，恰是对活人的责任最重的那几个？灾祸的黑影罩住我们的门户时，要紧的疑问不是多大的工夫够把日头重新照回来，而是我们手里有多少营生，逼着我们朝前走进日头正等着我们的地方。时间尽可以夸口打了许多胜仗，胜过哀伤这一仗却不在内。失去亡人的顶大安慰，是在惦记留下来的活人那份顶大的必要里。
罗丝蒙德的日常起居，如今罩上了一层深哀的黑暗，本身便是这条真理的足够注脚。把她重新扶起来的，不是时间慢慢地流，是不肯等时间的必要——那必要叫她记起：同她一起哀伤的丈夫，当得起她怎样一份照应；命脉同她连在一处的孩子，当得起她怎样一份看顾；那份孤苦无依的哀伤没有旁的支柱、只有她能给的安慰的老人，又只得从她立下的榜样里学顺受。
支撑他的担子，打头起便单双落在他肩上。白昼的尽头还没有被夜的头一个钟点数完，她已经被必要从床边拽走——必要她到门口去迎他，预备他知道：他踏进去的，是一间死亡躺着的屋子。把那可怕的真话一点一点、轻轻缓缓引到他跟前，叫他同它打了个照面；在他认出它的震动底下撑住他；等那免不了的一击到底落在他心上，再帮他的神志缓过来——这些便是神圣的责任，讨尽罗丝蒙德能给的忠诚，还禁止她的心，为了他的缘故，自私地守着自家的哀伤打转。

===Original===

He looked like a man whose faculties had been stunned past recovery. He would sit for hours with the musical box by his side, patting it absently from time to time, and whispering to himself as he looked at it, but never attempting to set it playing. It was the one memorial left that reminded him of all the joys and sorrows, the simple family interests and affections of his past life. When Rosamond first sat by his side and took his hand to comfort him, he looked backward and forward with forlorn eyes from her compassionate face to the musical box, and vacantly repeated to himself the same words over and over again: “They are all gone—my brother Max, my wife, my little Joseph, my sister Agatha, and Sarah, my niece! I and my little bit of box are left alone together in the world. Mozart can sing no more. He has sung to the last of them now!”
The second day there was no change in him. On the third, Rosamond placed the book of Hymns reverently on her mother’s bosom, laid a lock of her own hair round it, and kissed the sad, peaceful face for the last time.
The old man was with her at that silent leave-taking, and followed her away when it was over. By the side of the coffin, and afterward, when she took him back with her to her husband, he was still sunk in the same apathy of grief which had overwhelmed him from the first. But when they began to speak of the removal of the remains the next day to Porthgenna churchyard, they noticed that his dim eyes brightened suddenly, and that his wandering attention followed every word they said. After a while he rose from his chair, approached Rosamond, and looked anxiously in her face. “I think I could bear it better if you would let me go with her,” he said. “We two should have gone back to Cornwall together, if she had lived. Will you let us still go back together now that she has died?”
Rosamond gently remonstrated, and tried to make him see that it was best to leave the remains to be removed under the charge of her husband’s servant, whose fidelity could be depended on, and whose position made him the fittest person to be charged with cares and responsibilities which near relations were not capable of undertaking with sufficient composure.

===Chinese===

他看去像一个人，神志震坏了，再也缓不回来。他一坐几个钟头，音乐盒在身边，时不时心不在焉地拍它两下，望着它对自己低语，却从不试着叫它奏起来。那是留给他的唯一一件纪念，叫他想起前半生一切的欢乐哀愁、一切家常的关切和恩爱。罗丝蒙德头一回坐到他身边、握起他的手安慰他，他一双凄惶的眼睛前前后后望，从她怜惜的脸望到音乐盒，嘴里空空洞洞，同样几句话一遍一遍地念：「他们都去了——我的兄弟马克斯，我的妻子，我的小约瑟夫，我姐姐阿加莎，还有萨拉，我的侄女！世上剩下我和我这一小片盒子，孤零零两个。莫扎特再唱不成了。他如今把末一个人也唱过了！」
第二天，他照旧。第三天，罗丝蒙德恭恭敬敬把那本《赞美诗》安放在母亲胸口，绕上一缕自己的头发，末一次吻了那张悲哀安恬的脸。
那场无声的诀别，老人在她身边；完了，又跟着她走。停在棺木旁边，以及随后她带他回丈夫那里，他一直沉在起头罩住他的那份麻木的哀伤里。可是第二天大家谈起要把遗骸移送波思根纳教堂墓地，他们觉出他那双黯淡的眼睛陡然亮了，飘忽的注意力跟着他们的每一句话走。过了一阵，他从椅子上立起，走近罗丝蒙德，急切地望她的脸。「我想，你若容我陪她走，我心里倒好受些，」他说。「她若活着，我们两个原是要一同回康沃尔的。她既去了，你肯不肯容我们如今仍旧一同回去？」
罗丝蒙德婉言相劝，要他明白：遗骸交给丈夫的仆人押送最好，那人的忠实靠得住，他的身份也最合式，担得起那些劳神担干系的差——至亲反倒心绪翻腾，担不起那样从容。

===Original===

She told him that her husband intended to stop in London, to give her one day of rest and quiet, which she absolutely needed, and that they then proposed to return to Cornwall in time to be at Porthgenna before the funeral took place; and she begged earnestly that he would not think of separating his lot from theirs at a time of trouble and trial, when they ought to be all three most closely united by the ties of mutual sympathy and mutual sorrow.
He listened silently and submissively while Rosamond was speaking, but he only repeated his simple petition when she had done. The one idea in his mind now was the idea of going back to Cornwall with all that was left on earth of his sister’s child. Leonard and Rosamond both saw that it would be useless to oppose it, both felt that it would be cruelty to keep him with them, and kindness to let him go away. After privately charging the servant to spare him all trouble and difficulty, to humor him by acceding to any wishes that he might express, and to give him all possible protection and help without obtruding either officiously on his attention, they left him free to follow the one purpose of his heart which still connected him with the interests and events of the passing day. “I shall thank you better soon,” he said at leave-taking, “for letting me go away out of this din of London with all that is left to me of Sarah, my niece. I will dry up my tears as well as I can, and try to have more courage when we meet again.”
On the next day, when they were alone, Rosamond and her husband sought refuge from the oppression of the present in speaking together of the future, and of the influence which the change in their fortunes ought to be allowed to exercise on their plans and projects for the time to come. After exhausting this topic, the conversation turned next on the subject of their friends, and on the necessity of communicating to some of the oldest of their associates the events which had followed the discovery in the Myrtle Room.

===Chinese===

她又告诉他：丈夫打算在伦敦多留几天，好让她歇一天——她实在需要歇一天——随后他们便动身回康沃尔，赶在安葬之前到波思根纳；她恳切求他：遭难受试的时辰，他们三个本该靠同情的纽带、共同的哀伤拴得紧紧的，千万不要在这时候把他的命同他们的命分开。
罗丝蒙德说话的工夫，他默默听着，恭恭敬敬；可她说完，他还是把他那句简单的恳求重说一遍。他心里如今只有一个念头：带着他姐姐的孩子在世上剩下的那一点，回康沃尔去。伦纳德和罗丝蒙德都看出拦他无用，都觉得留他在身边是残忍，放他去是慈悲。他们私下嘱咐仆人：一路的麻烦困难替他一肩挑尽，他有什么愿望，一概依着；尽量护他帮他，又不可过殷勤，搅了他的清静；随后便放他自由，去追随他心头仅存的那一个目的——那一个还把他同眼前的世事牵连在一起的目的。「随后我还要好好谢你们，」分手时他说，「谢你们容我带着萨拉——我侄女——留给我的这一点，离开伦敦这片喧嚣。我尽量拭干眼泪，往后相见，再鼓起些胆气。」
第二天，屋里只剩他们两个，罗丝蒙德同丈夫把话头转向将来，转向命运的这个变故该怎样影响他们往后的打算和筹划，拿它躲避眼前的重压。这个题目说完，话锋又转到朋友们身上，转到一件必要的事上：桃金娘房间的发现跟着引出的种种事故，该向几位最老的朋友报个信。

===Original===

The first name on their lips while they were considering this question was the name of Doctor Chennery; and Rosamond, dreading the effect on her spirits of allowing her mind to remain unoccupied, volunteered to write to the vicar at once, referring briefly to what had happened since they had last communicated with him, and asking him to fulfill that year an engagement of long standing, which he had made with her husband and herself, to spend his autumn holiday with them at Porthgenna Tower. Rosamond’s heart yearned for a sight of her old friend; and she knew him well enough to be assured that a hint at the affliction which had befallen her, and at the hard trial which she had undergone, would be more than enough to bring them together the moment Doctor Chennery could make his arrangements for leaving home.
The writing of this letter suggested recollections which called to mind another friend, whose intimacy with Leonard and Rosamond was of recent date, but whose connection with the earlier among the train of circumstances which had led to the discovery of the Secret entitled him to a certain share in their confidence. This friend was Mr. Orridge, the doctor at West Winston, who had accidentally been the means of bringing Rosamond’s mother to her bedside. To him she now wrote, acknowledging the promise which she had made on leaving West Winston to communicate the result of their search for the Myrtle Room; and informing him that it had terminated in the discovery of some very sad events, of a family nature, which were now numbered with the events of the past. More than this it was not necessary to say to a friend who occupied such a position toward them as that held by Mr. Orridge.
Rosamond had written the address of this second letter, and was absently drawing lines on the blotting-paper with her pen, when she was startled by hearing a contention of angry voices in the passage outside. Almost before she had time to wonder what the noise meant, the door was violently pushed open, and a tall, shabbily dressed, elderly man, with a peevish, haggard face, and a ragged gray beard, stalked in, followed indignantly by the head waiter of the hotel.
“I have three times told this person,” began the waiter, with a strong emphasis on the word “person,” “that Mr. and Mrs.

===Chinese===

商量这个问题时，他们嘴上挂的头一个名字，是切纳里牧师的名字；罗丝蒙德怕自己空着心神，精神要垮，自告奋勇立刻写信给牧师：把他们上回通音信以后出的种种事情略略一提，又求他把同她丈夫和她自己立下的多年旧约——秋天到波思根纳塔楼同他们一起过假期——今年兑现。罗丝蒙德的心渴望见一见老朋友；她深知他的为人，拿得稳：只消暗示一句她遭了难、受了苦，够切纳里牧师一安排好离家的行止，立刻动身同他们聚首。
写这封信，勾起了另一段回忆，想起另一个朋友：同伦纳德、罗丝蒙德交情还浅，可他在引出秘密发现的那一串事由的前段里牵着一分干系，合该分他们一分信托。这朋友便是西温斯顿的医生奥里奇先生——无意之中把罗丝蒙德的母亲引到她病床边的便是他。她如今写信给他，认下离开西温斯顿时许的那句愿：寻访桃金娘房间的结果，要报给他知道；又告诉他：那场寻访收在一个发现里——几桩极可哀的家常事故，如今已归入既往的旧事。对奥里奇先生这样身份的朋友，话说到这里尽够了。
第二封信的地址写好了，罗丝蒙德正心不在焉地拿笔在吸墨纸上画道道，忽然听见外头走廊上一阵怒声相争，吓了一跳。几乎还没来得及想这吵嚷是什么意思，门猛地推开，一个高个子、衣衫寒酸的上了年纪的男人走了进来——一脸焦躁憔悴，灰胡子乱蓬蓬——昂首阔步，后头怒气冲冲跟着旅馆的领班侍者。
「我把这个『人』回过三遍了，」侍者起头，把『人』字咬得重重的，「说弗兰克兰先生和太太——

===Original===

Frankland—”
“Were not at home,” broke in the shabbily dressed man, finishing the sentence for the waiter. “Yes, you told me that; and I told you that the gift of speech was only used by mankind for the purpose of telling lies, and that consequently I didn’t believe you. You *have* told a lie. Here are Mr. and Mrs. Frankland both at home. I come on business, and I mean to have five minutes’ talk with them. I sit down unasked, and I announce my own name—Andrew Treverton.”
With those words, he took his seat coolly on the nearest chair. Leonard’s cheeks reddened with anger while he was speaking, but Rosamond interposed before her husband could say a word.
“It is useless, love, to be angry with him,” she whispered. “The quiet way is the best way with a man like that.” She made a sign to the waiter, which gave him permission to leave the room—then turned to Mr. Treverton. “You have forced your presence on us, Sir,” she said quietly, “at a time when a very sad affliction makes us quite unfit for contentions of any kind. We are willing to show more consideration for your age than you have shown for our grief. If you have anything to say to my husband, he is ready to control himself and to hear you quietly, for my sake.”
“And I shall be short with him and with you, for my own sake,” rejoined Mr. Treverton. “No woman has ever yet had the chance of sharpening her tongue long on me, or ever shall. I have come here to say three things. First, your lawyer has told me all about the discovery in the Myrtle Room, and how you made it. Secondly, I have got your money. Thirdly, I mean to keep it. What do you think of that?”
“I think you need not give yourself the trouble of remaining in the room any longer, if your only object in coming here is to tell us what we know already,” replied Leonard. “We know you have got the money; and we never doubted that you meant to keep it.”
“You are quite sure of that, I suppose?” said Mr. Treverton. “Quite sure you have no lingering hope that any future twists and turns of the law will take the money out of my pocket again and put it back into yours? It is only fair to tell you that there is not the shadow of a chance of any such thing ever happening, or of my ever turning generous and rewarding you of my own accord for the sacrifice you have made.

===Chinese===

「不在屋里，」那衣衫寒酸的男人插进来，替侍者把句子接完。「不错，你这样对我说过；我也对你说过：人类长一张嘴，专用来撒谎，所以你的话我不信。你*果然*撒了谎。弗兰克兰先生和太太分明都在屋里。我有公事，我要同他们说五分钟的话。不等请，我坐下了；我自己通名报姓——安德鲁·特雷弗顿。」
说罢，他随随便便在最近的一把椅子上坐下。他说这些话的工夫，伦纳德的两腮气得涨红；可他丈夫还没来得及开口，罗丝蒙德先插进来。
「犯不着同他生气，亲爱的，」她低声说。「同那样的人，安静的道儿是顶好的道儿。」她朝侍者打了个手势，许他退出去——随后转向特雷弗顿先生。「你把你的驾硬加在我们头上，先生，」她安安静静地说，「正当一场极可哀的苦难叫我们全不配同任何人争执的时候。你顾惜不到我们的哀伤，我们顾惜你的年纪。你若有话同我丈夫说，他愿意自家忍着，安安静静听你说——为了我。」
「为了我自己，我同他、同你，都要说得短短的，」特雷弗顿先生答。「一个女人在我身上磨嘴皮子磨长的，从来没有过；往后也决不会有。我到这里来说三件事。第一件，你们在桃金娘房间怎样发现、发现了什么，你们的律师全告诉我了。第二件，你们的钱在我手里。第三件，我打算留着它。你道这个怎么样？」
「我道你到这屋里来，若只为说我们已经知道的事，便不必再劳你坐下去，」伦纳德答。「钱在你手里，我们知道；你打算留着它，我们从来没疑过。」
「这话你拿得十分稳，我想？」特雷弗顿先生说。「十分稳——你心里没有一星残存的指望，盼着往后法律七弯八绕，把那笔钱重新从我口袋里掏出来，放回你口袋里去？公道起见，我告诉你：那样的事，影子也没有一丝；我行善发心、自愿酬报你们这一场牺牲，影子也没有一丝。

===Original===

I have been to Doctors’ Commons, I have taken out a grant of administration, I have got the money legally, I have lodged it safe at my banker’s, and I have never had one kind feeling in my heart since I was born. That was my brother’s character of me, and he knew more of my disposition, of course, than anyone else. Once again, I tell you both, not a farthing of all that large fortune will ever return to either of you.”
“And once again I tell *you*,” said Leonard, “that we have no desire to hear what we know already. It is a relief to my conscience and to my wife’s to have resigned a fortune which we had no right to possess; and I speak for her as well as for myself when I tell you that your attempt to attach an interested motive to our renunciation of that money is an insult to us both which you ought to have been ashamed to offer.”
“That is your opinion, is it?” said Mr. Treverton. “You, who have lost the money, speak to me, who have got it, in that manner, do you?—Pray, do you approve of your husband’s treating a rich man who might make both your fortunes in that way?” he inquired, addressing himself sharply to Rosamond.
“Most assuredly I approve of it,” she answered. “I never agreed with him more heartily in my life than I agree with him now.”
“Oh!” said Mr. Treverton. “Then it seems you care no more for the loss of the money than he does?”
“He has told you already,” said Rosamond, “that it is as great a relief to my conscience as to his, to have given it up.”
Mr. Treverton carefully placed a thick stick which he carried with him upright between his knees, crossed his hands on the top of it, rested his chin on them, and, in that investigating position, stared steadily in Rosamond’s face.
“I rather wish I had brought Shrowl here with me,” he said to himself. “I should like him to have seen this. It staggers *me*, and I rather think it would have staggered *him*. Both these people,” continued Mr. Treverton, looking perplexedly from Rosamond to Leonard, and from Leonard back again to Rosamond, “are, to all outward appearance, human beings. They walk on their hind legs, they express ideas readily by uttering articulate sounds, they have the usual allowance of features, and in respect of weight, height, and size, they appear to me to be mere average human creatures of the regular civilized sort.

===Chinese===

宗座法院我去过了，遗产管理证书我领下来了，钱我合法地拿到手了，稳稳当当存进我的银行了，我打娘胎里出来，心里没长过一根善芽。那是我兄弟给我的考语，论我的性子，他自然比谁都知底。再对你们两位说一遍：那一大份家私，一个法新也休想回到你们随便哪一个人手里。」
「那我也再对*你*说一遍，」伦纳德说，「我们已经知道的事，不想再听。退出我们无权占有的那份家私，我的良心、我妻子的良心都松了一口气。我告诉你：你想把逐利的心安在我们的弃财上——这侮辱加在我们两个人身上，你原该羞于出口。」
「这是你的意见，是不是？」特雷弗顿先生说。「丢了钱的人，对着得了钱的人，拿这样的腔调讲话，是不是？——请你评一评，」他猛地转向罗丝蒙德，「你丈夫这样对待一个财主——一个能叫你们两个都发财的财主，你赞成么？」
「最切实地赞成，」她答。「我平生同他一条心，没有比此刻更同的。」
「哦！」特雷弗顿先生说。「这么说，丢了那笔钱，你也不比他更心疼？」
「他已经告诉过你，」罗丝蒙德说，「把它交出去，我良心松快，同他一样。」
特雷弗顿先生小心把他随身带的一根粗手杖竖着立在两膝当中，两手交叉搭在杖头，下巴搁在手背上，摆出这个勘察的架势，直直地盯住罗丝蒙德的脸。
「我倒有些懊悔，没把施劳尔带来，」他自言自语。「我倒愿他看一看这个。这一下把我*钉*住了；我想，这一下也会把他*钉*住。这两个人，」特雷弗顿先生接着说，迷迷惘惘，眼睛从罗丝蒙德望到伦纳德，又从伦纳德望回罗丝蒙德，「外表上，都是人类。他们用后腿走路，他们吐出清楚的音节，流利地表达意思，五官一样不缺，论分量、高矮、大小，我看是道道地地的开化种族里寻常不过的人样。

===Original===

And yet, there they sit, taking the loss of a fortune of forty thousand pounds as easily as Croesus, King of Lydia, might have taken the loss of a halfpenny!”
He rose, put on his hat, tucked the thick stick under his arm, and advanced a few steps toward Rosamond.
“I am going now,” he said. “Would you like to shake hands?”
Rosamond turned her back on him contemptuously.
Mr. Treverton chuckled with an air of supreme satisfaction.
Meanwhile Leonard, who sat near the fireplace, and whose color was rising angrily once more, had been feeling for the bell-rope, and had just succeeded in getting it into his hand as Mr. Treverton approached the door.
“Don’t ring, Lenny,” said Rosamond. “He is going of his own accord.”
Mr. Treverton stepped out into the passage—then glanced back into the room with an expression of puzzled curiosity on his face, as if he was looking into a cage which contained two animals of a species that he had never heard of before. “I have seen some strange sights in my time,” he said to himself. “I have had some queer experience of this trumpery little planet, and of the creatures who inhabit it—but I never was staggered yet by any human phenomenon as I am staggered now by those two.” He shut the door without saying another word, and Rosamond heard him chuckle to himself again as he walked away along the passage.
Ten minutes afterward the waiter brought up a sealed letter addressed to Mrs. Frankland. It had been written, he said, in the coffee-room of the hotel by the “person” who had intruded himself into Mr. and Mrs. Frankland’s presence. After giving it to the waiter to deliver, he had gone away in a hurry, swinging his thick stick complacently, and laughing to himself.
Rosamond opened the letter.
On one side of it was a crossed check, drawn in her name, for Forty Thousand Pounds.
On the other side were these lines of explanation:

===Chinese===

可他们偏偏坐在那里，丢一份四万英镑的家私，丢得同吕底亚王克雷索斯丢一个铜板一样轻巧！」
他立起，戴上帽子，把粗手杖掖在腋下，朝罗丝蒙德前进几步。
「我走了，」他说。「肯不肯同我拉拉手？」
罗丝蒙德轻蔑地掉转身，把背对着他。
特雷弗顿先生低低地嗤嗤一笑，一副得意到十二分的神气。
这时候，坐在壁炉近旁的伦纳德脸上又怒气上涌，早摸着了铃绳，恰在特雷弗顿先生走向门口的当口，把它抓到了手里。
「别摇铃，伦尼，」罗丝蒙德说。「他自己要走的。」
特雷弗顿先生跨出门去——随后回头朝屋里又望了一眼，一脸纳闷好奇的神气，仿佛朝一只笼子里张望，笼里关着两只他从没听说过的种类的牲口。「我这辈子见过不少奇景，」他自言自语。「这颗破破烂烂的小行星，同它养着的这些活物，我的经验古怪也不算少——可是人类的现象叫我这样站不住脚的，眼前这两个还是头一遭。」他一句话不再说，把门关上；罗丝蒙德听见他沿着走廊走远，嘴里又嗤嗤地自笑。
十分钟后，侍者送上来一封封了口的信，信封上写着交弗兰克兰太太。他说，信是在旅馆的咖啡间里写的，写信的正是那个硬闯到弗兰克兰夫妇跟前的「人」。他把信交给侍者传送，随即匆匆去了，得意地抡着那根粗手杖，自顾自地笑。
罗丝蒙德拆开信。
信的一边是一张划线支票，抬头写着她的名字，票面是四万英镑。
另一边是几行解释：

===Original===

> “Take your money back again. First, because you and your husband are the only two people I have ever met with who are not likely to be made rascals by being made rich. Secondly, because you have told the truth, when letting it out meant losing money, and keeping it in, saving a fortune. Thirdly, because you are *not* the child of the player-woman. Fourthly, because you can’t help yourself—for I shall leave it to you at my death, if you won’t have it now. Goodbye. Don’t come and see me, don’t write grateful letters to me, don’t invite me into the country, don’t praise my generosity, and, above all things, don’t have anything more to do with Shrowl.
> Andrew Treverton.”

===Chinese===

> 「把你的钱拿回去。第一，我见过的人千千万，你们夫妻两个是独一份：发了财也不会变恶棍。第二，你们说了真话——说出真话，丢的是钱；瞒住真话，保的是一份家私。第三，你*不是*那个戏子婆生的。第四，你由不得自己——你如今不要，我死后也留给你。再会。不要来看我，不要写感激的信给我，不要请我下乡，不要夸我慷慨，顶要紧的，再不要同施劳尔有一丝一毫的来往。
> 安德鲁·特雷弗顿。」

===Original===

The first thing Rosamond did, when she and her husband had a little recovered from their astonishment, was to disobey the injunction which forbade her to address any grateful letters to Mr. Treverton. The messenger, who was sent with her note to Bayswater, returned without an answer, and reported that he had received directions from an invisible man, with a gruff voice, to throw it over the garden wall, and to go away immediately after, unless he wanted to have his head broken.
Mr. Nixon, to whom Leonard immediately sent word of what had happened, volunteered to go to Bayswater the same evening, and make an attempt to see Mr. Treverton on Mr. and Mrs. Frankland’s behalf. He found Timon of London more approachable than he had anticipated. The misanthrope was, for once in his life, in a good humor. This extraordinary change in him had been produced by the sense of satisfaction which he experienced in having just turned Shrowl out of his situation, on the ground that his master was not fit company for him after having committed such an act of folly as giving Mrs. Frankland back her forty thousand pounds.
“I told him,” said Mr. Treverton, chuckling over his recollection of the parting scene between his servant and himself—“I told him that I could not possibly expect to merit his continued approval after what I had done, and that I could not think of detaining him in his place under the circumstances. I begged him to view my conduct as leniently as he could, because the first cause that led to it was, after all, his copying the plan of Porthgenna, which guided Mrs. Frankland to the discovery in the Myrtle Room. I congratulated him on having got a reward of five pounds for being the means of restoring a fortune of forty thousand; and I bowed him out with a polite humility that half drove him mad. Shrowl and I have had a good many tussles in our time; he was always even with me till today, and now I’ve thrown him on his back at last!”
Although Mr. Treverton was willing to talk of the defeat and dismissal of Shrowl as long as the lawyer would listen to him, he was perfectly unmanageable on the subject of Mrs. Frankland, when Mr. Nixon tried to turn the conversation to that topic. He would hear no messages—he would give no promise of any sort for the future.

===Chinese===

夫妻两个从惊骇里缓过来一点，罗丝蒙德办的头一件事，便是违抗那道禁令——不许她写感激的信给特雷弗顿先生的那道禁令。差人带着她的字条往贝斯沃特去，回来说：没有回信；一个看不见的人、一条粗嗓门，吩咐他把信从园墙上头扔进去，扔完即刻滚蛋，不然要敲碎他的脑袋。
尼克松先生——伦纳德立刻把出的事报给他——自告奋勇当天傍晚就去贝斯沃特，替弗兰克兰夫妇试着见一见特雷弗顿先生。伦敦的泰门比他料想的肯见客。那厌世老头子有生以来头一遭兴致好。这一场异乎寻常的转变，来在他心里那股子得意上：他刚把施劳尔撵出大门，理由是：干出把四万英镑还给弗兰克兰太太这样的蠢事的主人，不配再同他作伴。
「我对他说，」特雷弗顿先生回味着主仆分手那一幕，呵呵地笑——「我对他说：干出这样的事，往后我再想博你的嘉许，是没有指望的了；这样的情形之下，留你在差事上，我是不忍心的。我还求他尽量把我这桩行事从宽评断，因为究其起头，还得算他那回描了波思根纳的图样，那张图把弗兰克兰太太引到了桃金娘房间的发现上。我又道贺：做了恢复四万家私的引线，挣得五镑的赏钱；末了恭恭敬敬、客客气气把他送出门去，那份谦卑，把他半个魂都气疯了。施劳尔同我，这些年较量过多少回合；他回回同我扯平，到今天，我到底把他仰面朝天撂倒了！」
特雷弗顿先生谈施劳尔的败绩和解职，律师听多久他谈多久；可尼克松先生一试把话头转到弗兰克兰太太身上，那一个题目他全然管束不住。传的话他一句不听——往后的应承，一概不给。

===Original===

All that he could be prevailed on to say about himself and his own projects was that he intended to give up the house at Bayswater, and to travel again for the purpose of studying human nature, in different countries, on a plan that he had not tried yet—the plan of endeavoring to find out the good that there might be in people as well as the bad. He said the idea had been suggested to his mind by his anxiety to ascertain whether Mr. and Mrs. Frankland were perfectly exceptional human beings or not. At present, he was disposed to think that they were, and that his travels were not likely to lead to anything at all remarkable in the shape of a satisfactory result. Mr. Nixon pleaded hard for something in the shape of a friendly message to take back, along with the news of his intended departure. The request produced nothing but a sardonic chuckle, followed by this parting speech, delivered to the lawyer at the garden gate.
“Tell those two superhuman people,” said Timon of London, “that I may give up my travels in disgust when they least expect it; and that I may possibly come back to look at them again—I don’t personally care about either of them—but I should like to get one satisfactory sensation more out of the lamentable spectacle of humanity before I die.”

===Chinese===

他自家的事、自家的筹划，旁人磨破嘴皮，也只磨出这样一句：他打算退掉贝斯沃特的宅子，重新出门游历，去研究人性，到各国去，照一个他还没试过的路数——努力寻一寻人身上的好，同坏一路寻。他说这个念头钻进心里，起因在急要查明一件事：弗兰克兰夫妇两个人，究竟是不是完全超出人类常格的例外。眼下他的意见倾向于是；照此推去，他这趟游历，怕也寻不出什么可观的收成。尼克松先生苦苦恳求：他动身的消息要带回去，总得捎上一句友好的话。恳求只博得一声冷冷的嗤笑，随后是临别的一句话，在园门口送给律师。
「告诉那两位超凡入圣的人物，」伦敦的泰门说，「我兴许在他们最不提防的时候，败兴罢了这趟游历；兴许还要回来看一看他们——他们两个，我谁也不放在心上——可是我死以前，人类这场可哀的把戏里，我还想再榨出一份满意的滋味来。」

## VI The Dawn of a New Life / 六 新生活的曙光
===Original===

Four days afterward, Rosamond and Leonard and Uncle Joseph met together in the cemetery of the church of Porthgenna.
The earth to which we all return had closed over her: the weary pilgrimage of Sarah Leeson had come to its quiet end at last. The miner’s grave from which she had twice plucked in secret her few memorial fragments of grass had given her the home, in death, which, in life, she had never known. The roar of the surf was stilled to a low murmur before it reached the place of her rest; and the wind that swept joyously over the open moor paused a little when it met the old trees that watched over the graves, and wound onward softly through the myrtle hedge which held them all embraced alike in its circle of lustrous green.
Some hours had passed since the last words of the burial service had been read. The fresh turf was heaped already over the mound, and the old headstone with the miner’s epitaph on it had been raised once more in its former place at the head of the grave. Rosamond was reading the inscription softly to her husband. Uncle Joseph had walked a little apart from them while she was thus engaged, and had knelt down by himself at the foot of the mound. He was fondly smoothing and patting the newly laid turf—as he had often smoothed Sarah’s hair in the long-past days of her youth—as he had often patted her hand in the after-time, when her heart was weary and her hair was gray.
“Shall we add any new words to the old, worn letters as they stand now?” said Rosamond, when she had read the inscription to the end. “There is a blank space left on the stone. Shall we fill it, love, with the initials of my mother’s name, and the date of her death? I feel something in my heart which seems to tell me to do that, and to do no more.”
“So let it be, Rosamond,” said her husband. “That short and simple inscription is the fittest and the best.”
She looked away, as he gave that answer, to the foot of the grave, and left him for a moment to approach the old man. “Take my hand, Uncle Joseph,” she said, and touched him gently on the shoulder. “Take my hand, and let us go back together to the house.”
He rose as she spoke, and looked at her doubtfully. The musical box, enclosed in its well-worn leather case, lay on the grave near the place where he had been kneeling.

===Chinese===

四天以后，罗丝蒙德、伦纳德和约瑟夫舅舅，在波思根纳教堂的墓园里聚首。
我们人人要归的泥土，把她盖上了：萨拉·利森困乏的长旅，到底安安静静走到了头。那座矿工的坟——她曾两回悄悄从坟头摘下几茎草留作纪念——在她生前没给过她家，到死倒给了她一个归宿。涛声到她安息之地，已经压成低低的絮语；在旷野上欢欢喜喜扫过的风，一碰上守着坟茔的老树便放缓了，又轻轻弯过桃金娘的树篱——那树篱把这一切都揽在它油亮的青翠的圆环里。
葬礼经文念完末一句，已经过了几个钟头。新草皮已经堆上坟头；那块旧碑——刻着矿工墓志的——重新在坟头原位立起。罗丝蒙德把碑文轻轻念给丈夫听。她这样念着的工夫，约瑟夫舅舅悄悄离开他们一点，独自在坟脚下跪下。他把新铺的草皮爱惜地抚平、轻轻拍着——像久远往日她年轻的时候，他常常抚平萨拉的头发；像后来的岁月，她心倦了、发白了，他常常轻轻拍她的手。
「旧碑上那些磨旧的字，如今站着不动，我们要不要添几个新字上去？」罗丝蒙德把碑文念到末尾，说。「石头上留着一块空白。要不要把它填上，亲爱的，填上我母亲名字的缩写、她去世的日子？我心里有个东西仿佛在教我：就做这一件，也不再多做。」
「就照这样办吧，罗丝蒙德，」丈夫说。「那样短短的一篇、简简单单的一篇，最合式，也最好。」
他这样答的时候，她掉开眼，朝坟脚下望去，暂时离开他，走近老人。「握住我的手，约瑟夫舅舅，」她说，轻轻碰了碰他的肩。「握住我的手，我们一同回宅子里去吧。」
她说话时，他立起来，疑惑地望她。音乐盒——装在磨旧了的皮匣里——躺在坟上，紧挨着他方才跪着的地方。

===Original===

Rosamond took it up from the grass, and slung it in the old place at his side, which it had always occupied when he was away from home. He sighed a little as he thanked her. “Mozart can sing no more,” he said. “He has sung to the last of them now!”
“Don’t say ‘to the last,’ yet,” said Rosamond—“don’t say ‘to the last,’ Uncle Joseph, while I am alive. Surely Mozart will sing to *me*, for my mother’s sake?”
A smile—the first she had seen since the time of their grief—trembled faintly round his lips. “There is comfort in that,” he said; “there is comfort for Uncle Joseph still, in hearing that.”
“Take my hand,” she repeated softly. “Come home with us now.”
He looked down wistfully at the grave. “I will follow you,” he said, “if you will go on before me to the gate.”
Rosamond took her husband’s arm, and guided him to the path that led out of the churchyard. As they passed from sight, Uncle Joseph knelt down once more at the foot of the grave, and pressed his lips on the fresh turf.
“Goodbye, my child,” he whispered, and laid his cheek for a moment against the grass before he rose again.
At the gate, Rosamond was waiting for him. Her right hand was resting on her husband’s arm; her left hand was held out for Uncle Joseph to take.
“How cool the breeze is!” said Leonard. “How pleasantly the sea sounds! Surely this is a fine summer day?”
“The calmest and loveliest of the year,” said Rosamond. “The only clouds on the sky are clouds of shining white; the only shadows over the moor lie light as down on the heather. Oh, Lenny, it is such a different day from that day of dull oppression and misty heat when we found the letter in the Myrtle Room! Even the dark tower of our old house, yonder, looks its brightest and best, as if it waited to welcome us to the beginning of a new life. I will make it a happy life to you, and to Uncle Joseph, if I can—happy as the sunshine we are walking in now. You shall never repent, love, if *I* can help it, that you have married a wife who has no claim of her own to the honors of a family name.”
“I can never repent my marriage, Rosamond, because I can never forget the lesson that my wife has taught me.”
“What lesson, Lenny?”
“An old one, my dear, which some of us can never learn too often. The highest honors, Rosamond, are those which no accident can take away—the honors that are conferred by **Love** and **Truth**.”

===Chinese===

罗丝蒙德把它从草上拾起，挎回他身边的老地方——他出门在外，它素来占着的那个地方。他道谢时微微叹了口气。「莫扎特再唱不成了，」他说。「他如今把末一个人也唱过了！」
「先别说『末一个人』，」罗丝蒙德说——「别说『末一个人』，约瑟夫舅舅，只要我活着。为了我母亲的缘故，莫扎特总还会*为我*一个人唱的吧？」
一丝微笑——打他们遭难以来她见着他的头一丝——在他唇边轻轻颤动。「这里有安慰，」他说。「听见这一句，约瑟夫舅舅还有安慰。」
「握住我的手，」她轻轻又说一遍。「这会儿同我们回家去吧。」
他恋恋地低头望坟。「我跟着你们来，」他说，「你们先朝大门走。」
罗丝蒙德挽起丈夫的胳膊，引他走上出墓园的小径。他们的背影一没，约瑟夫舅舅又在坟脚下跪下，把嘴唇贴上新铺的草皮。
「再会，我的孩子，」他低声说，把腮在草上贴了片刻，才重新立起。
大门前，罗丝蒙德等着他。她的右手搭在丈夫的臂弯上；左手伸出去，让约瑟夫舅舅握。
「风多凉爽！」伦纳德说。「海声多好听！今天准是个晴美的夏日吧？」
「一年里头顶静、顶可爱的一天，」罗丝蒙德说。「天上唯一的云是耀眼的白云；荒原上唯一的影子，轻得同羽绒一样，覆在石楠上。啊，伦尼，同我们在桃金娘房间找到那封信的那一天——那闷沉沉、雾腾腾、热昏昏的一天——多不一样！连那边我们老宅的黑塔楼，此刻也显得顶亮、顶精神，仿佛等着欢迎我们走进新生活的起头。我一定叫它成为你的快活日子，也成为约瑟夫舅舅的，只要我办得到——快活得同我们此刻走着的日光一样。亲爱的，只要*我*出得上力，你永远不会后悔——不会后悔娶了一个妻子：家族姓氏的荣光，她自己没有一分名分。」
「我的婚姻，我永远不会后悔，罗丝蒙德，因为我永远不会忘记我妻子教给我的一课。」
「什么一课，伦尼？」
「旧的一课，亲爱的，我们有些人学多少遍也学不够。顶高的荣光，罗丝蒙德，是任何意外夺不走的荣光——**爱**与**真**颁给人的荣光。」
